He looked at the figure posted on Account Day 310 for Hask, Agathe. Then he looked at the one for 309. Then 308. He took a fresh page on his slate and did the arithmetic by hand, the way he had done it as a boy, with the stylus scratching.

It was wrong. It had been wrong for eleven years. Some time around 299, when her mother's balance came to her, somebody — some counter, at some desk, in some hall — had keyed the surcharge against the whole of Agathe Hask's balance instead of the inherited part. And then, each year after, the surcharge itself had been treated as inherited, because it had been posted under the inheritance heading, and had been surcharged in turn. Three parts in a hundred, compounding, on a figure that had never been owed.

He worked it through to the present hour. It took him most of the morning. When he was done he sat back and looked at it.

Corrected balance: minus nine thousand and sixty-one.

Two thousand one hundred and forty-five credits. A little over two thousand credits that Agathe Hask had never owed, that her daughter had been cutting copper in the Cold to pay down, that would have gone on her son's chit at her death and on his children's after him.

Orrin turned to the list from the Comptroller-General's office and ran his stylus down it until he found the cut: the ten-thousandth account, the shallowest that would be Called. It was minus nine thousand four hundred and twelve.

He sat very still. A counter at the next desk glanced at him, and away.

At minus eleven thousand two hundred, Agathe Hask went into the Cold. At minus nine thousand and sixty-one, she did not.

* * *

A Calling could not be posted on a balance alone. The rules said a counter must also sight the account-holder, in their place of residence, and confirm by their own eyes that the person existed and was where the Tally said. It was an old rule and a kind one, Orrin had always thought: it meant that before the Count did anything to a family, someone from the Count had stood in their room.

He had never been below Gallery Fifteen before. He was ashamed of that, a little, going down. The lift stopped at Eighteen and he walked the rest, along the terrace roads with his grey coat and his slate, and he felt the light change as he went: the clean white of the Upper Wards going yellow, then grey, then a sort of brownish dusk, sideways, as the Wick fell away behind the curve. The terraces were narrower here and the houses smaller and nearer together, and there was washing hung between them, and the smell was of damp and cooking and people. Children stopped playing and watched him go by. An old man spat, not at him, but not far from him either.

Twenty-Two was the last gallery before the Lip. He found the Hask door by its number. He touched his chit to the panel, the counter's chit, which opened any door on Lantern for the purpose of a sighting, and the door sighed and clunked and let him in.

There were three people in the room. A woman of fifty or so, sitting up on the lower bunk with a blanket round her, very thin, with pale, almost colourless eyes. A boy on the top bunk, all knees, who looked at Orrin's coat and then at the door and then at Orrin's slate in a way that made Orrin hold the slate a little closer. And standing at the ring-stove with her back to him, a young woman in a cutter's rig with the upper half peeled down and tied round her waist by its arms, who did not turn round.

"Counter Sallow," Orrin said. "Third hall. I'm here for a sighting. Hask, Agathe?"

"That's me," said the woman on the bunk. She had a cough; he heard it under her voice, waiting.

"I'm sorry to disturb you. It won't take a moment." He held up the slate and read the particulars from it — name, gallery, birth year, number — and she nodded to each of them, and he marked them. His stylus was not quite steady. "Thank you."

"That's it?" said the young woman at the stove. She still had not turned. "That's the sighting? You come down twenty galleries to look at my mother and say her name, and then she's sighted, and then she can be Called?"

"Tam," said the woman on the bunk.

"No, I want to know. I want to know how it works." She turned round then. She had the same pale eyes, set in a hard, dirty, angular face that would have been handsome if it had not been so angry. "Does he get paid by the head? Is there a bonus for the deep ones?"

"I'm salaried," said Orrin, before he could stop himself.

She laughed. It was not a nice sound.

"Counter," said Agathe Hask quietly. "My daughter's had a bad week. So has everybody on this gallery. You'll forgive her. Is there anything else you need?"

He should have said no. He had his sighting. He had no business in this room any longer and every reason to leave it. Instead he heard himself say, "There's an error in your account."

The room went quiet.

"An error," said Tamsin Hask.

"In the inheritance surcharge. From 299. It was keyed to the whole of your mother's balance instead of the inherited part, and then compounded. It's—" he looked at the slate, though he knew the figure— "it's two thousand one hundred and forty-five credits. Over eleven years. I've corrected it on my slate. I'm going to file it today."

Agathe Hask looked at him for a long moment.

"How much does that leave?" she said.

"Nine thousand and sixty-one."

"And where's the line? For the Calling?"

He should not have told her. The cut was not posted. It would not be posted for another twenty-six days. "Nine thousand four hundred and twelve," he said.

Nobody said anything. On the top bunk the boy had sat up.

"So she's under," said Tamsin. "If you file it. She's under the line."

"If it's accepted. Yes."

"If."

"Corrections are— they have to be approved by a senior counter. It's a formality. The arithmetic is plain. Anyone can check it." He held out the slate, as if she might want to, and she looked at it, and at him, and he understood that she could not read the figures on it and that he had just shamed her, and he took it back.

"Why are you telling us?" she said.

"Because it's true."

"Lots of things are true. The Count doesn't come down here and tell us them." She folded her arms. "What do you want?"

"Nothing. I— nothing." He found that he did not know what to do with his hands. "It's my job. The Count is just. An account can't be Called in error. That's the rule."

"The Count is just," said Tamsin, flatly, the way you'd repeat a word in a language you didn't speak.

Agathe Hask coughed, then, for a long time, bent over the blanket; and her daughter went to her and held her shoulders until it passed, and Orrin stood in the middle of the small room with his slate and looked away, at the shelf over the stove, where there was a biscuit tin with a picture of a farmhouse on its lid, very old, the paint nearly gone.

When the coughing stopped, Agathe sat back and wiped her mouth and looked at him again with her pale eyes.

"Counter Sallow," she said. "Can I ask you something?"

"Of course."

"Who is Caul?"

He opened his mouth to say the catechism. _Caul is the dark that kept us when the Company left us in it. Caul is owed for every breath, and the Owing pay._ He had said it every Account Day of his life. He found that he did not want to say it here, in this room, to this woman, and he did not know why.

"Caul is the Account," he said at last.

"That's what I told my girl." Agathe nodded slowly. "She didn't think much of it either."

He left soon after. In the passage outside, as the door clunked shut behind him, he reached into his coat for his stylus to mark the sighting time on his slate, and found the clip empty. He looked back at the door. He thought about knocking. He thought about the boy on the top bunk, all knees and quick eyes.

He marked the time with his fingernail, and went back up the galleries into the light.

* * *

The correction came back in an hour, with a line through it in red and a note in the margin in the cramped hand of the senior counter of the third hall: _Surcharges are not corrected during a Calling. Refile after the lists are posted. — A._

Orrin read the note three times. After the lists were posted the Haskes would be in the Cold. After the lists were posted there would be no account to correct.

He sat at his desk while the hall emptied around him for the dinner bell. He was not hungry. He was angry, and he was not used to being angry, and he found he did not know what to do with it except work. So he did what a counter does when a number will not sit right: he went back to the beginning.

The inheritance surcharge had been in the Tally from the Founding. Every counter knew that. But nobody had ever told him where in the Tally it lived, or who had put it there, or what it had been written to do. He had never needed to know. Now he wanted to. He wanted to see, written down, the rule that said three parts in a hundred, and see whether it said _inherited portion_ or _whole_. He wanted to know whether the error was a counter's, or the Tally's own.

The Count's archive of the Tally's root entries was open to counters of the fourth rank and above. He was fourth rank. He had never opened it. Hardly anyone did; it was a long scroll of old rules, rate changes, adjustments to the dimming line, all of it three hundred years dead. He found the surcharge rule after half an hour of searching. It was where it should be, in the first year after the Closing, posted on the Founders' authority. It said _inherited portion_. It was not the Tally's error. Somebody in a ledger hall had simply keyed it wrong, eleven years ago, and nobody had looked since.

He should have stopped there. He nearly did. But the archive was open on his slate and he had never seen the very first page of it, and so — with the same small guilty pleasure as putting his hand on the Hulk at seven — he scrolled to the top.

The first entry in the root of the Tally of Lantern, as kept by the Count, was dated the forty-ninth day of the Closing, in 2387 of the old reckoning. It was very short. It established the Account.

ACCOUNT ESTABLISHED. CREDITOR: CAUL. ALL BALANCES ON THE TALLY ASSIGNED TO CREDITOR IN FULL. ACCOUNT HOLDER: NONE.

And under it, where every other root entry he had seen carried the same small paired mark — the two authorities of the Founders, the twin keys of the root, side by side — there was something different. Not the paired mark. A single line of the Tally's own plain text, which he had never seen it use about anything:

AUTHORISED BY A KEY NOT KNOWN TO THE TALLY.

He read it several times. He thought about what _account holder: none_ meant, in a ledger. Every account had a holder. Somebody's chit, somebody's number. An account with no holder was money owed to nobody: a debt that could be paid into for ever and drawn from by no one.

He was still looking at it when the hall door opened and a bailiff in grey said, "Counter Sallow? The Comptroller-General asks for you. Now, please."

* * *

He had never been to the top of the Counting House. The Comptroller-General's gallery was a single long room with a single long window, curving, that looked straight down the length of Lantern along the Wick, and when Orrin came through the door the light hit him so hard he had to stop and put his hand up. Beyond the glass the whole world hung, lit and upside down: the Upper Wards bright, the Galleries dimming, the Wick blazing down the middle of the sky to where, two-thirds of the way along, it simply stopped, and beyond it the dark of the Cold lay like a bruise.

"Few people are prepared for it," said Imre Vosk. "Sit, Counter. Please."

He was at a desk at the far end of the room, with the window behind him so that it was hard to see his face. Orrin sat. There was a cup of tea in front of him, already poured. He did not touch it.

"You've been in the root," said Vosk.

"Yes, Comptroller-General. I'm fourth rank. It's open to—"

"It is open to you. Yes. I'm not reproaching you." Vosk's voice was as soft as it had been at the Hook; it was a voice you leaned toward without meaning to. "I'm interested in what took you there. Counters of your age don't usually go reading three hundred years of rate tables for pleasure."

"There's an error in an account on the Calling list. A surcharge keyed to the whole balance. I wanted to see whether the rule was at fault, or the keying. It was the keying. The rule's sound."

"Of course it is. The Founders wrote it." Vosk nodded. "And the account?"

"Hask. Agathe. Twenty-Two. Corrected, she's above the cut. I filed it. Senior Counter Abney refused it until after the lists."

"Abney was correct. We can't have corrections in flight during a Calling, Counter. You understand that. If every counter went into every account looking for reasons to lift it over the line, the line would mean nothing, and the Calling would fail, and then all of us would go into the dark together." He spread his hands. "It's a hard rule. It's the rule."

"She doesn't owe it," said Orrin. "Two thousand credits of it. It's not hers."

"Then it will be corrected afterwards, as Abney said."

"Afterwards she'll be in the Cold."

"Yes," said Vosk gently. "That's a pity." He let a moment pass. "You saw the first entry. In the root."

It was not a question. Orrin said, "Yes."

"What did you make of it?"

"I didn't— I don't know. _Account holder, none._ And the key. I've never seen the Tally say that."

"The Tally says what it was told to say, at the Founding, by people who had a great deal more to think about than how it would read to a counter three hundred years later." Vosk leaned forward a little, and the light from the window lay along his shoulder and the side of his face. "Let me tell you what the doctrine says, Counter Sallow, since you know it already. The Account is held by Caul. Caul holds itself. The Founders gave the Account to the dark that kept us, so that no living man could ever own it, and no Company could ever come back and claim it. That is what _holder, none_ means. That is why the key is not known. Because it was not a man's key. Do you understand?"

"Yes, Comptroller-General."

"The Count administers the Account. It does not own it. I hold the Count Key, which lets me keep the books, and I hold it as my predecessors did, in trust, for Caul. That's all. There's nothing hidden in the root, Counter. There's only the Founding, which is a holy thing, and which is not improved by being puzzled over by clever young men at dinner time." He sat back. "You've done good work on the Calling lists. Abney tells me you're the most careful counter in the third hall. I'd like you to go on being careful. Finish your four hundred. Sign your sightings. And then, when the Calling is done, refile your correction, and I will approve it myself."

Orrin looked at the tea he had not drunk. He thought of the room on Twenty-Two, and the tin with the farmhouse on the lid.

"Thank you, Comptroller-General," he said.

"Owing and owed, Counter."

"Owing and owed."

* * *

The copper went for ninety at the Salt Docks, which was thirty more than the Count's foundry would have given and thirty less than it was worth, and Tamsin took it because she had to.

The fence was a man called Brannock who worked out of a dead hauler's cabin at the end of Spur Four, aft along the ring where the Count's bailiffs did not come without a reason. He weighed the copper on a beam scale older than the Closing and counted out the ninety in Salt tokens, which were brass washers stamped with a crude S and worth exactly what the Salt said they were worth and nothing anywhere else on the rock. Tamsin put them in the inside pocket of her rig. She would change them at a chit-shop on Nineteen that took Salt washers at a quarter off, and the quarter off would go to the Salt too, because everything on the Salt Docks ended up going to the Salt in the end.

"Somebody wants a word," said Brannock, not looking up from his scale.

"Who?"

He jerked his head toward the back of the cabin, where a curtain made of an old cargo-net hung over a doorway. Tamsin looked at it for a long moment. Then she went through.

The room behind was bigger than the cabin, cut back into the dock wall, and warm, warmer than any room Tamsin had been in all year. There was a stove with real coal-cake in it. There was a rug. There was a table with a lamp on it and a pot of something that smelled of meat. And at the table, eating out of the pot with a spoon, was a woman of sixty with one eye and a patch over the other, and grey hair cropped to a burr, and arms like a hauler's.

Everybody on Lantern knew who Wenna Blackwood was. Nobody below Fifteen had seen her and lived to boast of it. That was the story, anyway. Tamsin had always assumed it was the Salt's own story, put about by the Salt.

"Sit," said Wenna Blackwood. Her voice was a gravel chute. "Eat, if you want. It's goat. Real goat. Off the farms."

Tamsin sat. She didn't eat.

"You're Agathe Hask's girl," said Wenna. "Cutter. Good cutter, Brannock says. Clean metal, no lies about the weight." She chewed, swallowed. "And you've found a door."

Tamsin went cold.

"The Cold talks," said Wenna, not unkindly. "Not much. But I've got ears in it. Three cutters work the Fourteen run besides you, and one of them saw your light on the service way under the old pump bank, and saw where it stopped, and came and sold me that for a bottle." She put the spoon down. "Company door. Welded. Letters on it. That right?"

There was no point lying. "Aft Store 3."

Wenna nodded slowly, as if Tamsin had confirmed something she already knew. "Company stores," she said. "They had a dozen, aft, in the old days. Fuel and parts and medicine. They emptied them when they ran. Everybody knows that. Every one of them's been cut open and gutted three times over in three hundred years." She leaned forward. "Except that one. Never found it. Never even knew it was there. Know why?"

"No."

"Because somebody welded it shut and then iced over the way in, that's why. Somebody wanted it forgotten." Her one eye was very bright in the lamplight. "What's behind a door like that, cutter? Eh? What would the Company not take when it ran — and not want anybody else to have?"

"I don't know."

"No. Nor do I. But I'd pay to find out." Wenna Blackwood picked up her spoon again. "Half of whatever's in it. You cut the door, my people carry. I'll stand your tools and your air and your mother's tin besides. Think about it."

Tamsin stood up. Her legs were not quite steady; it was the warmth, she told herself, she wasn't used to it.

"My mother's going to be Called," she said. She didn't know why she said it. "Unless a counter in the Count does what he said he would."

Wenna looked up at her with no expression at all.

"Then you'd better think quick," she said.

# Chapter 6: The Throat

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

There were eleven ships in the queue at the mouth of the Throat, and the _Good Title_ was seventh.

The order had been settled at Port Ambry by the harbourmaster's lottery, which every captain had sworn to honour and which every captain, Ode said, would honour exactly as long as it suited them. The queue lay in a loose line along the approach, thirty thousand kilometres out from the mouth, each ship holding station on its neighbour with little flickers of manoeuvring thrust. From the _Good Title_'s bridge they looked like beads on a string. Beyond them was the Throat.

Close to, it was not a patch of wrong sky any longer. It was a wound. Corvin could find no other word for it. A great ragged lens of distortion, thousands of kilometres across, through which the stars on the far side swam and doubled and smeared, and at its heart a pulsing bruise of light, pink going to violet going to a deep hot red, like something seen through closed eyelids. The edges of the lens were never still. They rippled. Ode called them the lips, and the word had made him faintly sick the first time she said it and now seemed simply accurate. They opened and closed. Not much — a few hundred kilometres, the throat-men said, on a period of a few hours — but enough. A ship in the lips when they closed would not be a ship afterwards.

"First goes at the top of the next open," Ode said. She was in the master's chair with her boots on the console, which Corvin had learned was how she sat when she was worried. "That's the _Pellucid_. Then the _Harrow_, then the _Lady Jane_, then the _Saint Ishmael_. We go on the third open after. Nine hours, give or take."

"And until then?"

"Until then we sit here and watch better men than us go first." She did not take her eyes off the main screen. "Tol."

"Captain."

"How's she feel?"

Tol Brask's voice came from somewhere below, flat and absorbed. "Engine's fine. Engine's always fine. Hull's grumbling on the old Pellan scar. I'll watch it."

"Watch it."

Haste had come up from his cabin and was standing at the back of the bridge in a dark coat, quite still, watching the screen with the polite interest of a man at a concert. Corvin found that he disliked that more than anything else about him: that he could look at the Throat as if it were a performance being put on for him, and might be asked for an encore.

The _Pellucid_ went at the top of the open. Corvin watched her go: a long grey freighter, Concord-built, much newer than the _Good Title_, sliding forward on a steady burn into the swimming dark at the centre of the lens. For a while she was a clear shape. Then she was a smear. Then she was a red point that wavered and stretched and was gone.

Nobody on the bridge said anything for a minute.

"She's through," said Ode at last. "Or she's dead. Either way she's out of the queue." She took her boots off the console. "Next."

* * *

It was during the second open, with the _Harrow_ and the _Lady Jane_ both gone into the red, that the _Saint Ishmael_ broke the queue.

She was the ship with the priests: a converted pilgrim-hauler, broad and slow, with a cargo of the faithful in orange who had sold everything they owned, the noodle-woman had told Corvin, for a berth on the first ship through to the Return. She was fourth in the lottery. She should have waited for the third open, as the _Good Title_ was waiting. Instead, as the lips began to close at the end of the second, Corvin saw her drive light flare, very bright, and saw her begin to move.

"What's she doing?" he said.

"Cutting," said Ode. She sat up very straight. "Stupid. Stupid bloody—" She hit the comm. "_Saint Ishmael_, _Saint Ishmael_, this is _Good Title_, you are out of turn, the lips are closing, abort, abort your run—"

The comm hissed. Then a calm, pleasant voice — the priest's voice, Corvin thought, the one from the gallery rail — said: "_Good Title_, thank you for your concern. We have been shown the way. We go in faith. Peace be with you all."

"Faith's got nothing to do with the bloody lips, you lunatic—"

But the _Saint Ishmael_ was already going, faster than she had any business going, her drive a long white stroke against the swimming stars, straight at the heart of the lens. For a moment Corvin thought she would make it. She was so nearly through. The red of the throat's centre had begun to swallow her bow; he could see the light of it on her hull. Then the lips closed.

He had expected it to be sudden, and it was not. It was slow, and it was quite silent, and that was worse. The edge of the lens slid across the screen like a curtain drawn across a window, and where it crossed the _Saint Ishmael_ the ship simply came apart: not in an explosion, but as if she had been made of paper and somebody had folded her. Her stern went one way and her bow another and her middle went into a long bright streak of debris that was pulled out and out along the edge of the lips like taffy, glittering, and then the curtain was closed and there was only a thin bright line drawn along the rim of the Throat, slowly spreading, slowly dimming, where four hundred people had been.

The bridge was very quiet.

"Throats don't forgive," said Mabry Ode at last. She said it softly, without anger, the way you would say the name of someone who had died. "Every throat-runner learns that the first year, or doesn't get a second." She turned to the comm officer, a young man with his hands over his mouth. "Log it. _Saint Ishmael_, out of turn, lost in the lips, all hands. Time and position. Send it back to Ambry on the next relay."

"All hands," the young man said, through his fingers.

"All hands." Ode swung her chair round and looked at Haste, who had not moved. "That's the Rush, Mr. Haste. That's what your claim's worth, to some people. That's what it's worth to the rest of us too, if we don't keep our heads."

"I'm sure we'll keep them, Captain," said Haste.

* * *

They went in on the third open, and it took eleven hours.

Corvin had read about throat transit. Everybody had. It was in children's books: the long fall, the strange lights, the time that went wrong. He had read, as a boy, that people in transit saw their own lives, or the faces of the dead, or nothing at all; that some came out singing and some came out mad. What none of the books had said was how boring most of it was. The _Good Title_ went into the red and the red closed round her and for hours afterwards there was nothing on the screens but red, a deep dull red that moved slowly, like blood in water, and every so often a shudder through the hull as she rode some invisible swell, and Tol's voice from below saying _fine, fine, she's fine_, as if to himself.

After the third hour Ode sent everybody who was not on watch to their bunks. Corvin did not go to his bunk. He went looking for somewhere with a window, and found the observation blister on the ship's upper spine — a dome of old scratched glass with two worn couches under it, built in some optimistic decade when throat-runners had expected to carry tourists — and found that it was already occupied by Fen Adaire and her bell.

Tol had stowed the crate there, it turned out, because it was the only space aboard that was not cargo, quarters, or Brightwater's. Fen had opened it. She had the bell out of its foam and sitting upright on the deck, wedged with cargo straps to the couch legs, and she was lying on her back on the deck beside it with a hand-lamp and her loupe, reading it.

"Sorry," said Corvin. "I'll find somewhere else."

"There isn't anywhere else." She didn't look up. "Sit, if you want. It's a big window."

He sat on one of the couches. Above them, through the glass, the red moved. Every so often something moved in it that might have been a shape and might have been his eyes.

"Does it bother you?" he said. "That?"

"The Throat? No. I've been through four." She turned the loupe a fraction. "What bothers me is that I can read six hundred and twelve names on this bell and I don't know who any of them are."

"I thought you said nearly three thousand."

"I said I'd counted nearly three thousand. Counting isn't reading. Most of them are too worn. Or too small. Or cut in a hand I can't make out." She sat up, pushing her hair back with the hand that held the lamp. "There's an order to them, though. That much I've worked out. The oldest are round the lip. They're the crossing — thirty-one names, cut very carefully, all in one hand. Then the colony's dead, year by year, going up the waist. You can see the hand change every twenty or thirty years. A new dockmaster, maybe. Somebody whose job it was. I can date some of them roughly by the style." She shifted round, on her knees now, and put her lamp on the shoulder of the bell. "And up here, at the top, the last hand. Very good. Very steady. Old, I think — there's a little shake on the long strokes. These are the newest. That one—" she touched a bright line with her fingertip— "is the newest of all. Or one of them."

He leaned forward. He knew which name it was before he read it.

"Lemuel Adeyemi," he said.

She looked at him in surprise. "You saw it at Ambry."

"I saw it at Ambry." He did not know how to explain that he had thought about it since. That a name on a bell, cut clean and bright among thousands of worn ones, had followed him onto the ship and into his bunk at night. "Who was he?"

"I've no idea. A person who died on Lantern, in the last years before the Closing. That's all the bell can tell me." She sat back on her heels. "That's the thing about provenance, Mr. Saal. Objects remember things. They just don't remember them in sentences."

"Corvin."

"Corvin." She considered him. "And there's something else. Here." She moved the lamp down, to a band low on the waist, below the old weathered names and above the lip, where he had taken the bronze for blank. In the light it was not blank. It was cut, very small, very close, with hundreds of names: a dense grey band of letters, cramped together, so close that they almost touched. "These aren't in order with the others. They're cut over a blank band that the old dockmasters had left — the bell was cast with a plain belt there, for decoration — and they're in hands I don't see anywhere else on the bell. Three or four of them, I think, taking turns. Fast. Not as careful. Like people in a hurry, or people very tired." She looked up at him. "Hundreds of them. All at once, at the end."

"An epidemic," said Corvin. "A disaster. A fire."

"Maybe." She didn't sound convinced. "If there'd been a disaster, the Company would have filed it. There'd be a record. I've read every page they sent out before the Closing, Corvin. There's no fire. There's no plague. There's nothing."

He said nothing. He was thinking, for no reason he could name, of a line near the end of the Director's statement, about a mine.

"Why do you want to take it home?" he said instead. "Really."

Fen was quiet for a while. Above them the red moved, and the hull gave one of its long low shudders, and Tol's voice came faint through the deck: _fine, she's fine._

"Because they're names," she said at last. "Somebody cut every one of these by hand, for three hundred years, so that the people who died on that rock wouldn't just be numbers in a Company's books. And then somebody took the bell away from them. Took it out through the Throat, before the end, and left them with nothing to cut their names on. I don't know who did that, or why. But I've had it all my life, and I've never once felt it was mine." She ran her thumb along the dense grey band. "It's theirs. Whoever's left."

"There may be nobody left."

"Then it can go back to the rock. Back on whatever hook it came off. It'll still be home." She looked at him, with the lamp in her lap throwing its light up under her face. "Your turn. Why are you here?"

"I'm paid to be."

"Everybody's paid to be. That isn't what I asked."

He was quiet for a long time. Then, because they were inside a throat, in the long red dark between one universe and the next, where it seemed — the books were right about this much — that ordinary rules had been left on the other side, he told her. The water-office on Kessane. His father's hand shaking on the stylus. Thirty-five years. The discharge in Haste's pocket, wanting only a seal.

She listened without interrupting. When he was done she said, "So you're bonded."

"Everybody's bonded."

"I'm not."

"Then you're the first person I've met in ten years who isn't."

"My great-grandmother paid ours off. She sold a painting. It was the only thing we owned worth anything except this." She touched the bell. "She could have sold this instead. A bell off a lost colony ship — there are collectors who'd have paid ten times what the painting fetched. She didn't. She sold the painting and kept the bell, and paid the family out of bond, and died in a rented room." Fen smiled a little, not happily. "We don't really know why. She just said it wasn't hers to sell."

The hull shuddered again, harder. Somewhere below them something metal rang, once, like a struck pipe; and very faintly, as if in answer, the bell under Fen's hand hummed.

They both looked at it.

"Sympathetic resonance," said Fen, after a moment. "It does that. It's a bell."

"Yes," said Corvin.

Neither of them looked away from it for some time.

* * *

They came out of the Throat into a system that did not answer.

The red thinned and tore and fell away behind them, and there were stars — different stars, a different sky, the shapes of the constellations all broken and rearranged — and a sun. It was not much of a sun. It was a dim orange disc, much smaller than Kessane's, so faint you could look straight at it through the screen filters. And off to one side of it, a little way along the ecliptic, a great banded ball the colour of ochre and old bruises, with a thin hard line of ring drawn across its face.

"Sabra," said Ode. "And that's Nhai. The gas giant. Your rock's in orbit round it, if it's anywhere." She was grey with tiredness; she had not left the chair in eleven hours. "Comms. What have we got?"

The young comm officer had been listening since the red began to thin. He took his headset off and looked at her and then at Haste and then at Corvin, as though he would rather not say it to any of them.

"Nothing, Captain."

"Nothing on what?"

"On anything. I've been through every band in the Company's 2387 tables. Traffic control, haulers, emergency, the Concern's own channels. The throat-watch station's frequency. Then everything else. Nothing." He hesitated. "It's very quiet, Captain. Quieter than empty space usually is. Nobody's even leaking."

"The _Pellucid_? The _Harrow_?"

"Both through. I've got their transponders, both heading in-system. But they're not talking either. Not to us."

"Course they're not. They're racing." Ode rubbed her eyes. "And the rock?"

"Can't see it." The navigator answered this time, an older woman bent over a plot. "Lantern's orbit puts it on the far side of Nhai from us right now. It'll be behind the planet for days yet. We'll need to come round."

"How long?"

"At our best burn? Twelve days to clear the limb."

Twelve days. Corvin found himself looking at the banded face of the gas giant, at the hard bright line of the ring across it, and thinking that somewhere behind that, in its shadow, a rock eleven kilometres long had been turning for seven hundred and two years; and that nobody on this ship had the faintest idea whether there was a single living thing inside it.

"Silence is an answer of a kind," said Haste, behind him. He had come up onto the bridge without anyone hearing; he did that. "It narrows the possibilities. Captain, you'll lay a course for the rock, please, at your best burn. Mr. Saal, I'd like you to begin preparing for both contingencies. A populated habitat and an empty one. I expect you'll need a different speech for each."

"I'll need a different speech for each," Corvin agreed.

When Haste had gone, Ode looked at Corvin for a long moment, from under her grey brows.

"Different speech for each," she said. "Mine's the same either way. God help them."

* * *

Tol Brask found him two hours later, in the narrow corridor outside the engine spaces, where Corvin had gone to be somewhere that was not his cabin.

"You're the adjuster," said Tol. It was the first thing he had said directly to Corvin since Ambry.

"I am."

"You can read a power board?"

"After a fashion."

Tol jerked his head at the wall panel beside him, where a grid of little lights and figures showed what each section of the ship was drawing from the engine. He put one long greasy finger on a line near the bottom. HOLD 2 — SEALED. "Read that."

Corvin read it. He read it twice. The figure was larger than the figure for the bridge, the galley, and all the crew quarters together.

"That's a lot of power for seed," he said.

"That's a lot of power for anything," said Tol. "That's not a seed draw. I've hauled seed. Seed sits in a cold box and sips. That's a deep-cold draw. That's a cryo draw. That's the kind of draw you get from something you've got to keep at four degrees above nothing every second of every day, or it spoils." He took his finger off the board. "Captain doesn't want to know. Captain's got a mortgage. I'm not asking you to do anything. I'm just telling you what the board says. I tell the captain what the board says, every day, and now I've told you."

"Why me?"

Tol looked at him with his flat, absorbed, engineer's eyes.

"Because you're the one who's going to stand in front of them," he said, "and explain."

He went back into the engine spaces and shut the door. Corvin stood in the corridor for a long time with his hand on the cold wall, feeling through it the deep steady thrum of the _Good Title_'s great engine, driving them in toward the planet and the rock behind it; and, under that, so faint he could not be certain it was there at all, a second, lower note, steady as breath, coming up from somewhere aft.

# Chapter 7: Strike

*The Closing · 2387 CE*

They met in the old chapel on Gallery Four, because it was the only room on Lantern with no Company camera in it. The Company had never thought a chapel worth watching.

It was a long room cut into the rock with a low ceiling and benches made of hauler crate-wood, and at the far end, where in a chapel on a planet there might have been an altar, there was a window of coloured resin somebody had poured in the colony's first decade: a crude picture of a ship crossing a dark sea toward a lamp. The _Ardent Mercy_, coming to Lantern. The resin had yellowed. The lamp in the picture had gone the colour of tea. Under it, on the night of the fourteenth day of the Closing, sixty-one Speakers from every crew on the rock sat shoulder to shoulder on the crate-wood benches and argued for four hours.

Dagny let them. She had learned that from Hesketh, years ago, when she was a new Speaker and wanted every meeting to end in the first ten minutes. _People need to say it_, the old man had told her. _Even if it's been said. Especially if it's been said. You can't ask a man to risk his door for a thing he hasn't heard himself say out loud._ So she let them say it. That the Company was leaving. That the debt was staying. That the dimming line was a rope with a noose on the end, and that six hundred extra credits only made the rope longer. That there were children on Gallery Nine already sleeping in the corridors because their doors had dimmed. That the farms couldn't feed the rock without the Company's refinery-tech. That the stills couldn't run without the Company's spare parts. That the Company would take the parts. That the Company would take the fuel. That the Company would take everything except the debt and the dead.

At the end of the fourth hour, when people had stopped saying new things and begun saying old ones again more loudly, Mariam Oyelowo stood up in the front row, slow and broad, and said:

"The _Covenant_ eats."

The room quietened. Mariam did not raise her voice; she never did.

"Fourteen hundred people, eight months to the nearest Concord port, that's what my sister in the Company kitchens says. Fourteen hundred mouths for eight months. Where's that coming from? Not the Concord. There's no ship from the Concord. It's coming off the strip-farms. Off my farms. The Company's been taking our harvest into the _Covenant_'s cold holds for a week. They need two more harvests to provision her. Maybe three." She looked round at them, her face heavy and calm. "Farms don't grow in the dark."

Nobody said anything for a moment. Then everybody turned and looked at Dagny.

"The Wick," said somebody at the back.

"The Wick," said Dagny.

She stood up. Her knees hurt; they always did, now, after a long sitting. She went to the front, under the yellowed window, and turned and faced them.

"I can bring the lamp down," she said. "Not out. Never out — if it goes out, it may not light again, and then we're all dead in a week and the Company's won. But down. To a twilight. Enough to keep the scrubbers' bacteria alive and the water from freezing in the mains. Not enough to grow a crop." She looked at Mariam. "How long before the farms suffer?"

"Three days, the first beds start to turn. Ten and the harvest's gone."

"Then we tell them we'll bring it back up," Dagny said, "the day the Director goes into the root of the Tally with her seal and takes the dimming line out of it. Not lower it. Take it out. Nobody on this rock dimmed again, for anything, ever. Let them have their trust. Let them have their debt on paper and their books full of numbers. But nobody goes into a lock again and doesn't come out because the number's wrong."

"She'll say she can't," said Hesketh, from his bench, leaning on his stick.

"She can. Basu told me so." It was the first time she had said it aloud, and she felt the room take it in. "With the seal, at the root, she can do anything the Tally can do. She can take the line out. She won't, because a debt nobody's made to pay is worth nothing to anybody who might buy it. That's the only reason. Not the air. Not the safety. The price." She held up her wrist, the chit on it. "So we make the price higher."

There was a long silence. Then Joss Teague, sitting on the end of the second bench, with his bad knees stuck out into the aisle, said in his big cracked voice: "Wick crews are in."

"Pumps are in," said Hesketh.

"Farms," said Mariam.

"Stills."

"Docks."

It went round the room. Not everybody. The hauler crews held back, and the noodle-sellers' association, and a few of the old Gallery Two families who had lived nearest the Upper Wards for three generations and were not going to start being brave now. But enough. Dagny counted the hands, and counted them again, and then looked up at the tea-coloured lamp in the window over her head and thought, absurdly, that she ought to say a prayer, and did not know one.

"Tomorrow," she said. "Second shift. We bring her down."

* * *

There were forty collimators on the Wick, ring after ring of them down the three-kilometre length of the lamp, each a squat grey housing the size of a cupboard that shaped the magnetic bottle at that point and so decided how much light the lamp gave out there. Every Wick crew on Lantern knew how to trim one. You did it every shift, a little this way, a little that, to keep the light even as the fire inside the bottle wandered. Nobody had ever trimmed all forty at once, together, all the way down.

They did it at the start of second shift. Dagny took the master ring at the root of the lamp, by the Counting House, where she could see the whole length of the spine. Joss had the middle. Every collimator had a crew on it, two or three to a housing, clipped to the rails in the hard white light, waiting with their hands on the trim wheels and their eyes on her.

She raised her arm. She lowered it.

The light went down.

Not fast. The fire inside the bottle did not like to be squeezed, and fought it, and they had to coax it, a quarter-turn at a time, watching the gauges, easing it down by degrees. But it went. Dagny felt it before she saw it — a coolness on the backs of her hands, on her face, as if a cloud had passed over a sun she had never seen. Then the shadows began to lengthen. Then, all along the curving walls of the world, the terraces went from white to gold, and from gold to amber, and from amber to a deep slow blue, like the dusk in the picture-books Pim had read when he was small, from planets, where the sun went down.

It took twenty minutes. When it was done Lantern lay in twilight, and the Wick hung down the middle of it not as a sword any longer but as a dim, smouldering bar, like a coal blown on and left.

Dagny clipped off at the master ring and looked down.

She had expected noise. Shouting, sirens, the Company screens all lighting up at once. There was nothing. All over the rock, on every terrace, people had come out of their houses and were standing looking up. She could see them. Thousands of them, small and still, faces turned toward the lamp. Nobody spoke. Nobody moved. On Lantern you were taught at five not to look straight at the Wick, because it would burn your eyes; and here were nine thousand people looking straight at it, and it was not burning, and they could see it, for the first time in their lives, as a thing that someone had made.

On the Gallery Six terrace somebody began to sing. Dagny could not hear the words from so high. It was one of the old crossing songs, she thought, from the tune — one of the ones the _Mercy_'s people had brought, about a ship and a dark sea and a light on the far shore. Another voice took it up. Then a gallery's worth. It went round the curve of the world, terrace to terrace, under the dim red lamp, the way the bell's voice went round the Dock Ring, until it seemed to Dagny that the whole rock was singing, very quietly, to itself.

"Mum."

Pim was beside her on the master ring. He was not supposed to be on the Wick at all, let alone at the root of it, but he had come up the spoke-shaft behind the crews and nobody had had the heart to send him down. He was looking at the twilight with his mouth open.

"It's beautiful," he said. "Why's it beautiful?"

"Because it's ours," said Dagny, and was surprised to find her throat had closed on it.

Below them, across the gulf of the dim air, in the Counting House, the long window of the Director's office was a strip of hard white light: the only full-lit thing left on Lantern. While she watched, a figure came and stood in it, small and dark against the light, and stood there, looking up the length of the dimmed Wick toward her, for a long time.

* * *

The Company let it go for two days. On the third, Varga came up the Wick.

He came at shift-change, which was clever: the moment when there were most crews on the spine and least of them clipped on. He came up all four spoke-shafts at once with forty men in grey, in pressure armour, with stunners and with slug-guns slung across their backs, and he came fast. Dagny was at the master ring with the crews when the alarms on the spoke-hatches went, one after another, like bells, and she had time to shout once — "_Clip on! Everybody clip on!_" — before the first of them was out of the shaft and onto the crawl and coming hand over hand toward the collimators.

There was no fighting near the axis. Not real fighting. Nobody had weight there; you couldn't swing a punch, you couldn't plant your feet. What there was, was a long slow struggle of bodies on the rails, crews clinging to their trim wheels and Varga's men prising them off, one finger at a time, and the stunners going with their flat ugly crack, and men and women going limp on their tethers and floating out from the crawl like weed in water. It was almost quiet. That was what she remembered afterwards. How quiet it was.

She saw it happen to Joss because she was looking at him. He was on the middle ring, a hundred metres along, with two of his crew; he had his big arms wrapped round the housing of the twentieth collimator and his face turned toward her, and he was grinning, the mad grin he had when a job went bad and there was nothing to do but enjoy it. A man in grey was coming along the crawl toward him. Joss let go of the housing with one arm and swung at him — a stupid, slow, weightless swing that would never have connected — and the man in grey, startled, did what he had been trained to do with a target that moved. He fired.

Not the stunner. The slug-gun.

The shot made almost no sound in the thin air at the axis. Joss jerked once. Then he let go of the housing. His tether had not been clipped — he had unclipped it to swing — and he simply drifted out, away from the crawl, away from the lamp, turning very slowly, with his arms spread and his big body loose, into the open air at the centre of the world.

Dagny screamed his name. She did not remember doing it. She remembered the sound coming back to her off the curve of the rock.

At the axis, a body with no weight can drift for a long time. Then the spin finds it. Joss drifted out from the Wick for nearly a minute, so slowly that she thought — she let herself think — that somebody would reach him, that a crew would get a line to him, that he would come back. Then the slow invisible hand of the rock's turning took hold of him, and he began, very gently at first, to fall. Not down. Out. Toward the curving wall of the world, toward the terraces of the Galleries, which were spinning round underneath him at four hundred kilometres an hour and which he would meet, when he met them, at that speed.

She made herself watch. Someone had to. He fell for a long time, getting smaller, turning, through the twilight. She lost him against the terraces of Gallery Nineteen. She did not see him land. She saw, a long way down, a sudden small stir among the people on the terrace there, like a stone thrown into a pond.

When she turned round, Hale Varga was on the master ring beside her, clipped on, in his pressure armour with the faceplate up. His face was grey. He was looking where she had looked.

"I didn't order that," he said.

"You brought guns onto the Wick."

"I didn't order it." He turned his head and looked at her, and there was something in his face she had not expected and did not want: a sort of sickness. "Bring the lamp up, Ostrova. Please. Before anybody else goes off it."

She looked past him. All along the spine his men held the collimators now, their crews stunned on their tethers or pinned against the rails. Down at the far end of the master ring a man in grey had his arm across the throat of a boy, holding him clipped to the rail, and the boy was Pim.

"Let him go," she said.

"Bring the lamp up."

She brought the lamp up.

* * *

They took her to the Counting House afterwards, to the long window, and the Director told her what it would cost.

Every Speaker who had voted for the strike: five hundred credits, posted to their accounts at the hour, for the loss of three days' light to the Company's farms. Every crewman who had held a collimator: two hundred. The farms' lost harvest, which was the Company's property, apportioned across every account on the rock according to its share of the light. When the Tally had finished posting, four hundred and eleven doors on Lantern had dimmed that had not been dimmed the day before. Dagny's was one of them.

"I'm sorry about Mr. Teague," said Ruth Achterberg. She stood at her window with her back to the room, looking up the length of the Wick, which blazed again white and hard from end to end as if nothing had happened. "I did not want anyone hurt. Mr. Varga has disciplined the man who fired. I want you to understand that I have nothing against you, Ms. Ostrova. You were doing what your people asked. I'm doing what mine ask. In thirty-five days we'll both be relieved of it."

"You could take the line out," said Dagny. Her voice came out cracked, as if it belonged to someone else. "Today. Go down to the root and take it out. Nobody dimmed again. What does it cost you? You'll be gone."

The Director turned from the window.

"It costs a debt that nobody has to pay," she said. "And a debt that nobody has to pay is not a debt. It's a story. The Company does not hold stories in trust, Ms. Ostrova. It holds assets. You have a son, I understand. When he is grown, and the Throat opens, and somebody comes to ask what this place is worth, I'd like there to be an answer. That's all the line is for." She sat down at her bare desk. "Mr. Varga will see you down."

Varga saw her down. In the lift he stood with his faceplate under his arm and said nothing at all until the doors opened on the spine-road at the foot of the Wick. Then, as she stepped out, he said, low, not looking at her:

"His name's Corrie. The man who shot your friend. He's twenty-two. He's from Gallery Eleven."

"Is that supposed to make it better?"

"No," said Varga. "It's supposed to make you know who did it. Somebody ought to." And the doors closed.

* * *

Odile rang the bell for Joss Teague at nine that night, and the whole Dock Ring stopped for him, and then went on.

Dagny watched her cut the name. She had brought Pim, because there was nowhere to leave him — their door on Six was dimmed, and would stay dimmed until she found five hundred credits she did not have — and he sat on the bench under the Hook with his knees drawn up and his face closed, and did not look at the bell at all. He had not spoken since the Wick. When she had gone to him at the end of the master ring, after Varga's man let him go, he had let her hold him and said nothing, and he had said nothing since.

The engraver whined. _Joss Teague._ It came up bright on the bronze, high on the shoulder, just under Bronwen Price, just under Lemuel Adeyemi.

"I'm nearly out," said Odile, when she had finished. She said it differently this time. Not as a joke. "Look. There's the shoulder and the crown, and then there's nothing but the hook."

"There's the band on the waist," said Dagny. "The plain one."

"That's the founders' band. You don't cut the founders' band." Odile put the engraver down. Her hands, which had been still as stone on the bronze, had begun to shake again. "Thirty years I've kept her. Ninety-one years she's hung there. And now there's three hundred Company men going to walk past her up the ramp to their ship and not one of them's ever going to have his name on her, and she's full." She looked at the bell for a long moment. "Joss used to buy me a drink every Christmas. He said it was for keeping his mother. I cut his mother twelve years ago."

Dagny did not know what to say. So she said nothing, and sat down on the bench beside her son, and the three of them stayed there under the bell, as they had a week before, while the Dock Ring went about its night business round them.

That was where Teodor Basu found them.

He came along the ring road in a dark coat, without his Company suit, and without any of the two or three security men who usually walked a little behind Company officers on the lower rings. He looked as if he had not slept since she last saw him. He stopped in front of the bench and looked at Pim, and at Odile, and then at Dagny.

"I'm— I'm sorry," he said. "About Mr. Teague."

"Everyone's sorry."

"Yes." He sat down, uninvited, on the end of the bench, very upright, his hands on his knees. "I've come to tell you two things. I don't know if either of them is any use. I've decided that isn't for me to judge any longer."

Dagny waited.

"The first is about the keys." He spoke low and fast, without the stammer, the way he had on the Wick. "I told you the Director's seal never leaves her. That's true. And mine never leaves me. That's true too. But there's one more time they must be used together, before the _Covenant_ goes. On the morning of departure the Director and I go down into the root, together, and close the Company's own accounts on the Tally — the staff accounts, the Concern's operating accounts — and transfer the trust. It's in the charter. It has to be done by both keys, at the root, in person. It will take about twenty minutes." He looked at her. "That's the only time, between now and the Throat, that both keys will be in the same room as the root. With the door open."

Dagny was very still.

"And the second thing?" she said.

"The second thing is something I don't understand." Basu looked down at his hands. "I keep the _Covenant_'s manifest. Every crate. Every cassette. I've been through it a dozen times, because it's my job, and because— because I wanted to see what was being taken. There's one item on it I didn't put there. It came aboard under Mr. Varga's seal, from a Concord military shipment, three months ago, before the throat-watch said anything at all. It's listed as _device, Concord ordnance, class T_. It's in a shielded bay behind the drive. I asked the Director what it was. She told me it was a precaution." He raised his eyes. "I've worked for her for twenty years, Ms. Ostrova. I know when she's telling me the truth. I know when she isn't. She wasn't."

"What's class T?"

"I don't know." He stood up. "I'm going to find out. I think— I think I may not like it."

He went away along the ring road, very upright, into the night. Dagny sat and looked after him. Beside her, Odile had picked up the engraver again and was turning it in her shaking fingers.

"That man," Odile said, "has done something he can't carry."

"Yes."

"They always come to the bell in the end. The ones who can't carry it." She looked up at the bronze, at the bright new name on the shoulder. "Dagny."

"What?"

"If you're going to make them pay," said the old woman, "I'll need more bell."

# Chapter 8: The Salt

*The Owing · 311 AC*

The counter came back three days later, which Tamsin had not expected, and he came in plain clothes, which she had expected even less.

She almost didn't know him. Without the grey coat he was only a thin young man with soft hands and a clean face and hair cut the way they cut it in the Upper Wards, short at the sides, and he stood in the doorway of the Hask room holding a cloth bag against his chest as if it might be taken off him, which on Twenty-Two it might.

"I didn't use the chit," he said, before anybody could speak. "On the door. I knocked. I wanted you to know that."

"Door was open," said Tamsin.

"I knocked anyway."

Agathe was asleep, or pretending to be; she slept a great deal now, propped on her bunk with her mouth a little open and her breath whistling. Jory was on the top bunk with his knees up, watching the counter with bright interested eyes. Tamsin was mending her rig's heater-line at the table with a soldering pen and a borrowed light.

"Well?" she said.

He came in and shut the door behind him, and stood there.

"The correction was refused," he said.

She put the soldering pen down.

"Not refused. Deferred. Until after the lists are posted." He was speaking quickly, looking at the floor. "The Count doesn't allow corrections during a Calling. In case— so that the line holds. I went to the Comptroller-General himself. He says he'll approve it, afterwards. He gave me his word."

"After the lists are posted," said Tamsin, "she'll be in the Cold."

"Yes."

"So what's his word worth? Down there?"

"I don't know," said Orrin Sallow. He looked up at her. "That's why I came."

She didn't understand him for a moment. Then she did, and something tightened in her chest that she did not let reach her face.

"You came to tell us," she said. "That's all. You came down twenty galleries in your second-best clothes to tell my mother she's going into the dark with two thousand credits on her that aren't hers, so you'd feel better about it."

"No. I came because it isn't right, and I can't make it right, and I didn't want you to hear it from a list." He held out the cloth bag. "And I brought food. It's not much. It's what my mother keeps in the house. Bread. Some cheese. Real cheese. I didn't know what you— I'm sorry."

Tamsin looked at the bag and did not take it. On the top bunk, Jory sat up.

"He can stay," Jory said. "If he's got cheese."

"Jory."

"I'm only saying." Her brother swung his legs down, dropped lightly to the floor, and held out his hand to the counter, palm up. In it was a stylus. A good one, Upper Wards make, with a silver clip. "Here. You dropped it."

Orrin looked at the stylus. Then at Jory. Then, unexpectedly, he laughed — a short, startled sound, as though it had been surprised out of him. "I didn't drop it."

"No," Jory agreed. "But you can have it back. For the cheese."

Orrin took the stylus. Tamsin took the bag. Nobody said anything for a while. On the bunk, Agathe Hask's breath went on whistling in and out, in and out, each breath posting, somewhere far above them in the Count's vault, to an account that was two thousand credits deeper than it should have been.

"Why does it matter to you?" Tamsin said at last. "Really. There's ten thousand on that list. You'll have four hundred of them on your slate. Some of them must be wrong too. Why us?"

He thought about it. She watched him think about it, and was annoyed to find she believed him when he answered.

"Because I checked yours," he said. "I haven't finished the others."

* * *

She went to the Salt that night.

Wenna Blackwood was where she had been before, behind the cargo-net curtain at the back of Brannock's cabin, with the stove and the rug and a pot of something on the table. She looked up when Tamsin came in and did not seem surprised.

"Your counter came back," she said.

"How do you know that?"

"Because you're here." Wenna pushed out a chair with her boot. "Sit."

Tamsin sat. "Half," she said. "Of what's in the store. You carry. You stand my tools and my air."

"And your mother's tin."

"And my mother's tin. And something else."

The one eye considered her.

"If it's fuel," said Tamsin. "If there's cassettes in there. I want one. Not to sell. To give to the Wickwrights."

There was a pause. Then Wenna Blackwood laughed, a big rough sound like gravel going down a chute.

"To give to the Wickwrights," she said. "You hear that, Brannock? The girl wants to light the lamp." She wiped her eye. "Child. If there's a single live cassette behind that door — which there won't be, because the Company took every one it could carry and burnt the rest getting out — it's worth more than every soul on Twenty-Two put together. I'm not giving it to a guild of blind old men so the Count can burn it on the Upper Wards' tomato beds."

"Then I'm not cutting your door."

"It's not my door. It's not your door either." Wenna leaned back. "But all right. All right. If there's fuel, one's yours, to do as you like with. Burn it in your stove for all I care. Two's yours. And if there's not — and there won't be — you've still got half, and your tools, and a winter's air." She held out her hand across the table. It was huge and scarred and missing the top of one finger. "Done?"

Tamsin looked at the hand.

"There's a body," she said. "By the door. A woman. She's holding a shoe. Your people don't touch her. I move her. And they don't touch anything small."

Something changed in Wenna Blackwood's face. It was there and gone, too quick to name.

"Done," she said.

Tamsin took the hand.

Behind her, from the doorway, Jory said, "I'm coming."

She had not heard him follow her. She turned round, and he was there in the gap of the cargo-net curtain, all elbows, with his chin up.

"No," she said.

"I'm coming. I can carry. I'm good on a tether. You know I am. And if Mam goes into the Cold—" his voice cracked, and he pushed through it— "if Mam goes into the Cold I'm going to need to know the way."

* * *

It took a day to get the lance in.

Wenna sent four cutters: Dobbs, a slab of a man who said nothing all day, and three others whose names Tamsin did not bother to learn, with sledges and a thermal lance and two tanks of oxygen and enough tether to rope off half the Cold. They went in at the Lip in the dead of third shift, when the Count's bailiffs were thinnest on the lower roads, and Tamsin led them by her old way through the back of Pump Gallery Fourteen and down the service way that ended at the door.

The woman was where Tamsin had left her, sitting with her back to the hatch and the shoe held against her chest. Dobbs looked at her and then at Tamsin and stood back. Tamsin knelt and put her arms round the dried, light, terrible body, and lifted her — she weighed less than a child — and carried her ten metres back along the service way and set her down against the wall in the same position, knees up, the shoe in her hands, facing the door. Jory watched. He did not say anything.

Then they lit the lance.

The weld was good. Tamsin had known it would be. Whoever had laid it had laid it as if they meant it to outlast them, a wide even bead all the way round the frame, and the steel under it was thick, old Company plate. The lance ate into it slowly, white and roaring, with the steel running red down the face of the door and freezing as it ran into long black icicles of slag. They took it in turns. It was hot, hard, choking work, in masks, in the cold, with the sweat freezing in their rigs, and it took them eleven hours. At the end of it Dobbs put a pry-bar the length of a man into the gap and leaned on it, and two of the others leaned with him, and the wheel at the centre of the door, which none of them had dared touch, turned on its own a quarter-circle with a groan like an animal, and the hatch of Aft Store 3 swung open into the dark.

The air that came out was colder than the Cold.

They stood back from it and let it settle. Then Tamsin put her brow-lamp up and went in.

It was a long room. Longer than she had imagined. A vault cut into the raw rock of the outer wall, forty metres deep, with a rounded roof, and down the length of it on both sides, in rows of three, racks: tall steel cradles with clamps and cables and coffin-shaped hollows in them, each hollow the size of a man. Tamsin had never seen a fuel cassette. Nobody below the Wickwrights had. But she knew what had stood in those hollows as soon as she saw them. There were a hundred of them. Two hundred. More.

They were all empty.

Dobbs came in behind her with a bigger lamp and swung it along the racks, slowly, all the way to the end. Empty. Every one. The clamps hung open. The cables lay coiled on the floor where somebody had unplugged them and dropped them. At the far end, on a pallet, there was a heap of what looked like spare clamps and a sealed medical locker with a red cross on it, frosted white.

"Well," said Dobbs, the only word he said all day. "There's your fuel."

Tamsin did not answer. She was looking at the wall beside the door.

There was a paper on it. An actual paper, a Company docket in a plastic sleeve, pinned to a board at eye height where somebody signing things out would see it. The plastic had gone yellow and brittle and the paper inside was brown, but the cold had kept it, and the print was clear. She did not read well. She read slowly, moving her lips, putting her light on each line.

TRANSFER DOCKET — AFT STORE 3.
CASSETTES 1–212 TO WICK STORE, SPINE ROAD. FOR THE LAMP.
STORE TO BE SEALED ON COMPLETION.

And a date she could not make out, and a stamp, the two tired letters, H and A, and under the stamp a signature. She could read that, just. It was small and neat and careful, the hand of somebody who signed a great many things and wanted each of them to be legible. _T. Basu._

"Two hundred and twelve," she said aloud. "For the lamp."

"So that's where the Wick's fuel came from," said one of the cutters. "Out of here. Three hundred years back. Huh."

"So that's where it went," said Tamsin. She did not know why she said it like that. She took the docket off the board, sleeve and all, and folded it carefully, and put it inside her rig.

It was Jory who found the other thing. He had gone along the back of the racks with his own light, the way he went everywhere, sideways and quick, and he had stopped at the end nearest the door, in a corner behind the last rack where somebody had heaped up a nest of old grey insulation blankets. He was standing very still.

"Tam," he said, in a voice she had not heard him use since he was small.

She went. She looked. Then she put her hand on his shoulder and turned him round, gently, and walked him back toward the door, and went back on her own.

They were very small bones. They were mostly hidden in the blankets, which was something. And on the rock wall above the nest, scratched in with something sharp — a buckle, maybe, or a spoon — at the height a child could reach, there was a column of short lines. Tally marks. Tamsin counted them because she could not help it. Eleven.

Under the eleven marks, in big uneven letters, the kind a child would make who had only lately learned them, there were five words.

SHE SAID SHE WOULD COME BACK.

Tamsin stood there for a long time. Then she went out of the store and along the service way to where she had set the woman down against the wall with the red shoe in her hands, facing the door that had been welded shut ten metres from her for three hundred years; and she knelt down in front of her and could not, for a while, see anything at all.

When she came back, Dobbs and the others had already begun to unbolt the empty racks for the steel. Nobody had touched the corner. Nobody looked at her.

"Leave the blankets," she said. "And the wall."

"We know," said Dobbs.

* * *

Orrin Sallow had been told by the Comptroller-General himself to finish his four hundred, sign his sightings, and stop puzzling over the root. He finished his four hundred. He signed his sightings. Then he went back into the archive, because once a counter has seen a number that will not sit right he cannot stop seeing it, and because he had begun to understand, in the days since he had stood in the Hask room, that he was going to be ashamed of himself either way and would rather be ashamed of something he had done.

He did not go back into the root. He was not that brave, yet. He went into the light.

Every account on the Tally was charged for light, as for air and water and heat. Every counter knew the rate. What Orrin had never looked at, because no counter ever had any reason to look at it, was the other side of the light ledger: not what the Owing were charged for the Wick, but what the Wick was given. Fuel in, light out. The Wickwrights reported to the Count every quarter how much of the last cassette was spent, and the Count posted it, and the posting went into a ledger that nobody read.

He read it. He read three hundred years of it in two nights, sitting late in the empty third hall with the lamps turned down to save the light he was reading about.

It was very simple. At the Closing the Founders had held a stock of cassettes for the Wick. They had burned them, one at a time, through three centuries, shortening the lamp each time a cassette ran low, as the Count had always said. The stock was nearly gone. The last cassette was in the Wick now, and Ninian the Wickwright's quarterly report put it at a fifth remaining. That was the gutter. That was what the Comptroller-General had said at the Hook. All true.

And then, at the very bottom of the ledger, under the long column of spent cassettes, there was a second line that had not changed in three hundred years. A single entry, carried forward every quarter, posted on the Founders' authority in the first year after the Closing, and then never touched again.

RESERVE — HULL 7731. 14 UNITS. HELD.

Fourteen units.

He did not know what Hull 7731 was. He had never heard the words. They were not in the catechism, or the Account Day tours, or the rolls of the Count's property. He searched the archive for them and found them nowhere else at all.

But he knew what a unit was, in the light ledger. Every line above it said so. A unit was a cassette.

He sat in the dark hall for a long time, with the faint hum of the Tally coming up through the floor — or a pump, or the Hulk, or nothing — and thought about fourteen cassettes, held, for three hundred years, while the Wick was shortened and shortened over the Galleries and the Cold crept up from the aft end of the world. He thought about ten thousand accounts on a list. He thought about a woman on Twenty-Two asking him who Caul was.

Then he wrote _Hull 7731_ on the back of his hand, in ink, with the stylus that a boy had stolen from him and given back for cheese, and went home.

# Chapter 9: Dead Air

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

The throat-watch station was where the Company's charts said it would be, which was the first thing in the Sabra system that had been.

It hung forty thousand kilometres sunward of the Throat, in the slow orbit the Company's engineers had given it seven hundred years ago, a squat grey cylinder the size of a house with a ring of dishes round one end and a long boom at the other carrying instruments nobody aboard the _Good Title_ could name. It was dark. Its dishes were pointed, still, at the place where the Throat had been and was now again, a pink bruise on the sky behind it. Frost had grown on it from somewhere — outgassing, Tol said, from its own seals, over centuries — so that it looked, in the ship's floodlights, like something that had been left out overnight in a garden.

"It's an asset of the Concern," Haste had said, at breakfast, in the voice of a man reading from a list, "and its records may be of value in establishing the circumstances of the Closing. Mr. Saal will go across. Miss Adaire will go with him, since she reads the formats. Captain, you'll lend them your engineer."

"I'll lend them my engineer," Ode had said, "and I'll want him back."

So the three of them went across, in suits, on a line from the _Good Title_'s lock, with the dark between them and the station and the stars very still. Corvin had done vacuum work on the colony circuit, a little, the way adjusters did — boarding hulks to tag them, walking frozen domes. He did not like it. He liked it less with the bruise of the Throat over his shoulder, breathing.

Tol got the station's lock open with a pry-bar and a good deal of muttering. Inside there was no air. There was a short corridor, very narrow, lined with lockers whose doors had sprung open over the years, and at the end of it a round room with a ring of consoles and a single chair.

There was a man in the chair.

He had been dead a very long time. The cold and the vacuum had kept him, after a fashion: he was dry and dark and sunken, his face drawn back from his teeth, his hands shrunk to claws on the arms of the chair. He wore a Company coverall with the two leaning letters on the breast, and a name-strip that said ROACH, and he was sitting facing the main console, where a single dead screen looked back at him. On the console in front of him there was a cup, a paper logbook, and a pen.

Nobody said anything for a while. Their breath sounded very loud in the suits.

"He stayed," said Fen at last. "At his post. To the end."

"Or he was left at it," said Corvin.

She looked at him through her faceplate. Then she looked away.

Tol went to the console banks and started opening panels, muttering to the machines in the way he did. Fen went to the logbook. She did not touch the dead man; she reached past him, very carefully, as if he might wake, and slid the book out from under his hand. Its pages were brittle with cold. She turned them with the tip of a gloved finger.

"Readings," she said. "Every six hours. Columns of figures. I don't know what they mean." She turned more pages. "Then notes. Personal. He's lonely. He writes about a woman called Hennie on Lantern. Something about her garden." Another page. "Then— it changes." Her voice changed with it. "The columns stop. Just notes. _Asked the Concern again for confirmation of the closing-phase figures. No reply._ And then a few days later, _No reply._ And then _No reply_ again. And then nothing for a long time, and then just a line."

"What does it say?"

Fen held the book up so that his helmet lamp fell on the page. The hand was a scrawl, the letters crowding, as if written in the dark or in a hurry or by someone very cold.

_They told me someone would come for me._

That was all. Under it the page was blank, and all the pages after.

"Right," said Tol, from somewhere inside a console with only his boots showing. "I've got the core. It's intact. Cold kept it. But it's locked six ways to Sunday with Company keys, and I'm no codebreaker."

"I am," said Fen. "Bring it."

They took the core and the logbook. They left the man. Corvin stood a moment longer than the others in the round room, looking at him in his chair, with his dead screen and his cup and his name-strip, ROACH, and thought about four hundred and twelve settlements and the faces on them, and a water-office on Kessane; and then, because nobody else was going to, he reached out and closed what was left of the man's eyelids, which did not close.

"Corvin," said Fen, from the corridor.

"Coming."

* * *

Haste wanted to talk about the instrument. He wanted to talk about it most evenings, in the _Good Title_'s cramped wardroom, over a glass of the good Kessane brandy he had brought aboard in a case of his own, and Corvin had come to understand that this was not because Haste needed to explain it to him. It was because Haste liked to hear it.

"Persons and issue," Haste said, turning the glass. "Do you know, I think it's the most beautiful phrase in commercial law? Persons. And issue. A debt attaches to the person who incurred it, and to every person who comes out of them, until it's paid. It sounds monstrous when you put it like that. People do put it like that. But think what it means." He held the glass to the light. "It means a promise outlives the man who made it. It means that what you owe doesn't vanish because you've had the good fortune to die. That's not monstrous, Mr. Saal. That's civilisation. That's the whole of it. A world where promises die with the promisor is a world where nobody will lend you anything at all."

"The people on Lantern didn't make the promise," said Corvin. "Their ancestors did. Seven hundred years ago. Most of them under contract to people who owned the air they were breathing."

"Their ancestors signed. Freely, under the law of the day. In return for passage to a new world, which they received." Haste sipped. "Your father signed for you, too. You've never once in thirty-five years suggested to me that his signature was void."

Corvin said nothing.

At the end of the wardroom table Sergeant Kell had a slug-gun in pieces on an oilcloth and was cleaning it, part by part, with the slow absorbed care of a man shelling peas. He did not look up. He had not said a word all evening. He seemed to Corvin like part of the furniture: something solid and practical bolted to the deck, waiting.

"I've been meaning to ask," said Corvin. "About the hold."

"Agricultural stock," said Haste, without a pause.

"Your agricultural stock draws more power than the rest of the ship together. The engineer showed me the board."

"Does it?" Haste raised his eyebrows, mildly. "I'm no engineer. I imagine it's the embryo library. Embryos are delicate. They have to be kept very cold." He set his glass down. "Is there a reason you're interested, Mr. Saal? Beyond the general curiosity of a careful man?"

There it was. The pleasant voice, and under it the other thing. Corvin had heard that voice in his own mouth on four hundred and twelve settlements: _is there a reason you're interested?_ It was the voice that told a man where the edge was, and invited him to step back from it.

He stepped back from it. He was aware of himself doing so. He felt it the way he had felt, once, on a colony on Malory's Reach, the moment when the floor of a dome had started to give under his boot and he had shifted his weight without thinking.

"No," he said. "No reason."

"Good," said Haste, and smiled, and poured him another brandy he did not want.

At the end of the table Kell slid the bolt back into the slug-gun with a small oiled click, and looked up for the first time, straight at Corvin, with his bored, patient, uninterested eyes; and then looked down again and went on cleaning.

* * *

The signal came on the third day out from the Throat, at nine minutes past the hour.

The young comm officer heard it first. His name was Dacey, and he was nineteen, and he had been listening to the silence of the Sabra system for four days with the dogged attention of somebody who has been told by his captain that silence is never really silent if you listen hard enough. He had been working through the Company's old frequency tables band by band, the dull ones, the housekeeping channels, the ones nobody would ever have used to call for help.
