On the eleventh of them, a narrow band labelled in the 2387 tables simply _HOURLY — INTERNAL_, he heard a click.

He sat up. He waited. He was a patient boy. At nine minutes past the next hour, it came again: not a click this time, but a burst. A short, dense squeal of data, two seconds long, and then nothing.

He called the captain. The captain called Tol, and Tol called Fen, and by the time the next hour came round there were six people crowded into the comm space behind the bridge, Corvin among them, in a silence so complete that he could hear Ode's breathing.

Nine minutes past. The burst came.

"Again," said Fen. "Run it back. Slower."

Dacey ran it back. On his screen the two-second squeal unfolded into a shape: a long ragged comb of pulses, dense at the front, thinning toward the end. Fen leaned over his shoulder, her face very close to the screen, her lips moving.

"It's Company format," she said. "Their old internal packet. I've seen it in the archives. There's a header—" she put her finger on the dense front of the comb— "and then a long body, and then a checksum. The header's encrypted. The body's encrypted. But the structure—" She stopped.

"What?" said Ode.

"The structure's a posting." Fen straightened up. She looked, Corvin thought, as if she had seen something in the room behind them. "That's what the Company called it. An hourly posting. It's the Tally."

Nobody spoke.

"The ledger," said Fen. "The thing that ran the habitat. The Company's documents are full of it. Every hour, on the hour, it posted every account — every debit and credit, air and water and heat, every person on the rock. And it broadcast a summary on this band, to the Company's receivers. Here. The throat-watch. The haulers. So the Company would always know what it was owed." She looked at the screen. "It's still doing it. Seven hundred years. On the hour. Plus nine minutes, because its clock has drifted. It's still posting the accounts."

"So they're alive," said Dacey. His voice cracked on it, and he flushed.

"No," said Fen. "Not necessarily. That's the point. The Tally doesn't need anyone. The Director's statement said so: it's fully autonomous, it requires no Company supervision. If everyone on Lantern died six hundred years ago it would still post their accounts every hour, for as long as it had power. It'd post the dead. It'd post them as closed, or as estate, or—" She stopped again. "I don't know what it would post them as. I can't read it."

"The core," said Corvin. "From the throat-watch. It'll have the Company's keys."

"It might. If I can get into it. If the keys are the same ones. If the Tally hasn't changed its own in seven hundred years." She pushed her hair back with both hands. "If I could read the body of that packet, I could tell you how many accounts it's posting, and whether the balances are moving. If they're moving, someone's breathing. If they're not—"

"How long?" said Haste.

He was in the doorway. Nobody had heard him come. Fen turned round and looked at him, and Corvin saw her face close like a door.

"I don't know," she said. "Days. A week. It's seven-hundred-year-old cryptography on a core that's been in vacuum since before my family had a name."

"Take as long as you need," said Haste pleasantly. "It doesn't change anything, of course. Either way, we're going to the rock." He looked at the screen, at the ragged comb of the burst, as though it were an interesting curio in a dealer's window. "But I'd like to know which speech Mr. Saal will be giving."

He went away again. After a while, so did everybody else, back to their posts, back to their bunks, until there was only Dacey at his console and Fen at his shoulder and Corvin in the doorway.

Ten minutes before the hour, Fen said, without turning round: "Do you want to hear it again?"

"Yes," said Corvin.

So they waited. Nine minutes past the hour, from somewhere on the far side of the great banded planet ahead of them, behind which a rock eleven kilometres long was turning in the dark, the burst came: two seconds of dense, patient, meaningless noise, exactly as it had come every hour for seven hundred and two years, to receivers that had been dead for most of them.

Something was still counting.

Corvin stood in the doorway of the comm space and listened to it, and thought — he could not help thinking it, and despised himself a little for it — that it was the sound of a debt being kept. And that he had been sent all this way, at the end of thirty-five years of his own, to collect it.

# Chapter 10: The Shear

*The Closing · 2387 CE*

He asked to meet her in the chapel on Gallery Four, at the end of third shift, when the galleries were as empty as they ever were; and when she came in, he was sitting on the front bench under the tea-coloured window with a paper folder on his knees, and he did not get up.

"Sit down, please," said Teodor Basu. "I'd rather— I'd rather not have to look at you while I say it. If that's all right."

She sat at the other end of the bench. Between them on the crate-wood was the folder. It was thick. Its edges were worn soft, as though it had been carried a long way, or handled a great deal.

"In the spring," said Basu, "there was an accident at Caul Deep."

He said it to the window. To the ship in the resin, crossing the dark sea toward its lamp.

"The main shaft. Shaft One. Six hundred metres down through the crust into the old ice. The ice down there isn't like the ice on the surface. It's under pressure. It moves, very slowly, like— like glass in an old window. The shaft engineers had been reporting creep in the lower bracing for two years. Every quarter. Every quarter I took their figures and set them against the budget for re-bracing, and every quarter the re-bracing would have cost the Deep its season's yield, and every quarter the Director decided it could wait, and I agreed with her." He drew a breath. "I agreed with her. I want to be clear about that. It isn't her decision I've come to confess. It's mine. I had the figures. I could have refused to sign the budget. I didn't."

Dagny did not move.

"On the fourth of April the lower bracing failed at four hundred and eighty metres," Basu said. "The pressure ice came into the shaft. All of it, all at once. It's— there's a word the engineers used. A blowout. It fills a space the way water fills a bucket, except that it's ice, and it freezes as it comes. Shaft One was at full shift. Nine hundred and four people were below the three-hundred-metre station." His voice did not change. "None of them came up."

The chapel was very quiet. Somewhere far below them a pump thudded, and stopped.

"Varga's men went down the next day. There were— there were some at the lift station at three hundred metres. Who had got that far. Behind them the shaft was solid. Varga reported that recovery was impossible: it would take a year to cut the ice, and the shaft might go again while they cut. The Director asked me what a year's recovery would cost. I told her. She asked me what it would cost if it were known. In the Concord. The Labour Inspectorate had already fined the Company twice for Caul. A third finding, with nine hundred dead, would end the Concern. Possibly the Company." He swallowed. "So the Director ordered the shaft sealed at the three-hundred-metre station. And she asked me to make the nine hundred go away."

"Go away," said Dagny.

"From the Tally. From the books." He put his hand on the folder, flat, as if holding it shut. "Every person on Lantern is an account. When an account closes, the Tally posts why. Death, or completion of contract. If nine hundred accounts had closed by death in one hour, it would be in every hourly posting that went out to the Concord on the data-runs. So I closed them another way. I posted them as contracts bought out by the Company. Terms completed. Repatriated, through the Throat, on the spring transport." He was speaking very precisely now, every word placed. "I wrote letters, for the ones who had family here. From the Concord. _Arrived safe. Doing well. Will send for you._ I wrote four hundred and thirty-one letters, Ms. Ostrova. I sent them out on one data-run and had them come back in on the next. I copied their handwriting from their payroll signatures."

Dagny stood up.

She did not decide to. She was on her feet and then she was in front of him and she had hit him, open-handed, so hard across the face that his head turned and his glasses went skittering off along the bench and onto the floor. He did not put up his hands. He sat there with his face turned away and the mark of her hand coming up red on his cheek, and waited, and when she did not hit him again, he turned his head back and looked at her for the first time, with his naked short-sighted eyes.

"Yes," he said. "Thank you."

"Don't you _thank_ me—"

"I'm sorry. I mean it. Nobody's— in eight months, nobody has done that." He bent, slowly, and felt along the floor for his glasses, and found them, and put them on. One lens was cracked. "There's more. I'm afraid there's more."

She found she was shaking. She sat down again, not on the bench, on the step in front of it, with her back to him, looking at the dark empty chapel.

"The Inspectorate noticed," Basu said behind her. "Not the deaths. The arrivals. Nine hundred people repatriated through the Throat on the spring transport, and not one of them ever landed anywhere in the Concord. Not one ever registered for work, or drew a pension, or wrote home. It took them a few months to notice. Then it took them a few months more to be sure. The notice came in on the data-run three weeks before the Director's announcement. A Labour Inspectorate fleet, four ships, with a commission. Due through the Throat in a hundred days." He paused. "Three weeks later, the throat-watch recorded the onset of a closing phase."

Dagny turned round.

"That's—"

"Yes," said Teodor Basu. "I thought so too."

He held out the folder. She did not take it. He set it on the step beside her.

"Those are their names," he said. "All nine hundred and four. From payroll. I've— I've been carrying them. I don't know what else to do with them."

* * *

Kas took her to Caul the next day in the _Magpie_, off the books, on a run he logged as a tank inspection, and for the six hours of the flight he did not make a single joke.

Caul came up out of the dark as a white ball streaked with grey, very bright against the bruise-coloured bulk of Nhai behind it. Close to, it was not white at all. It was a hundred shades of blue and grey and dirty yellow, cracked and ridged and pitted, with long straight scars across it where the haulers' landing-tracks ran and, at the bottom of a vast frozen basin on its leading face, a cluster of gantries and domes and towers that had been, until the spring, the biggest working mine in the Sabra Concern.

It was dark. Kas had said so, the day Lemuel died. _Used to be you'd see the lights of it from a thousand klicks out like a city._ It looked now like what it was: a town where nobody lived. The landing beacon was dead. The dome lights were dead. Frost had grown on the gantries, long feathers of it, the way frost grew on anything metal that was left alone on Caul. Kas brought the _Magpie_ down on the old pad by hand, slowly, with the floodlights on, and their light went out over a field of abandoned equipment — ice-cutters, haul-sleds, a crawler on its side — and threw long black shadows away across the ice.

"Shaft One's under the big tower," Kas said. "The lift head. I used to see the men going down in the morning. Shift-change, you'd get a queue of a hundred, two hundred, on the gantry. Waving at the haulers." He was looking straight ahead through the cracked window. "I waved back. Every week."

They suited up. They went across the pad to the tower, and in at the lift-head door, which was not locked — who would come? — and down.

The lift was dead, so they used the emergency ladder, a cage of steel rungs bolted down the side of the shaft beside the cable runs. It was three hundred metres to the station. Dagny had climbed the Wick every day of her life and she had never climbed anything like it: down and down in the narrow dark with her lamp on the rungs and the walls of the shaft going past, smooth grey ice-crete at first and then, lower, the old ice itself, blue-black and glassy, so clear in places that her light went into it a metre and showed her things frozen there, stones and bubbles and once a dead tool, hanging in the ice as if in air. Kas came down above her. She could hear his breathing on the suit channel, harsh and quick.

At three hundred metres the ladder ended on a steel platform, and the platform ended in a wall.

It was ice-crete. Poured, new, grey, filling the whole width of the shaft from side to side like a cork in a bottle, with a heavy steel inspection hatch set in the middle of it and the hatch welded shut. Somebody had stencilled on it, in Company blue, SHAFT CLOSED — NO ENTRY — BY ORDER. Under the stencil, very small, the leaning letters.

Beside the hatch there was a port. A thick round window of armoured glass, the kind they put in pressure walls, at head height, with its own little lamp behind it, dead.

Dagny put her light to the glass.

At first she saw only blue. A deep cold glassy blue, like looking into a lake. Then her eyes understood what they were looking at, and she did not move, and did not breathe, and heard on the channel behind her Kas say a word that was not a word.

The lift station was full of ice. It had come up the shaft from below and filled the station to the roof and frozen there. And it was full of people.

They were standing. Most of them were standing. Caught in the act of running toward the lift — toward her — in their mine-suits and helmets, frozen in mid-stride, arms out, heads turned, some of them holding on to each other. The ice was so clear she could see their faces. Some had their eyes open. A woman near the front had her hand up, flat, the palm toward the port, as if she had been reaching for it. Behind her, deeper in the blue, there were more. And more behind those. As far as the light would go, there were people in the ice, crowded, standing, reaching, going back and back into the dark of the shaft until the blue swallowed them.

And on the inner face of the hatch — on the inside, where the ice had not quite reached, in a pocket of clear dark space between the frozen people and the steel — there were marks. Long pale gouges in the paint. Five of them. Then five more. Like fingers dragged down a door.

Dagny looked at them for a long time.

"They poured the seal," she said. Her voice on the channel sounded very far away. "With some of them still on this side of the ice. Still alive. They poured it, and welded the hatch, and went up."

Kas said nothing. She heard him breathing.

"Count them," she said.

"Dag—"

"Count them. The ones you can see. I'll count too. Then we'll put them together."

They counted. It took a long time. The faces in the ice went back further than either of them could see, and they lost count, and began again, and lost it again. In the end Dagny said "That's enough," and Kas said "I got a hundred and twelve," and she said "A hundred and nine," and they stood together on the steel platform in the dark with their lamps on the port and did not say anything else for a while.

Then Dagny took the folder out of her suit's thigh pocket, where she had carried it all the way down — Teodor's paper folder, with nine hundred and four names in it — and held it up flat against the armoured glass, so that the woman with her palm raised could see it, if she could see anything. She did not know why. It was a stupid thing to do. She held it there anyway, until her arm ached.

"I'll bring them up," she said to the glass. "Your names. I promise you. I'll bring your names up."

On the way back, in the _Magpie_'s cabin, with the white ball of Caul shrinking behind them against the planet, Kas said the only thing he said on the whole flight home.

"Every week," he said. "I flew past them every week, and waved."

* * *

Basu was waiting for her at the foot of the Wick when the _Magpie_ docked, and he did not need to ask. He looked at her face and nodded, and then he said, "Come up. Please. There's something I can show you now. I couldn't before."

He took her up into the Counting House by the service stair, past the offices, to a narrow room at the back of the comptroller's floor where there was nothing but a wall of old screens and a chair. There were no windows. On every screen there were figures.

"The throat-watch station," he said. "Its raw feed. Not the summaries that come to the Director's office. The raw readings, every six hours, from the instruments. They come to my floor first, for the accounts — the Company charges the Concern for the station's running, so I log its output. Nobody's ever looked at them but me and the man who sends them. A man called Roach. He's been out there eleven years." He sat down in the chair and began touching screens, bringing up columns. "I'm not a physicist. But I can read a trend. I've read nothing but trends for thirty years. I went back through every reading for the last six months, last night, after I left you."

He turned the chair round to face her.

"There is no closing phase," said Teodor Basu. "There never was. The Throat's breathing is exactly what it's been for ninety-one years. Regular as a clock. The numbers in the Director's announcement — the ones she said came from the throat-watch — they aren't in the throat-watch's feed. They aren't anywhere. Somebody in the Director's office made them up." He took his glasses off and rubbed his eyes, carefully, round the cracked lens. "Roach has been asking for confirmation. Three times. Nobody's answered him."

Dagny sat down on the floor with her back against the door, because there was nowhere else to sit and her legs had stopped holding her.

"Then it's not closing," she said. "It's not going to close. We could just— wait. Let them go. And in fifty days the inspectors come through, and—"

"It's going to close," said Basu.

He turned back to the screens and touched one, and the columns of throat-watch figures gave way to a single page of text. A manifest entry. She knew the format; she had seen him look at it before, on the bench by the bell.

_Device, Concord ordnance, class T. Shielded bay 4, aft of drive. Received under Security seal._

"I found out what class T means," he said. "It took me a while. It's not in any Company catalogue. It's in the Concord's own military register, the part that's public, under _throat ordnance_. They were made during the Succession Wars, two hundred years ago, when the Concord was fighting itself through every throat it had. A fleet that was losing would go back through a throat and then—" he made a small movement with his hand, a closing— "behind them. So the enemy couldn't follow."

"Close it," said Dagny.

"Collapse it. The register calls it a shear. A throat-shear. It's fired into the mouth of a throat by a ship in transit, and it— the physics is beyond me. It cuts the throat's lips. They fold in. The throat collapses. And it stays collapsed, Ms. Ostrova, for six or seven or eight hundred years, exactly as the Director said in her announcement, because that much of what she said is true: a collapsed throat heals on its own time and no one else's." He looked at her. "The Company bought a shear from a Concord arms broker three months ago. Before the throat-watch said anything at all. Before the Director announced anything at all. The _Covenant_ will carry it into the Throat on the day she leaves, with four hundred and six essential staff aboard, and fire it behind her as she comes out the other side. And the Throat will close on the day the Director said it would, and the inspectors will be on the other side of it, and the nine hundred and four will be under the ice of Caul for ever, and nobody in the Concord will ever know. Except as a line in a Company report. _Repatriated._"

"And us."

"And you. Nine thousand people who know what Caul is, sealed in a box with a meter on the air, with your contracts held in trust." He spread his hands. "The Company doesn't need to silence you. The Throat will do it."

For a long time neither of them said anything. Dagny sat against the door with her knees up and her hands over her face and saw, on the inside of her eyelids, the blue of the ice and the woman with her palm on the glass.

Then she took her hands away.

"Then we stop the shear," she said. "We stop it, and we let the inspectors come."

Teodor Basu looked at her for a long moment through his cracked glasses.

"Yes," he said. "I hoped you would say that."

* * *

On the security floor of the Counting House, two levels below them, in an office with a desk and a cot and a wall of camera screens, Hale Varga sat alone in the dark and watched a recording.

It was from the Dock Ring traffic log. An unscheduled departure, nineteen hours ago: hauler _Magpie_, pilot Ostrova K., logged as tank inspection, flight plan filed for the Caul run. And a return, an hour ago, from the same heading. Six hours out, six hours back. Long enough for a landing. Long enough for a climb.

He sat with it for a while, his big scarred hands flat on the desk. On another screen, from a camera on the comptroller's floor that the Comptroller did not know was there, Dagny Ostrova sat against a door with her face in her hands.

Then Varga reached out, and deleted the _Magpie_'s flight from the traffic log, and deleted the camera's last hour, and sat on in the dark with nothing on his screens at all.

# Chapter 11: Hull 7731

*The Owing · 311 AC*

The answer had been under his feet all his life.

Orrin found it on the sixth night, on his knees, in the fifth ledger hall, which was the lowest and oldest of the five, and the only one whose floor was not rock. He had not gone there looking for it. He had gone there because he had run out of places to look, and because when a counter has run out of places to look he goes back to the beginning, and the beginning of the Count, everybody knew, was the Hulk.

The fifth hall was the Account Day hall. It was where the tours came. It was kept a little cleaner than the others and lit a little brighter, and at its centre, where the desks stopped, there was a square of floor fenced off by a low brass rail, with a plaque on the rail that every child on Lantern had read aloud on their first visit, slowly, sounding out the long words. _HERE THE FOUNDERS RAISED THE COUNT UPON THE BONES OF THE SHIP THAT BROUGHT US. OWING AND OWED._ Inside the rail the floor was the Hulk's own plating, unpainted for the tours: a great curved sheet of riveted metal, dark blue-grey, worn smooth and shiny in the middle where three hundred years of small hands had touched it.

Outside the rail the same plating ran on under the desks, under the walls, under the whole of the hall, painted over in Count grey. And under the grey, in places — where chairs had been dragged, where feet had scuffed — the old blue showed through.

He had noticed it, absently, since he was a boy. He noticed it properly on the sixth night because he had been staring at the floor for an hour, sitting at a desk in the empty hall with his head in his hands, trying to think where else in the Count a hull might be numbered; and his eye had fallen on a place by the wall where a filing chest had stood for a century and been moved, and the paint under it had peeled in a long curling strip, and under the peeled grey there was blue, and on the blue there was white.

A stencil. Very old. The edge of a letter, or a number.

He got down on his knees. He had nothing to scrape with but the stylus Jory had given back to him. He used that. The Count grey came away in flakes and dust and then, as he worked down the strip, in long satisfying curls, and under it, in a stencil a hand's-breadth high, in the square white Company type that he had seen only on the oldest ledgers in the archive, letters came up out of the floor.

HA 7731.

And beside it, smaller: REACTOR ACCESS — FRAME 40 — AUTHORISED PERSONNEL.

He knelt there for a long time with paint dust on his knees and his hands. Then he got up, and went to the brass rail, and looked at the bare shining metal inside it that he had touched at seven and felt nothing.

_The bones of the ship that brought us._

The Hulk was Hull 7731. And the Hulk had a reactor. And in the light ledger, carried forward every quarter for three hundred years on the Founders' own authority, the Hulk's reactor held fourteen units in reserve.

He went back to the third hall and opened the light ledger again and did the arithmetic, because that was what he was for. A cassette, at the Wick's present length — a third of what it had been at the Founding — lasted a little under three years. At full length, a little under one. Fourteen units would light the Wick as it burned now for forty years. Would light it from end to end, the whole length of the world, the Cold Wards and all, for fourteen.

He sat back. He thought about the Comptroller-General standing on the gallery above the Hook, saying in his soft voice that the last cassette would be spent before the end of the year. That the Count had made such provision as it could. That it was arithmetic.

It was arithmetic. Orrin had simply not been given all of the numbers.

There was one more thing to look for, and he found it in the Wickwrights' own orders, which the Count filed and nobody read: a work order, issued two years ago, signed by the Comptroller-General, for an _auxiliary lamp, Upper Wards, to be commissioned on the failure of the Wick_. Specifications attached. It was a small lamp. A lamp for three terraces and the Counting House. A lamp for perhaps four thousand people.

Its fuel source, in the specifications, in Vosk's own neat hand, was given as _Reserve_.

* * *

He went down to Twenty-Two in the morning without changing his clothes, with grey paint dust still in the creases of his trousers, and when Tamsin Hask opened the door to him he said, before she could speak: "There's fuel."

She looked at him. Then she stood aside and let him in and shut the door.

Agathe was asleep. Jory was not there. Tamsin pulled out the room's one chair for him and sat on her mat on the floor, cross-legged, in her undersuit with her hair tied back, and listened while he told her. He told it badly, in the wrong order, the ledger and then the floor and then the ledger again and then the Wickwrights' order, and she did not interrupt him once. When he had finished she sat for a while looking at her hands.

Then she reached inside her undersuit and took out a folded square of yellowed plastic and held it out to him.

He unfolded it. A Company docket, its paper brown with age. He read it, and read it again. _Cassettes 1–212 to Wick store, Spine Road. For the lamp. Store to be sealed on completion._ The stamp. The neat small signature.

"Where did you get this?"

"Aft Store 3. In the Cold. Behind a welded door." She told him about the door, and the empty racks, and — after a moment, not looking at him — about the corner behind the last rack, and the wall. He did not know what to say to that. Nobody would have. She did not seem to need him to say anything.

"Two hundred and twelve," she said. "That's where the Wick's fuel came from. Those two hundred and twelve, and whatever was in the other stores. The Company put them in the Wick store for the lamp before it ran. Didn't it?"

"I think so. Yes."

"And your Count's been burning them down for three hundred years, and shortening the lamp, and shortening it, and telling us it was all there was." She took the docket back and folded it, carefully, along its old creases. "And it's got fourteen more under its floor."

"In reserve," said Orrin. "Yes."

"For the Upper Wards."

"For an auxiliary lamp. In the Upper Wards. When the Wick goes out." He made himself say the rest of it. "For about four thousand people. The Count. The counting families. The farms they've moved up there. It's— I think it's always been the plan. Since long before Vosk. The reserve was set aside at the Founding. Somebody's always known it was there. Every Comptroller-General. They just never needed it until now."

"And the Calling?"

He had thought about that all night, on his knees, and then at his desk, and then walking down the galleries in the grey morning.

"Thirty thousand people can't live under a lamp built for four," he said. "But ten thousand fewer is a start. And then, when the Wick goes out, the Galleries go dark, and the Cold comes up from the aft end, and—" He stopped. "And it doesn't matter, then, how many there are. Because they'll be in the dark, and the Count will be in the light, and the Account will go on being kept, by the ones who are left."

Tamsin Hask said nothing for a long time. On the bunk Agathe stirred, and coughed in her sleep, and settled.

"It's not arithmetic," Tamsin said at last. "It's a lifeboat."

"Yes."

"With fourteen seats in it, and a door, and a man with a stick at the door."

"Yes."

She got up off the floor. She went to the shelf over the stove and took down the biscuit tin with the farmhouse on the lid and opened it, and stood looking into it — at the chits, all the family's chits, three hundred years of them — and then she closed it again and put it back.

"I'm going to take them," she said.

He stared at her.

"The fourteen. Out of the Hulk, up the spine, into the Wick. All of them. And light it. All the way to the aft end." She turned round, and her face was quite calm. "You're going to tell me how to get into the Hulk."

"I can't—" he began, and stopped. He had been going to say _I can't, it's the Count's_, and found that the words did not mean anything any longer. He said instead: "I don't know how. I found a stencil on a floor. That's all. _Reactor access, frame forty._ I don't know where frame forty is. I don't know if there's a way in from the halls. The Sanctum's down there. The Tally's root. Nobody goes into the Hulk but the Comptroller-General."

"Then find out."

"And if I do? You'll walk in with a cutter's lance and fourteen cassettes the size of coffins will just— come out with you? Up through the Count, up the spine, past the bailiffs, past Lusk—"

"No," said Tamsin. "Not on my own." And she looked past him, at the door, and he turned and saw that it had opened while they were talking, without the sigh and clunk it usually made, and Jory was standing in it, with his finger on his lips, grinning; and behind him, filling the doorway from side to side, there was a slab of a man in a cutter's rig whom Orrin had never seen before, and who looked at him with no expression at all.

"This is Dobbs," said Tamsin. "He works for the Salt. Dobbs, this is my counter."

* * *

The Count posted the lists thirteen days early.

It came without warning on the morning of the seventeenth day after the Hook. The gutter had come four times in one night — four times the Wick had stammered and nearly died, and four times the whole of Lantern had woken in the dark and lain listening to the silence — and when the light steadied the last time and the third shift began, the boards at the Hook had been papered over in the night with long grey sheets, and the criers were out on every gallery with their slates, and the first thousand names were being read.

The Count called it mercy. The lamp was failing faster than the Wickwrights had said; every day's delay cost air. Better the first of the Called go now, in good order, while there was still light enough to see them into the Cold, than in a panic later in the dark. The rest would follow in their thousands, every few days. The criers read it out in the Comptroller-General's words, over and over, all morning, and then they began on the names.

Agathe Hask was not in the first thousand. She was in the third.

Tamsin heard it from a crier on Twenty-Two and stood very still on the terrace road while he read the rest of the third list, family by family, and the people round her stood still too, and then one of them began to weep, and then a door down the terrace opened and a man ran out of it shouting, and the crier stepped back against the wall with his slate held up in front of him like a shield.

The first thousand went at second shift.

The bailiffs brought them down from all over the Galleries in columns, to the ring road at the aft end of the Dock Ring, where it ran past the Salt Docks and on into the dark. There they were counted. There was a table set up in the middle of the road with a lamp on it and a counter behind it, and each family as it came was stopped, and its names read, and its chits touched to a counter's slate, and the slate chimed, and the chits went grey. Released from the Tally's charge. Owing nothing more. And then they were waved on, along the ring road, to the Lip.

Tamsin watched from the steps of a dead noodle stall at the corner of Spur Two. Orrin was beside her. He had come down without his coat. He had not said a word since the columns began.

They came past for an hour. Old people, mostly — the deepest accounts were the longest lives — and the sick, and whole families together where the head of the family was Called and the children under sixteen went with them by law, carrying what they could carry. Bedding. Pans. A man with a goat on a string. A woman with a birdcage, with no bird in it. Nobody fought. That was what Tamsin could not get over afterwards. A thousand people, and the bailiffs were perhaps forty, with their grey coats and their docket-batons, and nobody fought. They walked. They had been walking toward this all their lives, one hour's air at a time, and when it came they knew the way.

Brother Lusk stood at the Lip.

He was where the light ended, at the iron post where cutters clipped their tethers, a big pale man in grey with his baton through his belt and his hands folded in front of him, and as each family reached him he nodded to them, gravely, the way a priest nods at a door, and they walked past him and on into the blue dusk of the Cold, and their lamps went down the dark road and grew small.

"He's counting them," said Orrin. It was the first thing he had said. "Look. His lips. He's counting them in."

The counter at the table was a heavy-set grey-haired man with ink on his cuffs. Tamsin did not know him. Orrin did.

"Abney," Orrin said. "Senior Counter Abney. Third hall. He taught me long division." His voice did not change. "He refused my correction. _Surcharges are not corrected during a Calling._ He's very careful. He's the most careful man I know."

The column was ending. Along the Galleries, faint, a sound had begun — a sound Tamsin had heard once before in her life, at her grandmother's funeral, when the whole of Twenty-Two had done it. Somebody on a terrace high above had taken an iron bar and struck a pipe. A water main, a rail, anything that would ring. A flat hard clang, like the Hook. Then another, on another terrace. Then another. It went round the curve of the world, gallery to gallery, the way the songs went round, until all of Lantern was ringing, thousands of iron bars on thousands of pipes, not in time, not together, a great ragged clanging din that went on and on, under the stammering Wick, while the last of the first thousand walked past Brother Lusk into the dark.

Lusk turned his head, slowly, and looked up at the terraces, at the ringing.

He did not seem angry. He seemed — Tamsin thought, afterwards — interested. As if it were a sum that had come out in an unexpected way, and he would want to check it later.

Beside her, Orrin Sallow was crying. He was doing it without any noise, standing quite straight, with the tears running down his face and his hands at his sides.

"I believed it," he said. "All of it. _The Count is just._ I said it every morning. I said it to her." He did not wipe his face. "I want to see the floor of the Hulk, Tamsin. Frame forty. I want to see the fourteen. I want to see them with my own eyes."

"You will," said Tamsin.

She found that her hand was in his. She did not remember taking it. She did not let go.

# Chapter 12: The Hold

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

Fen broke the throat-watch core on the sixth day out from the Throat, at four in the morning, and came and knocked on Corvin's cabin door with her hair standing up and her eyes red and a slate held against her chest, and said, "I need you to look at something so I know I'm not mad."

He followed her up to the observation blister. The bell was there, strapped upright to the couch legs, and round it on the deck she had spread every slate and printout she owned in a rough ring, like a ritual circle. The core from the throat-watch station sat in the middle of them, cabled to her own machine.

"It was Roach," she said. "He kept his own keys. In a text file. Unencrypted. Called _keys — do not lose_." She laughed, a little wildly. "Seven hundred years of Company cryptography and a lonely man in a can wrote his passwords down because he was afraid of forgetting them. I could kiss him."

"What did you find?"

She sat down cross-legged among the slates and pulled one into her lap.

"Three things," she said. "The first one's the throat. The raw readings. Every six hours, for eleven years, all the way to the end." She turned the slate toward him. On it was a long line, rising and falling in a slow, perfectly regular wave, like breathing. "That's the Throat. That's its mouth opening and closing, the way the lips did when we went through. Except it's steady. Look at it. There's no change. No closing phase. Not in the last month, not in the last year, not ever. It's breathing exactly the way it always breathed, right up to—" she ran her finger along the wave to its end, where it simply stopped, cut off in the middle of a rise— "there."

"Where the station died."

"No. The station didn't die there. It went on recording for weeks after. Roach went on writing in his book. That's where the Throat died." She looked up at him. "There's no closing phase, Corvin. The Director's statement — the thing that came through on the last data-run, that every lien-house in the Concord's been trading on for seven hundred years, _the throat-watch has confirmed the onset of a closing phase_ — it's not in the throat-watch. The Throat was fine. And then, on one day, in one reading, it wasn't there any more."

He looked at the line on the slate for a long time. The slow regular breathing. The cut.

"Something closed it," he said.

"Something closed it. Or somebody." She put the slate down. "And there's a thing in the Concord military register, from the Succession Wars—"

"I know what's in the register." He did. Every adjuster did; throat law was half of lien law. "That's the first thing. What's the second?"

She pulled another slate into her lap. This one was a list.

"The Director's statement said nine hundred people from the Caul Deep mine were repatriated through the Throat before the Closing. Contracts bought out. That line." He nodded; he knew the line. "The statement came with staff rolls. The whole Concern. Including Caul Deep — every miner, name and number, on the day the mine was shut. Nine hundred and four of them. I've got the Concord immigration registry for that decade on file. It's what I do. Provenance. Where people went." She held up the slate. "None of them went anywhere. Not one of the nine hundred and four ever came through a Concord port. Ever registered. Ever worked. Ever died, in the Concord's books. They were repatriated, and then they just— weren't."

"Lost in transit," said Corvin. He said it the way an adjuster says it. He heard himself.

"There's no record of a transport being lost. There's no record of a transport. There's a line in a report, and nine hundred and four people who stop." She looked at him steadily. "And then there's the third thing."

She got up on her knees and reached for the bell.

She had marked the grey band on the waist — the dense cramped band of names in the hurried hands, cut over the bell's plain belt — with small strips of white tape, every so often, like the ruled lines on a page. Now she put her lamp to it and her loupe to her eye, and began to read aloud, very slowly, one name and then the next, and as she read she ran her other hand down the slate in her lap.

"Abara, Joseph. Achebe, Ruth. Adair, Michael. Agbaje, Femi." Her finger moved on the slate. "Abara, Joseph. Achebe, Ruth. Adair, Michael. Agbaje—"

She stopped.

"They're alphabetical," she said. "On the bell. In the band. Whoever cut them was working down a list. And it's this list." She held up the slate. "The Caul Deep roster. I've checked four hundred. I'll check the rest. But I don't need to, do I." She lowered the loupe. "They're all here. All nine hundred and four. Cut on Lantern's bell, by hand, in a hurry, at the very end. With all the rest of Lantern's dead."

The blister was very quiet. Above them, through the scratched glass, the banded face of Nhai filled half the sky, ochre and bruise-purple, enormous now, with the hard bright line of its ring.

"They didn't go home," said Corvin.

"No."

"They died. On Caul. And the Company wrote them out of the books and put a line in a report and closed the Throat behind it—"

"And somebody on Lantern knew," said Fen. "Knew enough to get every name. And cut every one of them into the bell, so they'd be remembered with everybody else. And then—" she put her hand flat on the bronze— "somebody took the bell away. Out through the Throat. Before the end." She shook her head. "I don't understand it, Corvin. I've had this thing in my house my whole life. I don't understand it at all."

He sat down on the couch, heavily, with his elbows on his knees.

"There's one more thing," Fen said, after a while. "Small. I nearly didn't notice. The throat-watch recorded the Tally's hourly postings, the same ones we've been hearing. Every hour, for eleven years, Roach's station logged the packet and filed it. Roach's keys open them. Most of them." She turned a slate toward him, and on it was a long column of dates, each with a little green mark, and then, near the bottom, the marks went red. "Until the forty-ninth day after the Director's statement. Then the Tally changed its keys. All of them. In one hour. And nothing after that opens."

"Changed them," said Corvin. "Who changed them?"

"I don't know." Fen looked at the bell. "Somebody who didn't want the Company reading their books any more."

* * *

He went into the hold that night, because he had run out of ways not to.

Tol took him. He had not asked Tol; Tol had simply been waiting in the corridor outside the engine spaces when Corvin came down it, as though they had arranged to meet, and had nodded once and turned and led him, without a word, through a hatch in the engine-room deck and along a service crawl so narrow Corvin had to go on his elbows, past cable runs and coolant pipes and the great humming flank of the drive, to a small square panel in a bulkhead.

"Hold 2," said Tol. "Inspection hatch. It's on the ship's plans, not on Brightwater's. They sealed the main doors. They didn't know about this." He took a key from his pocket — an ordinary mechanical key, old, on a loop of wire. "My ship. My hatches."

He opened it. Cold came out — a dry, burning, utter cold, that made Corvin's eyes water and his breath smoke. Tol handed him a lamp and a pair of thick gloves, and stood back.

"I've looked," said Tol. "Your turn."

Corvin went through.

Hold 2 was the biggest space on the _Good Title_, a long vault running half the length of the ship, and it was full. He had known it would be full; the power board had told him that. He had not known what full would look like. The pods stood in racks, floor to ceiling, row after row, aisle after aisle, going away into the dark beyond his lamp: long white capsules like seed-cases, each the length of a person, each with a small round window of frosted glass at one end and a little green light at the other, steady, steady, steady. Hundreds of them. He walked down the first aisle with the lamp held up and kept walking and the pods kept going. Thousands.

He stopped at one at random and rubbed the frost off its window with his glove.

A woman's face. Young — thirty, perhaps — dark-skinned, with her hair shaved close for the cold, her eyes shut, her lips a little blue. Asleep. Not dead; the light at the foot of the pod was green. Very still, very far away, as people in cold sleep always looked, as if they had gone down a long road inside themselves and not yet turned to come back.

There was a tag on the pod. A plastic sleeve, at eye level, with a printed card in it. He put the lamp to it.

_BRIGHTWATER RECOVERY — LABOUR CONTRACT 41-0973._
_TEY, Marisol. Born Kessane, 3058. Source: Kessane Water Authority, debt assignment._
_Contract assigned: SABRA CONCERN (Brightwater) — persons and issue._
_Dependants: one (1), female, b. 3086, retained Kessane, attaching on majority._

He read it three times. He read the words _Kessane Water Authority_ three times. He stood in the cold with the lamp shaking a little in his hand and read the words _persons and issue_ until they stopped being words.

Then he went to the next pod, and the next. The cards were all the same. Different names, different debts, different worlds — Kessane, Malory's Reach, the Tannet shipyards, the Pellan colonies, places he had been, places he had stood in front of people and explained. Water debts. Medical debts. Family debts sold on at auction. All of them bought, all of them assigned, all of them to the same instrument, the same seven-hundred-year-old promise that he had in a case in his cabin. _Sabra Concern. Persons and issue._

He did not hear them come in. He heard Tol say, from the inspection hatch, very quietly, "Mr. Saal," and turned, and the main doors at the far end of the hold were open, and there was light in the aisle, and Quennell Haste was walking down it toward him in his dark coat with Sergeant Kell a pace behind.

"Mr. Saal," said Haste. "You've found the agricultural stock."

Corvin said nothing. He found that he had put his hand flat on the frosted window of Marisol Tey's pod, as if to keep her from hearing.

"Three thousand and twelve," said Haste. He stopped a few metres away and looked about him at the racks, with that same mild collector's interest. "Water debts, mostly. A few medical. All bought at very good prices; there's a glut of labour contracts at the moment, nobody wants them. The Concord has more debtors than it knows what to do with." He looked back at Corvin. "Please don't look at me like that. I've done nothing illegal. Every one of these people signed, or was signed for, under the law. Every one of these contracts was sold to us freely. We have simply assigned them to our principal instrument. That's all."

"To populate it," said Corvin. His voice sounded strange to him.

"If necessary. If the rock is empty, an instrument with no persons attached to it is worth only the rock. An instrument with three thousand persons attached — and their issue — is worth rather more. A colony. A workforce. A balance that grows every hour they breathe on it." He spread his hands. "And if the rock isn't empty, then we will need labour for the consolidation, and these people will provide it, and their accounts and the natives' will be merged into one book. Either way the instrument has persons. Either way it's enforced. You see?"

"Their children," said Corvin. "The cards say _attaching on majority_. Their children back on Kessane. When they grow up—"

"They attach. Yes. Persons and issue." Haste tilted his head. "You know this, Mr. Saal. You know it better than anyone aboard. It's what happened to you. Your father signed, and you attached, at nine. It's why you're here." He smiled, quite kindly. "This is what a lien is. It always has been. I'm a little surprised you need to be shown."

Behind him, Kell had not moved. He stood with his hands loose at his sides and his weight easy, and looked at Corvin with his bored, patient eyes, and Corvin understood, as clearly as he had ever understood anything, that if he said the wrong thing in the next minute, Kell would not need to be told what to do.

"I'll want you on the bridge at eight," said Haste. "We'll be clearing Nhai's limb in six days, and I'd like to go over the populated-habitat speech. Sergeant, please make sure the engineer's hatch is sealed after Mr. Saal has gone." He turned, and then turned back. "And Mr. Saal. Your discharge is in my desk. It hasn't gone anywhere. Nor have you. Good night."

* * *

Ode was in her cabin, which Corvin had never seen: a cell like his own, with a bunk and a desk and on the wall a single picture, an old print of a ship he didn't recognise under full sail on a blue sea. She listened to him with her arms folded and her face like a shut hatch. When he had finished she said nothing for a long time.

"I didn't know," she said at last.

"I didn't think you did."

"I'm telling you anyway. I want it said. I've run throats for thirty-four years and I've carried every rotten thing there is to carry, guns and bodies and cargoes you wouldn't want to know about, and I knew what every one of them was. I didn't know this." She unfolded her arms and put her hands flat on her desk and looked at them. "Three thousand."

"Three thousand and twelve."

"And their kids."

"And their kids."

"And mine's mortgaged to him." She laughed, without any amusement. "Every rivet. If I refuse the cargo he forecloses the moment we're back through the Throat, and I lose her, and my crew lose their berths, and the sleepers go on another ship. Or he forecloses now, here, and Kell puts somebody else in this chair. I know the law, Mr. Saal. I've had it explained to me by people like you."

"I know."

"So what are you going to do?"

He didn't answer. After a moment she nodded, as if that were an answer.

"Tol," she said, without raising her voice.

The engineer was in the doorway; Corvin had not heard him come. He was wiping his hands on a rag.

"We could drop the hold," Tol said. "There's a way. Emergency jettison, from the old cargo days. The whole bay. We'd say it was a fault."

"Drop it where?"

"Somewhere they could be picked up. If anyone was there to pick them up."

"Nobody's dropping anybody," said Ode. "Not three thousand people in cold sleep into a gas giant's orbit in a system nobody's lived in for seven hundred years. Not on my ship." She looked at Corvin. "Not yet."

* * *

Fen was still in the blister when he went back up, sitting on the deck in her ring of slates with her back against the couch and the bell beside her. She had stopped working. She was just looking at it.

He sat down beside her on the deck. He told her about the hold. She listened without moving, and when he was done she reached out, without a word, and took his hand, and held it — not tenderly; hard, the way you would hold on to something on a moving ship — and they sat together in silence for a long time, with the bell between them and the planet filling the window.

"Read me some," he said at last. "From the band."

She picked up the loupe and the lamp, without letting go of his hand, and leaned toward the bronze.

"Okafor, Grace," she read. "Okeke, Samuel. Oladipo, Bisi. Olsen, Erik. Olsen, Marta." She stopped. "Husband and wife, maybe. Or brother and sister."

"Go on."

"Ortiz, Benedicto. Osei, Kwame. Ostrowski, Anna." She moved the lamp. "Owusu, Adwoa. Page, Thomas."

She went on reading. He listened. Outside, on the far side of the great banded world, something went on counting; and in the hold below them three thousand and twelve people slept in the cold with their names on cards; and Fen read the dead of Caul aloud, one by one, in alphabetical order, to the man who had come to collect what they owed.

# Chapter 13: In Trust

*The Closing · 2387 CE*

The Wick crews kept a locker room at the foot of the spine, a long low cave of steel cabinets and benches where they changed in and out of their rigs, and since her door on Gallery Six had dimmed Dagny had slept there, on a bench, with Pim on the next bench and her coat for a blanket. It was warm. The lamp's waste heat came down through the rock. That was something.

Varga came at the start of third shift, when the room was empty. She woke because the door opened and a big shape filled it against the white light of the spine-road, and she was on her feet with a wrench in her hand before she knew she was awake.

"Put it down," said Hale Varga. "If I'd come to take you, I wouldn't have come alone."

He was not in armour. He was in Company grey fatigues with the sleeves rolled, and he was alone, and he stood in the doorway with his hands open at his sides where she could see them. Behind her, on the other bench, Pim had sat up.

"Outside," said Dagny.

They stood on the spine-road with the Wick going up over them into the white, and the long curve of the world falling away on every side. Varga looked up at it for a while before he spoke. He had the face of a man who had not slept either.

"I know you went to Caul," he said.

She said nothing.

"I took it off the log. The flight. And the camera on the comptroller's floor. Nobody else knows." He did not look at her. "I wanted you to know that I know. And that I took it off."

"Why?"

"Because I was there." He said it flatly. "At the three-hundred-metre station. I took the squad down. We got there twenty hours after the blowout. There were eleven of them at the lift station, on our side of the ice. Alive. Hurt, but alive. Behind them the shaft was solid. You've seen it."

"I've seen it."

"I called up to the Director. I told her I had eleven alive and could bring them up. She asked me if they'd seen the shaft. I said yes. She asked me if they'd talk." He was quiet for a moment. "She said the seal was to be poured at the station. She said it had to be poured at the station, because the shaft below was unstable, and a seal any higher wouldn't hold. She said the eleven were to be— she said they'd been exposed to the pressure ice and would not survive the climb anyway, and it would be kinder."

"And you poured it."

"I poured it." He turned his head and looked at her for the first time. His eyes were very pale in the white light. "My squad poured it. I gave the order. We put the hatch in and welded it, and they were on the other side of it, and we could hear them. Then we went up the ladder. It's three hundred metres. You can hear a long way, in a shaft."

Dagny did not say anything. She thought of the marks on the inside of the hatch. Five, then five more.

"I'm not telling you so you'll forgive me," said Varga. "I don't want that. I'm telling you because you're going to try something, and Basu's going to help you, and I want you to try it knowing the truth. Not the truth you'd like."

"Which is?"

"The inspectors won't save you."

She looked at him.

"I was a Concord marine for twelve years before the Company bought my contract," he said. "I did two tours on throat garrisons. I've seen what happens when the Labour Inspectorate comes through a throat. They'll come. They'll find Caul — you'll show them, and they'll find it. They'll fine the Company. They may put the Director in front of a court; they may not. And then they'll look at the Sabra Concern and see nine thousand indentures held in trust by a company that's about to go bankrupt, and they'll do what the Concord Court of Instruments told them to do in the Tannet Yards ruling seven years ago. They'll appoint a receiver. And the receiver will sell the instrument. Your contracts. Your debt. Every chit on every wrist on this rock, sold at auction in a lien-house on Kessane to whoever pays most for it." He shrugged his heavy shoulders. "And the new owners will send their own men. Better men than the Company's, probably. More efficient. Less tired. They'll have a better Tally. They'll take the line down from fifteen hundred back to nine. And they'll run Lantern for a hundred years, or two hundred, or six, until there's nothing left in it worth having."

The Wick hummed over their heads. Somewhere down the spine-road a crew was coming off shift, laughing at something.

"There is no rescue through that door, Ostrova," said Hale Varga. "There's only a different landlord."

She found that her hands had closed into fists, and made them open.

"Then why does it matter?" she said. "Why tell me? If it's the Company or it's the Concord, if it's the same chain either way—"

"Because it isn't the same," said Varga. "Not for them." He turned and looked aft along the spine, past the curve of the rock, as if he could see through it to the white ball of Caul. "If the Throat closes, Caul's under the ice for ever. Nobody ever hears the names. Nobody ever has to answer for it. Not her. Not Basu. Not me." He was quiet for a while. "I've been a soldier all my life. I've done things. I've never had to carry one where nobody would ever even know it had been done."

"So you want the Throat open."

"I don't know what I want." He said it simply, the way a man says he does not know the time. "I know what I did. That's all I know. I'll be on the _Covenant_ when she goes. I'm on the list. I don't know what I'll do when I'm on her." He looked back at her. "I just wanted you to know where the floor was. Before you jumped."

He went away up the spine-road without saying goodbye. She stood and watched him go, a big grey shape in the white light, getting smaller, until he turned in at the Counting House door and was gone.

* * *

The Tally dimmed Pim on the thirty-fourth day.

She found out at noon, when he came back to the locker room from school three hours early with his face very white and his chit held out in front of him like something he had found dead. The little screen on it was grey. When she touched it to the reader on the wall, the reader told her, in its polite blue letters, that account Ostrova, Pim, dependant of Ostrova, Dagny, had passed the dimming line at the eleven o'clock posting, following apportionment of strike-related losses to dependant accounts under section forty of the Concern's charter.

"They wouldn't let me in," he said. "The school door. It said I'd have to see a counter. Mister Okonkwo came out and said he was sorry and it wasn't up to him." He swallowed. "Everybody was looking."

"I know."

"I didn't strike. I wasn't even on the Wick. I was just—" He stopped.

"You were on the Wick," said Dagny. "At the end."

"Because you were there!" It came out of him in a shout, the first time he had raised his voice since the strike, and he looked shocked at it himself. "Because you were there and they— and Joss—" And then he was crying, at last, the way he had not cried on the Wick or at the bell or since, great ugly gulping sobs that bent him over, and she held him on the bench in the empty locker room with the waste heat of the lamp coming down through the rock round them, and let him.

When it had worn itself out she took him to a counter.

There was one at the chit-shop on Gallery Five, a tired man with a tired slate behind a grille, and Dagny put her own chit and Pim's on the counter in front of him and said she wanted to transfer six hundred from her account to her son's. The counter looked at the chits. He touched them to his slate. He looked at the slate for a long time.

"I can't," he said.

"I'm in credit. Just. I've got six hundred and eleven."

"You're flagged." He turned the slate a little so she could see it, and kept his voice low. "Every Speaker who voted for the strike. Transfers out are frozen for the rest of the closure. Director's order. I'm sorry."

"He's twelve."

"I know how old he is." The counter looked at Pim, and away. "I've got three of my own. I'm sorry."

So she took him back to the locker room and gave him her ration — the cold bean paste and the hard bread that the Tally still issued to her because she was still, by eleven credits, on the right side of the line — and watched him eat it, and did not eat anything herself. She was not hungry. She was something else, something hard and clear and cold, like the ice in the shaft at Caul.

"Mum," said Pim, when he had finished.

"What?"

"Is Uncle Kas going on the _Covenant_?"

She looked at him.

"People are saying." He was picking at the bread crumbs on the bench, not looking at her. "At school. Abi Adeyemi's aunt does nights loading the harvest up at the fore dock. She says Uncle Kas has been hauling for the Company, every day, fuel and stuff, up to the _Covenant_. She says he drinks with the Company men after. In their bar. In the Upper Wards." He looked up. "She says he's been asking about seats."

"People say a lot of things."

"I know." He looked down again. "I just wanted to know if it's true."

"I'll ask him," said Dagny.

* * *

She brought Odile the folder that night.

The old woman took it on the bench under the bell and opened it on her knees and turned the pages under the dim night-lamps of the ring, one after another, slowly, with her lips moving. Teodor had typed them in Company format, surname first, in alphabetical order, with a payroll number beside each and, for some, a Gallery address on Lantern where somebody had once been sent a letter. Odile did not say anything for a long time. She turned pages.

"Nine hundred and four," she said at last.

"Yes."

"I've cut a hundred names in a year. In a bad year. When the stills fever came, in seventy-one, I cut a hundred and forty, and my hands were no good for a month after." She closed the folder. "You've got eighteen days."

"I know."

"I'm seventy-four, Dagny."

"I know."

Odile looked up at the bell. Brown and furred and silent on its iron hook, with Joss's name bright on its shoulder, and Lemuel's, and Bronwen Price's, and above them, round the crown, the last of the room.

"There's no bell left," she said.

"There's the band."

"The founders' band." Odile's voice went sharp. "Ninety-one years, nobody's touched the founders' band. My master told me. His master told him. The _Mercy_'s people left it plain when they cut the crossing round the lip, so there'd be one clean thing on her, for the founding. You don't cut it."

"The founders are dead," said Dagny. "These are dead. Every one of them. Under the ice, standing up, with their eyes open. The Company wrote them out. The Throat's going to bury them. Nobody's ever going to know their names except you and me and a man who forged their letters home." She put her hand on the folder in Odile's lap. "Cut the band, Odile. Please."

The old woman looked at her for a long, long time.

Then she looked past her, at Pim, who was sitting on the end of the bench where he always sat, with his school bag at his feet and his dimmed chit on its cord round his neck.

"How steady are your hands, boy?" said Odile Marchbank.

* * *

They began at midnight.

Odile cut the first name herself. _Abara, Joseph._ She stood on her box under the bell with the engraver in her right hand and her left flat on the bronze, on the plain cast belt round the bell's waist that nobody had touched in ninety-one years, and set the burr to it, and the whine went up into the night ring. The letters came up bright. When she had finished she stood back and looked at them, and her face did not change at all, and Dagny could not tell whether she was grieving or furious or both.

"Next," Odile said, and held out the engraver to Pim.

He cut _Achebe, Ruth_. It took him forty minutes. The letters were uneven, too deep at the start and too shallow at the end, and the second _e_ went wandering off the line. Odile stood at his shoulder the whole time with her hand over his, not guiding, only resting there, and when he had finished she looked at it and said, "It's a name. That's all it has to be," and Pim, who had not smiled since the Wick, looked as if he might.

By the end of the night there were four of them. Mariam Oyelowo's daughter Efe came down from the farms, because Mariam had heard; and a widow from Gallery Ten called Grace Mbeki, whose husband had been on Lemuel's pump crew, who had worked fine metal in a jeweller's in the Concord before she came, and whose hands were better than Odile's. They took turns on the box. Two hours on, two off. One read the next name aloud from Teodor's folder; one cut; one rested; one held the lamp. By morning they had cut thirty-one names in a straight cramped line round the founders' band, and Odile was asleep on the bench with her mouth open and her shaking hands folded in her lap.

People came to watch, the next day. By the day after there was a crowd. Nobody on the Dock Ring had ever seen the bell being cut by anyone but the dockmaster, and certainly never round the clock, never like this, never so many names. They did not know whose names they were. Odile would not tell them. _Dead_, she said, if anyone asked. _Ours._ And people stood and watched the engravers take turns on the box, and listened to the names being read out one by one in the night ring, Achebe and Adair and Agbaje and on, and after a while some of them began to stay, and to sit on the ring road with their backs to the rock, and to say each name after the reader, under their breath, the way they said _Owing and owed_ at the bell.

* * *

She found Kas in the Slake two nights later, and he was not playing shove, and he was not losing. He was sitting at the end of the counter with a cup of the good Concord spirit in front of him — the real stuff, amber, that cost a week's wage a glass and was only ever served in the Upper Wards — and he was alone.

He saw her in the mirror behind the bar and did not turn round.

"Pim says you've been asking about seats," she said, and sat down beside him.

"Pim says a lot." He drank. "Clever boy. Gets it from you."

"Is it true?"

Kas turned the glass in his fingers for a while. Then he said, in a light easy voice that she had known since he was five, "Course I've asked. Everyone who's hauled for them's asked. They tell you there's no seats, there's never going to be seats, and then they pour you another one of these." He lifted the glass. "Hard credit, Dag. Spends on the other side. Not on this. I'm trying to drink it all before the door shuts."

She looked at him. He looked back at her in the mirror, with his handsome tired face and his easy smile, and she could not read him at all, for the first time in her life.

"I need you," she said.

"Course you do."

"Not for hauling. For a run." She leaned close, and kept her voice under the noise of the bar. "The Throat isn't closing, Kas. The Company's closing it. There's a shear on the _Covenant_ — a military thing, from the wars, Basu found it — and she's going to fire it behind her when she goes through, to bury Caul." She watched his face in the mirror. It did not change. "And Varga says the Concord won't save us. If the Throat stays open, they'll just sell us. Some lien-house on Kessane. Same chain, new name."

"Varga says."

"I believe him."

"So what's left?"

"Two things." She held up a finger. "One. On the morning the _Covenant_ leaves, the Director and Basu go down to the root together to close the Company's books. Both keys, one room, door open. We go in after them. Basu gives us his. We take hers. And we take the line out of the Tally — for good, for everybody, before they ever get on that ship." Another finger. "Two. The bell."

"The bell?"

"Odile's cutting them, Kas. The Caul dead. Every one of them, round the founders' band. Nine hundred and four. They'll be done by the last morning, if she lives." She held his eyes in the mirror. "And then somebody's going to take that bell through the Throat. In the _Covenant_'s wake, before the shear goes. A hauler can't make a throat on her own. But in a big ship's wake, with the lips held open for her—"

"The _Magpie_," said Kas.

"The _Magpie_. Out through the Throat, behind them, with the bell in her tank-frame. So the names get out. So there's one thing on the other side, in the Concord, that says what the Company did. Let them close the door on us. Fine. Let them bury us. But the names get out, and somebody has to answer for them." She drew breath. "Even if it's never us. Even if we never know."

Kas didn't say anything for a long time. He finished the amber spirit in his glass, slowly, and set it down on the heat-shield counter with a small click.

"Out through the Throat," he said. "In her wake. With the shear about to go off behind me."

"Yes."

"And then?"

"And then you're in the Concord. With the bell. And you find somebody who'll listen."

"And I never come back."

"No," said Dagny. "Nobody does."

He turned, then, and looked at her — not in the mirror, straight on — and for a moment his face was not easy at all; it was younger, and frightened, and very like their mother's.

"All right," he said. "All right, Dag. I'll fly it."

She put her hand over his on the counter. He let it stay there for a moment. Then he slid his own out from under it, and smiled — the smile from when he was five and had broken something — and signalled the barman for another glass of the amber.

In the doorway of the Slake, where neither of them saw him, Pim stood with his dimmed chit in his fist and watched his uncle's face in the mirror behind the bar, and did not come in.

# Chapter 14: The Wickwright

*The Owing · 311 AC*

The last master Wickwright on Lantern lived in a tin hut on the master ring at the root of the Wick, and he had not come down in eleven years.

Tamsin had never been up the spine. Nobody from below Fifteen had. The spoke-shafts were Count property, and the spine-road at the top of them ran past the Counting House, and the bailiffs on the spine-road knew a cutter's rig when they saw one. So they went up the way Orrin knew, which was the way counters went: through the Counting House by the service stair, at the change of third shift, with Orrin in his grey coat walking a little ahead and Tamsin a pace behind in a borrowed grey coat of his that was too long in the sleeves, carrying a slate she could not read, with her eyes down. Nobody stopped them. Nobody looked twice at two counters. That was the thing about the Count, Orrin had said on the stair, not quite joking: it had never once imagined that anybody would want to be in it who was not.

At the top of the service stair there was a door, and beyond the door there was the light.

She had lived her whole life under the Wick. She had never been close to it. It was the difference between a fire seen across a valley and a fire you could put your hand in. The lamp went up from the master ring in front of her, a column of white so bright it had no edges, straight up, into a sky that was not a sky but the far side of the world — terraces, farms, roofs, tanks, all hanging overhead, all upside down, all lit — and dwindling away along the axis, kilometre after kilometre, smaller and smaller, until two-thirds of the way down it simply stopped. Beyond the stop was the dark. From here, at the root, she could see the whole length of what was left: the lamp, the light, the Galleries under it; and beyond it, at the aft end of the world, the Cold, a great grey bowl of shadow, like the inside of a cup.

There was no weight. She had not been ready for that either. Her stomach turned over, and her feet lifted a little off the grating, and Orrin caught her sleeve and clipped her to the rail with a tether from his belt, the way someone must have taught him to.

"Don't look down," he said. "There isn't a down. That's what makes it worse."

The hut was on the far side of the master ring, a box of corrugated tin welded to the catwalk, with a stovepipe and a door made of an old collimator cover. The man who opened it was the oldest person Tamsin had ever seen. He was tiny, bent, bald, with skin like folded paper, and his eyes were milky white from edge to edge, both of them, with no colour left in them at all.

"Counter," said Ninian. He had not looked at them; he was looking at a point between them, a little to the left. "You're the one who's been reading my light ledger."

Orrin went still. "How do you—"

"Counters' boots. Soft soles. And you breathe like a man who's been sitting down all his life. And you've got somebody with you who hasn't." He turned his blind eyes toward Tamsin, not quite on her. "Cutter. You smell of the Cold. Copper and frost." He stood back from the door. "Come in, then. I don't get visitors. I've got tea."

* * *

The tea was terrible. The hut was warm, and smelled of hot metal and old man, and every surface in it was covered with tools Tamsin did not know the names of, laid out in rows so precise that she understood at once that he found every one of them by touch. Ninian sat on his cot with his cup in both hands and listened while Orrin told him. He did not interrupt. When Orrin got to the stencil on the floor of the fifth hall, and the fourteen units, he set his cup down very carefully on the floor between his feet.

"Fourteen," he said.

"In the Hulk's reactor. Held. Since the Founding."

The old man was silent for a long time. Then he laughed — a thin, dry, terrible sound, with no breath in it.

"I asked," he said. "Did you know that? Forty years ago. When I was made master. I went to the Comptroller-General of the day — not Vosk, the one before the one before him — and I said, _There's a line in the light ledger I don't understand. Reserve. Held. What's held?_ And he told me it was a story. A Founders' story. A line they'd carried forward for luck. He said, _You're a Wickwright, Ninian, you keep the lamp. Leave the ledgers to us._ And I did." He turned his white eyes toward the doorway, toward the blaze of the lamp outside. "I've shortened her six times since then. Six times I've gone down the spine with my crew and closed the collimators on the aft end and watched another two hundred metres of her go dark, and another gallery go cold. Every time, the Count told me there was nothing else. Every time I believed it." He picked up the cup again. His hands were quite steady. "Fourteen."

"Can the Wick take them?" said Tamsin. "If we got them up here. Could you load them?"

"Could I load them." He said it flatly, as if she had asked whether he could breathe. "Girl, I've loaded every cassette that's gone into her for fifty years. There's a port at the master ring. The Founders' port. Right there." He pointed, without looking, at the wall of the hut, beyond which, she supposed, it was. "She takes them one at a time, through the port, into the bottle. Each one burns about a third of her length for three years, or the whole of her for one. Fourteen—" He stopped. "Fourteen would light her end to end. All the way down. For fourteen years. The Cold Wards and all."

Nobody said anything. Outside, the lamp hummed.

"But you'd have to get them here," said Ninian. "And they're not light. Each one's the weight of four men, and the size of a coffin, and hot, a little, always, even asleep. You don't carry a cassette. You move it on a sled, on rails, with a winch. And the Hulk's under the Counting House, and the Counting House is the Count."

"There's a lift," said Orrin. "On the old plans. In the archive. A freight lift from the Hulk's reactor deck straight up to the spine-road, beside the Counting House. It's marked _sealed_."

"The Founders' lift." Ninian nodded slowly. "I know it. There's a door on the spine-road. Big. Welded. I've walked past it every day of my life." He was quiet again. "You'd have to come up from inside the Hulk. From frame forty. Through the Sanctum."

"Yes."

"Nobody goes through the Sanctum but the Comptroller-General."

"Yes," said Orrin.

The old Wickwright sat with his cup in his hands and his blind eyes on the doorway for a long time. Then he said, so quietly that Tamsin almost did not hear it over the hum of the lamp:

"Do you know the Twilight Song?"

Tamsin did. Everybody below Fifteen did. It was one of the old ones, from before the Count, that they sang at the Hook sometimes: a slow, low tune, about a time when the Wick crews had turned the lamp down to a dusk and the whole world had stood on its terraces and looked up at the light and seen it, for once, as a thing someone had made. Nobody knew when it was from. Nobody knew if it had happened.

"My master taught me that the Wick crews made it," said Ninian. "In the Closing year. Before the Count. The crews took the lamp down, to make the Company listen. And the Company didn't, and men died on the spine, and the crews brought her back up." He turned his head toward Tamsin, and for a moment his white eyes seemed almost to find her. "But they took her down. For three days, they took her down. Because she was theirs." He set the cup on the floor again. "I'll load your fourteen, cutter. If you bring them. I'll load every one of them if it's the last thing I do. It will be, probably. I'm ninety."

* * *

The second thousand went into the Cold at second shift the next day. Agathe Hask was in the third, in three days.

Tamsin tried to pay.

She had known it would not work. She did it anyway, because her mother had been a churchgoer for thirty years and had believed in the Count's arithmetic as she believed in the air, and Tamsin wanted, once, to have done the thing the Count said you could do, so that afterwards she would know. She sold the copper. All of it: the stash under the floor of the Hask room that she had been keeping for the winter, eighteen metres of it, to Brannock at the Salt Docks for washers, and the washers at the chit-shop on Nineteen at a quarter off. She sold her spare rig. She sold the good saw, and kept the old one. She sold her mother's winter coat, which her mother would not need where she was going — the thought came to her like that, plain and cold, and she sold the coat. When she was done she had three hundred and eleven credits on her own chit. It was more money than she had ever held at once in her life.

The Count's public window was on the ground floor of the Counting House, at the end of the third hall, behind a brass grille. There was a queue. There had been a queue every day since the lists went up — people from the third list and the fourth and the fifth, with whatever they had been able to sell, trying to buy their way back over the line. Tamsin queued for three hours. When she got to the grille the counter behind it was a heavy grey-haired man with ink on his cuffs, and she knew him at once, from the table at the Lip.

"Payment," she said. "Against the account of Hask, Agathe. From Hask, Tamsin."

Senior Counter Abney took her chit and touched it to his slate, and the slate chimed. He looked at it.

"Three hundred and eleven," he said. "Posted. The account of Hask, Agathe, now stands at minus ten thousand eight hundred and ninety-five."

"So she's off the list."

"No."

Tamsin stood very still.

"Payments during a Calling are accepted and posted," said Abney, in the voice of a man who has said the same sentence two thousand times in three days and will say it two thousand more. "They reduce the balance. They do not alter the order of the lists, which was fixed at the time of posting. Hask, Agathe, is on the third list. The third list goes on the twenty-fourth."

"Then what's the payment for?"

"It reduces the balance," said Abney, "which will be released from the Tally's charge on the twenty-fourth, and held quit by the Account." He handed her back her chit. Her own balance on it read zero. "Next."

She did not move. Behind her the queue shifted and muttered.

"You taught him long division," she said.

Abney looked at her properly for the first time. His eyes were small and grey and tired.
