"Who?"

"Orrin Sallow. Your counter. Third hall. He says you're the most careful man he knows." She leaned close to the grille. "He found an error in her account. Two thousand credits that aren't hers. You refused it. And now she goes into the dark on the twenty-fourth with two thousand on her that she doesn't owe, and three hundred of mine that she does, and there's not a thing in your whole careful Count that will stop it. Is that right? Have I done the sum right?"

Senior Counter Abney was silent for a moment. Then he said, quite quietly, without any change of expression: "Yes. You've done it right."

"Next," he said, past her.

She walked out of the Counting House into the white light of the Upper Wards with her empty chit in her fist. Orrin was waiting on the steps. He had watched it all through the window of the third hall; she could see it on his face. He did not say anything. He fell into step beside her and they walked down the terraces together, out of the light, toward the galleries, in silence, all the way to Fifteen.

* * *

"Ten," said Wenna Blackwood.

They were behind the cargo-net curtain at the back of Brannock's cabin, the four of them — Tamsin and Orrin on one side of the table, Wenna on the other, Jory on an upturned crate by the stove eating the goat stew nobody else had touched — and Wenna had just been told about the fourteen.

She had listened to all of it without a word. When Orrin got to the stencil she had shut her one eye for a moment, as if something hurt. When he got to the auxiliary lamp in the Upper Wards she had laughed, once, softly. And when he had finished, and Tamsin had told her what they meant to do, she had sat back in her chair and folded her great scarred arms and said: "Ten."

"Ten what?" said Orrin.

"Ten of the fourteen. To the Salt. You want my people to go into the Hulk with you, under the Count, past Lusk and every bailiff he's got, and bring out fourteen cassettes the size of coffins and get them up the spine on sleds. That's a war, counter. That's my people's lives. Ten." She shrugged. "You can have four for your blind old man and his lamp."

"Four's a year at full length," said Tamsin. "Barely three at a third."

"Then he'd better make them last."

"And what do you do with ten?" said Orrin. "There's nobody to sell them to. There's no market for fuel on Lantern except the Count."

"Then I sell them to the Count." Wenna smiled, without any warmth. "At a price. Or I build a lamp of my own on the Salt Docks, and light it, and see how many people come aft along the ring road to live under it, and what they'll pay." She leaned forward. "You think I'm a bad woman, counter. I can see it on your soft face. I'm not. I'm a practical one. The Count's going to put four thousand people in the light and leave the rest to the dark. I'm offering to put a few thousand more in a different light. Mine. That's not a lifeboat with a man at the door. That's two lifeboats."

"With a man at each door," said Tamsin.

Wenna looked at her with her one eye. "There's always a man at the door, girl. Always. The only question's whether you're on the inside of it when it shuts."

Nobody said anything. By the stove Jory had stopped eating.

"Seven," said Tamsin at last. "Seven to the Wick. Seven to you. And you light yours on the Salt Docks, and you open it to everyone, Owing or Called or whoever comes aft, and you don't charge for the light."

"I don't charge for the light," Wenna repeated, as if testing the taste of it.

"You've never charged for it. You've never had any. Start as you mean to go on."

The silence went on for a long time. Then Wenna Blackwood laughed — the big gravel laugh — and slapped the table so hard the stew jumped in the pot.

"Agathe Hask's girl," she said. "God help the Count." She put out her hand. "Seven. And I'll see you on the spine."

Tamsin took it.

* * *

Orrin went back up to the Counting House alone, late, through the back of the Upper Wards, with the old freight-lift plans folded flat inside his shirt where he had carried them all day, and his head full of frame numbers and sled weights and the timing of the bailiffs' rounds in the fifth hall. He was thinking so hard about all of it that he did not see the man on the steps until he was almost on top of him.

Brother Lusk was sitting on the top step of the Counting House, in the white light, with his docket-baton across his knees. He did not get up.

"Counter Sallow," he said. He had a slow, mild, unremarkable voice. Orrin had never heard it before; Lusk did not, as a rule, talk to counters. "You've been going down a great deal."

Orrin stopped. "I've had sightings."

"Have you." Lusk looked at him, pleasantly, from under his pale brows. "Twenty-Two, mostly. And the Salt Docks. I didn't know the Count had accounts on the Salt Docks."

"It— there are some. Yes."

"I'm sure there are." Lusk turned the baton over in his big pale hands, once, and laid it back across his knees. "The galleries are no place for a counter, Counter Sallow. Not these days. People are upset. Things happen to men in good coats, down there. It would grieve the Comptroller-General very much if anything happened to you. He thinks highly of you. He's told me so."

"Thank you, Brother."

"Owing and owed," said Brother Lusk.

"Owing and owed."

He went up the steps past him, and in at the door, and along the third hall, and sat down at his desk in the dark with his hands flat on the slate and the plans pressing into his chest under his shirt, and did not move for a long time, because he could not make his hands stop shaking.

# Chapter 15: The Far Side of Nhai

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

Kell's bailiffs drilled in Hold 1, the forward cargo bay, which they had cleared of everything but crates stacked to make corridors.

Corvin went down to watch on the third morning, because Haste had told him to — _you'll need to know how it's done, Mr. Saal, when it comes to it; the speech is only the beginning_ — and because, after the night in Hold 2, he had found that he could not sit in his cabin with the case on his desk any longer without wanting to put his fist through the porthole. So he stood on the gantry at the top of the bay and watched them.

They were good. He had seen bailiffs on the colony circuit, plenty of them, hired muscle in borrowed uniforms who drank on duty and broke things for fun. Kell's people were nothing like that. They moved through the crate-corridors in pairs, quietly, with their stun-batons held low, and they practised the same thing over and over. A door. A knock. A pair going in. Two of their own playing residents — a man and a woman, in civilian coats, sitting on crates — and the pair standing over them, not threatening, not raising their voices. One holding out a scanner. The other with a slate. _Name. Number, if you have one. Hold out your wrist, please._ And the residents, who had been told to resist, saying no, or saying nothing, or getting up to go — and the bailiffs, without hurry, without anger, putting them down. A baton across the back of the knees, and a hand on the neck, and the scanner on the wrist, and the slate chiming. Then up again, dusted off, and on to the next door.

Over and over. Hours.

Kell came up onto the gantry beside him at the end of the second hour, wiping his face with a towel.

"Enumeration," he said. "That's the first day. Every person in the habitat, scanned, named, numbered, entered on our book. Any old book they've got, we read across. Any account they're carrying, we merge into ours. Takes a week, for a big one."

"And if they won't be numbered?"

"Then we number them anyway." Kell said it the way a man explains how to tie a knot. "Most people don't fight. People want to know where they stand. They'd rather owe somebody than nobody. You'd be surprised."

"And the ones who fight?"

Kell looked down into the bay, where the bailiff playing the resident woman was sitting on a crate rubbing the back of her knee and laughing at something her partner had said.

"Then they come off the books," he said.

"Meaning what?"

"Meaning they haven't got an account. No account, no ration. No ration, no air, in a place like that. No legal person, under the instrument. You can't owe if you're not on the books, and you can't be owed. You just stop." He folded the towel. "It's quieter than it sounds. Mostly they come back in after a day or two. Hungry."

Corvin said nothing for a while. In the bay below, the pairs had started again from the first door.

"Are you bonded, Sergeant?" he said.

Kell turned his head and looked at him with his bored, patient eyes, and for the first time there was something in them that was not boredom. It might have been amusement.

"Everybody's bonded, Mr. Saal," he said. "I've got eleven years left. My daughter's got twenty-six, from her mother's side, unless I clear mine first, and then it comes off hers." He put the towel round his neck. "This job clears four. If it goes well."

"And if it doesn't?"

"Then it doesn't clear four," said Kell, and went back down the ladder to his people.

* * *

Fen played him the bell that night.

Not the bell itself; it was strapped upright in the blister where it had been all voyage, silent, and she had never once, he realised, struck it. A recording. She had it on a tiny old player, the size of a coin, that she wore on a chain round her neck under her shirt, next to the loupe; she unclipped it and held it out to him on her palm in the dim light of the blister.

"My grandmother made it," she said. "When I was six. She hung it from a beam in our kitchen at Tannet and rang it once, with a padded hammer, so I'd know what it sounded like. She said I should know. She said everybody in the family should hear it once. It's the only time anyone's rung it, as far as I know, since it came out of the Throat." She put the player against his ear. "Listen."

He listened.

There was a hiss, an old kitchen's hiss, a child's voice somewhere saying something he could not make out. Then a single stroke. A low note, not loud, a little sour — not the pure clean note he had expected from a bell, but something rougher and sadder, with a waver in it, as if the bronze were tired. It went on for a long time. It hummed and hummed and slowly faded, and under it, faint, the child's voice again, and an old woman's voice saying, quite clearly, _There. That's her. That's what they heard, every time somebody died._

Then nothing.

He handed it back. He found he did not trust his voice for a moment.

"She'd have liked you," said Fen. "My grandmother. She liked people who looked like they'd been hit."

"Is that how I look?"

"Since the hold. Yes." She clipped the player back on its chain and tucked it away. "Tell me something. One of your four hundred and twelve. Tell me one."

So he told her one. He had not meant to. He told her about Malory's Reach, nine years ago, a frost-farm settlement on a moon so cold the domes had to be heated every hour of the year, and a family called Abernathy whose heating debt had been bought by a lien-house he worked for, and the morning he had stood in their kitchen and explained, in the voice, that the dome's heating contract was being reassigned to the lien-house's account and that their quarter would be serviced on the new schedule. Which meant every second day. Which meant, in Malory's Reach in the long winter, nothing at all. He told her how the mother had listened without saying anything, and nodded, and asked him if he would like tea. How he had said no. How he had gone back nine weeks later to tag the empty quarter for resale, and found it was not empty.

Fen listened with her knees drawn up and her arms round them and did not say anything at all until he had finished, and then for a long time after.

"Why are you telling me?" she said at last.

"Because you asked."

"That isn't why."

He looked at the bell. "Because there's a hold full of people under us who are going to be Abernathys," he said. "And the rock behind that planet's full of them already, or was. And I came all this way to explain it to them. And I don't know what I'm going to do."

She reached out, then, and put her hand flat against the side of his face — briefly, firmly, the way she put her hand on the bell — and held it there for a moment, and took it away.

"Then don't know," she said. "For now. It's better than knowing the wrong thing."

* * *

The alarm went at four in the morning: a long rising whoop from somewhere aft, that went through the whole ship and had everybody out of their bunks before it had finished its first cycle.

Corvin was in the engine-room corridor in two minutes. Tol was already there, half inside the panel of the Hold 2 power board with a torch in his teeth, and Ode was behind him in a coat over her nightshirt, and through the open main doors of Hold 2, which somebody had unsealed, there was light and cold and the sound of people moving fast.

"Cascade fault," said Tol, round the torch. "Power bus to Rack Forty-One. Three pods dropped out of the cycle. Two came back on the backup. One didn't."

"Which one?"

Tol took the torch out of his mouth and looked at him.

"Forty-one oh-nine-seven-three," he said.

Corvin went into the hold.

The ship's medic was there, a heavy woman called Petrosyan, and two of Kell's bailiffs with lamps, and the pod at the end of Rack Forty-One stood open, its lid folded back, steam pouring off it in long white ropes into the cold air of the hold. The light at its foot was red. In the pod, half sitting up, with the medic's arm behind her shoulders and a foil blanket round her, was Marisol Tey.

She was awake. That was the worst of it. Corvin knew enough about cold sleep to know what an uncontrolled revival did — the blood thawing too fast, the cells bursting — and the medic's face told him the rest. But Marisol Tey was awake, for the moment, with her shaved head and her blue lips and her eyes open, looking up at the lights of the hold with an expression of enormous, patient confusion, the way a woman might look on waking in a stranger's house.

"Is this Sabra?" she said. Her voice was a thread. "Are we there?"

Corvin knelt beside the pod. He did not know why he did. The medic glanced at him and shook her head, once, very slightly.

"Nearly," he said. "We're nearly there."

"They said there'd be work." Her eyes found him. "They said— the contract— is my girl's contract— did they say? It attaches on majority. She's six. They said if I worked it down there— if I worked hard, the attachment would—" She stopped. She seemed to lose the thread of it. Her hand came out of the foil blanket and found his sleeve and held it. "Will you ask them? For me? Whether it attaches?"

"I'll ask them," said Corvin.

"Thank you." She closed her eyes. "It's very cold."

She died about four minutes later, with her hand still on his sleeve. The medic said the time aloud, and one of the bailiffs wrote it on a slate. Nobody else said anything.

When Corvin stood up, Quennell Haste was in the aisle behind him, in a dressing-gown of dark silk, with his hands in its pockets.

"Unfortunate," said Haste. "Sergeant, log one unit lost to equipment failure, please, and have the pod cleared. Mr. Saal, I'd like a word. And Captain—" he turned to Ode, who had come in behind him— "I'd like your engineer confined to quarters until we've established why a power bus on a certified cold-sleep rack failed at four in the morning in a hold that only he knows how to enter."

"Tol didn't touch your bloody rack," said Ode.

"Then he'll have nothing to fear from an inquiry." Haste turned back to Corvin. "Walk with me."

* * *

He walked Corvin up through the ship, not to his cabin, but to the observation blister.

Fen was there. She had not come down for the alarm. She was sitting on the deck in her nightclothes beside the bell, and when she saw Haste in the hatchway she got up, slowly, and stood between him and the bronze without seeming to know she had done it.

Behind Haste, on the ladder, there were two of Kell's bailiffs with a cargo sled.

"Miss Adaire," said Haste. "I'm sorry to disturb you. I'm afraid I've been remiss. I should have attended to this at Ambry." He took a slate from the pocket of his dressing-gown. "The bell of the colony carrier _Ardent Mercy_ was, at the date of the Closing, the property of the Halden-Ash Company's Sabra Concern. It was removed from the Concern's habitat at or about the Closing by persons unknown. It has since passed through several hands, in good faith, I'm sure. But property of a concern that is removed from it attaches, on recovery, to the instrument under which that concern's assets are held." He turned the slate round so that she could see it. "That instrument is now Brightwater's. The bell is an asset of the Sabra Concern. I'm taking it into secure storage in Hold 1 for the remainder of the voyage."

Fen looked at the slate. Then at him.

"It's mine," she said. "My family's. Six generations. We paid for it."

"Eleven crowns, I believe, at Tannet. Brightwater will of course reimburse you." He nodded to the bailiffs. "Please."

They came past him into the blister with the sled. Fen did not move. One of them put a hand on her arm — not roughly; the drilled, patient hand from Hold 1 — and she shook it off, and the other one stepped in, and Corvin saw the stun-baton come up, held low.

"Don't," said Corvin.

Everyone looked at him. Haste raised his eyebrows.

"Mr. Saal?"

Corvin stood in the hatchway of the blister with the cold of Hold 2 still in his clothes and Marisol Tey's hand still, he could have sworn, on his sleeve. He looked at Fen, at the baton, at the bell. He looked at Haste, who was waiting with polite interest for whatever he was going to say. He knew what he wanted to say. He had known it since the hold. He could feel the shape of it in his mouth.

"Don't hurt her," he said instead.

"Of course not," said Haste. "Miss Adaire, please stand aside."

Fen looked at Corvin for a long moment. Then she stood aside.

They unstrapped the bell from the couch legs and tipped it onto the sled, and it rang as it tipped — a single soft unintended note, low and sour, the note from the recording, humming in the small glass dome — and then it was on the sled and strapped down and going out through the hatch, and Haste was holding out the slate to Corvin.

"The seizure docket," he said. "It wants the adjuster's concurrence. It's only a formality."

Corvin looked at the slate. _Property recovered under instrument. Adjuster concurs._ A line for his mark.

He did not take the stylus. He did not refuse it either. He stood there.

After a moment Haste smiled, and marked the line himself — _Adjuster concurs (verbal)_ — and put the slate back in his pocket.

"Good night, Mr. Saal. Miss Adaire."

When he had gone, the blister was very quiet. The ring of slates still lay on the deck where Fen had left them, round the empty place where the bell had stood, like a circle round a grave.

"You said nothing," said Fen.

"I said don't hurt you."

"You said nothing." She was not shouting. She was looking at him with an expression he had seen on four hundred and twelve settlements, and had never once, until now, had turned on him by someone whose good opinion he wanted. "You stood there and you let him write it down. _Adjuster concurs._ That's you, Corvin. That's what you are. Verbal."

She went past him down the ladder and did not look back.

# Chapter 16: The Sale

*The Closing · 2387 CE*

They cut the last name at eleven at night on the forty-seventh day, with half the Dock Ring watching.

It was Grace Mbeki's turn on the box, but she stood down from it when they came to the last page of Teodor's folder, and held out the engraver to Odile. The old woman had not cut a name in four days. Her hands had gone. They shook all the time now, even in her lap, even asleep; she had spent the last four days on the bench under the bell with a blanket round her, reading names aloud for the others in a voice that had gone to a whisper, and sleeping between them. She looked at the engraver for a long time.

"I'll hold it," said Pim. "You hold my hand. Like you did."

So they did it that way. Pim on the box, with the engraver, and Odile standing behind him on the ring road with her shaking hand laid over his steady one, the way she had laid it over his on the first night; and the last name came up bright on the founders' band, at the very end of the long cramped grey line that went all the way round the bell's waist and met its own beginning.

_Zeller, Hanna._

Pim stepped down.

Then Odile took the engraver out of his hand. Nobody stopped her. She climbed onto the box herself, slowly, with Grace Mbeki holding her elbow, and put the burr to the crown of the bell, up among the old cast leaves of the decoration where there was no room left for a name, and cut something there, very small, with her own shaking hand, so that the letters wandered off their line and came back to it. It took her half an hour. When she came down she would not say what she had written. _It's for whoever has her next_, was all she said.

Nobody said anything. All along the ring road, where people had been sitting with their backs to the rock every night for fourteen nights saying the names after the reader, there was a silence like the silence for the bell. Odile looked up at the bronze for a long time — at the band, full now, nine hundred and four names round the founders' belt that nobody had touched for ninety-one years — and then she reached up, shaking, and took the bell-rope, and rang it.

Once.

It was not for anybody. There was no new death. She rang it anyway, and the low sour note went out across the Dock Ring and round the curve of the world in the dark, and on the ring road the people who had been saying the names stood up, one by one, and stood still for it, and then, without anyone telling them to, began to go home.

"That's all of them," said Odile. "That's all of them, Dagny."

"Thank you."

"Don't thank me." The old woman lowered herself onto the bench. "Go and do what you're going to do with it."

* * *

They took the bell down at two in the morning.

Kas brought the _Magpie_'s crane-arm round from the hauler berth on the ring's outer face, where she lay in her cradle with her tank-frame open and empty, and three of the Wick crew climbed the Hook with chains and slings. It took an hour. The iron hook had held the bell for ninety-one years and did not want to let it go; the shackle had rusted into the bracket, and in the end Dagny had to cut it with a torch, with the bell hanging in the slings beside her face, close enough to read the names on the shoulder by the light of the flame. _Joss Teague. Bronwen Price. Lemuel Adeyemi._ When the shackle parted the bell swung, once, in the slings, and gave a small low hum, and the whole ring road heard it, and nobody spoke.

They lowered it into a cargo cradle and wheeled it out through the inner lock along the berth to the _Magpie_, and Kas's crane lifted it into the tank-frame and his crew strapped it down in a nest of ice-sacking, between the empty tanks, where it looked very small.

Then there was nothing on the Hook.

Dagny stood under it afterwards for a while, on her own, when the others had gone. It was strange. She had never once in her life looked up at the Dock Ring's inner lock and not seen the bell. The Hook was only an iron bracket now, black, empty, with the stub of the cut shackle hanging from it. She reached up and touched the stub. It was still warm from the torch.

"Mum."

Pim had not gone home. He had been sitting on the bench in the dark all along, watching.

"Bed," she said. "It's nearly morning."

"Is Uncle Kas going tomorrow? With the bell?"

"After the _Covenant_. If we can do what we have to do in the morning. Then he goes, in her wake." She sat down beside him on the bench. "He'll be in the Concord. With the names. Somebody there will have to listen."

"And he won't come back."

"No."

Pim was quiet for a long time. Then he said, in a very small voice, "I saw him in the Slake. When you told him. In the mirror." He did not look at her. "He looked funny."

"Funny how?"

"I don't know." He was turning his dimmed chit over and over in his fingers. "Like when he loses at shove and pretends he doesn't mind."

She put her arm round him. "He's frightened, Pim. He's going through a throat in a tug with a shear going off behind him. Anyone would be."

"Yes," said Pim. "I suppose."

Kas came back along the berth a little before dawn, with his helmet under his arm, to say goodnight. He hugged Dagny hard, for a long time, harder than he had since their mother's funeral, and then he crouched in front of Pim on the bench and unpinned the little tin pilot's wings from his collar — the cheap enamel pin the hauler crews gave each other when somebody qualified — and pinned them on Pim's school jacket.

"Look after her," he said.

"You look after the bell," said Pim.

Kas laughed, and ruffled his hair, and went.

* * *

The root of the Tally was three doors down, under the Counting House, in a place that nobody on Lantern had ever seen.

Teodor brought them in. He had told Dagny the way three nights before, in the locker room, with a diagram scratched on the back of a ration card: the comptroller's service stair down past the archive levels, to a door that opened to his key alone; then a corridor; then the second door, which opened to the Director's seal or the comptroller's key, either; then the third door, which opened only to both. The Director and he would go through all three together at seven. They would leave the second and third open behind them while they worked, because the charter required a witness from Security to stand in the doorway, and the witness would be Varga, or one of Varga's, alone. The first door, Teodor would leave unlocked behind him.

"Twenty minutes," he had said. "That's all there'll be. I'll keep her at the console for twenty minutes. After that she'll close the trust and come out."

So at five past seven on the forty-eighth day of the Closing, Dagny Ostrova came down the comptroller's service stair with Ezra Hesketh behind her, leaning on his stick, and six of the Wick crew behind him with pry-bars and wrenches and a stunner one of them had taken off a Company man in the strike, and they went through the first door, which was unlocked, as Teodor had said it would be.

The corridor beyond was long and plain and lit with a cold blue light. At the end of it the second door stood open. Beyond that, the third.

Dagny went through them both at a run.

The root was a round room, not large, with a low domed ceiling, and at its centre on a pedestal a console no bigger than a writing desk — a dark slab of glass and brass, very old, very plain, with two slots in its face side by side and a little grille beside them like the grille of a confessional. That was all. She had expected more. She had expected, she realised, something like the Wick: a machine you could see was the heart of the world. This was a desk. The Tally of Lantern, that every valve on the rock asked whose air it was, lived in a desk.

Ruth Achterberg stood at it with her hand on the seal, which was in the left-hand slot, on its chain. Teodor Basu stood beside her, with his comptroller's key in the right-hand slot. They both turned as Dagny came through the door.

Teodor's face, when he saw her, was the face of a man who has been hit and has not yet fallen down.

"No," he said. "No— get back— _go_—"

And Hale Varga said from behind her, quite quietly, "Stand still, Ostrova."

She turned. They were in the corridor behind her. They had been in the second doorway, she understood afterwards, in the alcoves either side of it, where the cold blue light did not reach — twelve of them, in armour, with slug-guns, not stunners. They had let her pass. They had let her people pass. Now they were in the doorway, and Hesketh was standing in the middle of the corridor between them and her with his stick in his hand and his old face quite calm, and the Wick crew were behind him with their pry-bars, frozen.

"Put them down," said Varga. "All of you. Put them down and nobody else gets hurt."

"Nobody else," said Hesketh. "Who got hurt already, Mr. Varga?"

One of the Wick crew — a girl called Dorothea Clay, nineteen, who had been on the master ring with Dagny in the strike — had the stunner. Dagny saw her hand move. She saw it the way she had seen Joss's arm swing on the Wick: slow, weightless, stupid, too late to stop.

"Dorrie, no—"

The corridor was very loud. The slug-guns were nothing like the stunners. They were short hard flat bangs, like doors slammed in a stairwell, a great many of them, all at once, and the blue light jumped with them. When they stopped, Dorothea Clay was on the floor of the corridor, and so were two of the crew behind her, and so was Ezra Hesketh, on his side, with his stick a metre from his hand, looking at the ceiling with an expression of mild surprise, as if somebody had told him a joke he had not heard before.

And Teodor Basu was on the floor of the root, at the foot of the console, holding his side.

Dagny did not see him fall. She saw him afterwards — the blood coming through his fingers, very bright on the Company grey, and his face gone the colour of paper, and his glasses with the cracked lens lying beside him. She understood later that he had moved when the shooting started. That he had come round the console toward her, toward the door, with his hands out, to put himself between her and the corridor; and that one of Varga's men had seen a figure move in the root and fired.

"Teodor," she said.

"I'm sorry," he said, from the floor. "I'm so sorry. I didn't— I didn't tell them. I swear. I never—"

"I know you didn't," said Ruth Achterberg.

She had not moved from the console. Her hand was still on the seal. She looked down at her comptroller on the floor with an expression Dagny could not read, and then at Dagny, in the doorway, with Varga's men behind her.

"Your brother came to me twelve days ago, Ms. Ostrova," said the Director. "In the bar in the Upper Wards. He was very frank. He told me everything: the root, the time, the doors, the bell, his run in the _Covenant_'s wake. He said he'd been asked to fly it and he wasn't a fool, and he'd rather have a seat." She turned the seal in its slot, and the console made a soft chime. "I gave him two. One for him. One for your son."

Dagny did not say anything. She found she could not.

"I'm sorry," said Ruth Achterberg, and Dagny believed, for the first time, that she meant it. "Truly. I would have liked this to end differently, for both of us. Mr. Varga, take her up. Have the comptroller seen to. I'll finish the transfer myself." She looked down at Teodor. "His key's in the slot. I'll only need him to say the words."

* * *

They put her in a cell on the security floor, a steel box with a cot and a drain and a slot in the door. Nobody hit her. Nobody spoke to her. At ten o'clock the slot opened and a tray came through with bread and bean paste and a cup of water, and she left it on the floor where it fell.

At eleven the door opened and Kas came in.

He was in a good coat. A Company coat, dark blue, with the H-A on the breast, too big for him across the shoulders, as if it had been somebody else's. He had his helmet under his arm. He stood just inside the door and did not come any further, and behind him in the corridor she could see Varga's broad back, and beside Varga, small, in his school jacket, with the tin pilot's wings on the collar, Pim.

"Dag," said Kas.

She sat on the cot and looked at him.

"I didn't know they'd shoot," he said. "I swear to God, Dag. I told her the time and the doors. I said, _Just stop them, just get there first, nobody has to get hurt._ She said nobody would. She said Varga would take you at the door and that'd be it. I didn't— I didn't know about Hesketh. And the girl. And Basu." His face moved. "I didn't know."

"What did you think they'd do?"

"I don't know! I didn't think!" It came out of him in a rush. "I thought about _him_." He jerked his head back at the corridor, at Pim. "I thought about him with his chit dimmed at twelve, sleeping on a bench in a locker room, and you telling me to fly a tug through a throat with a shear going off behind me, and the pair of you staying here to owe air to a box for six hundred years, and I thought— I thought, I can get one of us out. One. Just one. I can get the boy out." He was breathing hard. "I got him a seat, Dag. A seat out. On the _Covenant_. To the Concord. He'll have a life. He'll go to a real school. He'll never see a lock in recovery again in his life. You can hate me. Go on. But he'll be out."

"You sold nine thousand for one," said Dagny.

"I sold nine thousand who were already sold!" He was shouting now. "Everyone on this rock was sold before they were born! Varga told you that! What was your plan going to do? Take the line out of a box for a few hundred years? Send a bell to the Concord so some court could fine the Company and sell us all to somebody else? We were always going to lose, Dag. Always. I just— I made sure we didn't lose him too."

The cell was very quiet after that. Varga, in the corridor, had not turned round.

Dagny looked past her brother, at her son.

Pim was standing very straight beside Varga, the way he had stood at school the day his chit dimmed, with everybody looking. He was looking at her. He had not cried. She thought he had probably decided not to.

"Mum," he said. "I'm not going."

"Pim—"

"I'm not. I told him. I'm staying with you. I don't care about a seat."

And she understood, then, that this was the last thing, and the worst. That Kas was a liar and a coward and had sold Hesketh and Dorrie Clay and Teodor Basu for a seat on a ship — and that he was also, about this one thing, right. That her son was twelve and dimmed. That in six hundred years he would still be dimmed, and his children after him, and that there was nothing she had left, nothing at all, that could change it.

"You're going," said Dagny.

Pim stared at her.

"You're going with your uncle. On the _Covenant_. Through the Throat." She made herself say it level, the way she had said numbers out loud on the Wick all her life. "You're going to go to a real school, and you're going to grow up somewhere with a sky, and you're never going to see a lock in recovery again. And you're going to remember. Everything. The bell. The names. The Wick. All of it. You're going to remember it for me."

"No—"

"Yes," said Dagny. "Kas. Take him."

Her brother looked at her for a long moment, with his handsome ruined face. Then he turned and put his hand on Pim's shoulder. Pim did not move. Then he did — not toward Kas; toward her, one step, two, into the cell — and she got up off the cot and caught him and held him, hard, the whole thin length of him, with her face in his hair, for as long as she could stand it, which was not very long.

Then she let him go, and Kas took him out, and Varga closed the door.

* * *

At noon she felt it through the floor of the cell: a long, deep, rising shudder that came up through the rock and the steel and her bones, as the _Covenant_'s great drive lit at the fore dock and she pulled away from Lantern for the last time.

It went on for a long while. Then it faded. Then there was nothing but the ordinary hum of the rock.

She sat on the cot with her hands in her lap and looked at the door.

Some time later — she did not know how long; she had stopped counting — the slot in the door opened, and nobody pushed a tray through it. Somebody was looking in.

"Ostrova," said Hale Varga's voice. "You awake?"

She did not answer.

"I didn't get on," said Varga. "The ship. I was on the list. I didn't get on."

The slot closed. And then, after a moment, very slowly, as if whoever was on the other side were still making up his mind, the lock of the cell door began to turn.

# Chapter 17: The Calling

*The Owing · 311 AC*

They went in on the night before the third list, because there was no other night left.

Orrin had found the hatch on the second evening, on his knees again in the fifth hall with his stylus, scraping. It was where the stencil had said it would be: a square of plating a metre across, two paces from the wall, with a recessed ring-pull at one edge and a seam all the way round that three centuries of grey paint had filled until it looked like floor. REACTOR ACCESS — FRAME 40. Under the Count, under the ledger halls, under the plaque and the brass rail and the children's hands, a way down into the Hulk that nobody had opened since the Founding.

"It'll be welded," Tamsin had said, when he told her.

"It won't." He had been very sure. "It's a reactor hatch. You don't weld those. You'd need to get in, if anything went wrong. It'll be locked. With a Company lock. And I've read every Company lock manual in the archive in the last four days." He had smiled, a little wildly. "I've had trouble sleeping."

So at two in the morning on the twenty-third day, when the fifth hall was dark and the bailiffs' round had passed and would not come again for an hour, five people came up the counters' service stair in grey coats: Orrin in front, and Tamsin, and Dobbs, and two of Wenna's cutters with a sled and a winch folded flat between them, and a coil of tether, and a case of tools. The coats were Orrin's, and his mother's, and three he had taken from the counters' cloakroom on the second floor. Dobbs's did not close across his chest.

The hall was very quiet. The plaque inside the brass rail caught a little light from the night-lamps by the door. Tamsin walked past it without looking.

Orrin knelt by the seam with his case of tools open beside him and began on the lock. Tamsin knelt beside him and held the lamp. She could hear him breathing — fast, shallow, a counter's breathing — and see his hands, which were quite steady. She would remember that afterwards. That his hands were steady.

It took him eleven minutes. Then something under the plating went _clunk_, deep and heavy, and Orrin sat back on his heels and looked at her, and his face in the lamplight was white and astonished and very young.

"Dobbs," he said.

Dobbs put his fingers in the ring-pull and lifted, and the hatch came up out of the floor of the Count on hinges that screamed like an animal, and under it there was a shaft going down into the Hulk, and a ladder, and warm air coming up, smelling of oil and metal and something else, something faint and dry and hot, like a stove.

"That's it," said Tamsin. She did not know how she knew. "That's them. That's the fourteen."

"Counter Sallow," said Brother Lusk.

He was standing in the doorway of the fifth hall. He had a lamp in one hand and his docket-baton in the other, and behind him in the corridor there were bailiffs, a great many of them, more than Tamsin could count, in grey, with batons, and some of them with the short ugly slug-guns that the Count kept in a locker on the third floor and had not taken out of it, Orrin said later, in living memory.

"I did warn you," said Lusk, mildly. "About the galleries."

Afterwards Tamsin could never put it in order. Dobbs moved first. He came up off his knees between the hatch and the door like a wall going up, with the pry-bar in his hands, and roared — the only sound she ever heard him make — and went at the doorway; and the bailiffs came through it at him, and the hall was full of shouting and lamps swinging and the flat hard crack of a slug-gun, once, twice, very loud under the low roof. Somebody had her arm. It was Orrin. He was dragging her backward, away from the door, toward the far end of the hall where there was another door, the service door, the one they had come in by. She was fighting him. She wanted the hatch. It was _there_, open, a metre away, with the warm dry air coming up out of it—

"Go," Orrin said. "Go, go, there's too many, go—"

She saw Dobbs go down in the doorway with three bailiffs on him and his pry-bar spinning away across the floor. She saw one of Wenna's cutters run past her for the service door, and the other one not run. She saw Lusk walking toward them up the middle of the hall, not hurrying, with his baton held loosely at his side, the way a man walks toward a door he means to close.

Then she was through the service door and on the stair, and Orrin was not.

She turned. He was in the doorway behind her, with his back to her, facing into the hall, with his arms out on either side of him, holding the frame. He was not strong. He would not hold it for more than a moment. He was holding it anyway.

"Orrin—"

"Tell your mother," he said over his shoulder, quite clearly, "that I'm sorry about the arithmetic."

Then Lusk reached him.

* * *

They did not hurt him much. He had expected them to. He had sat in the bailiffs' room on the third floor for four hours with his wrists tied behind the chair and blood drying in his eyebrow from the one blow Lusk had given him in the doorway, almost absently, the way you would brush away a fly; and he had waited for the rest, and it had not come. At six in the morning they untied him and took him up to the long window.

Imre Vosk was standing at the glass with his hands behind his back, looking down the length of the world, at the Wick and the Galleries and the Cold. He did not turn round.

"Sit down, Counter."

Orrin stood.

"As you like." Vosk was silent for a while. Outside, the Wick stammered — once, twice, a long flicker that sent a shadow rippling down the whole length of the Galleries — and steadied. "It's getting worse. Ninian says weeks now. Not months. You know that, I imagine. You've been reading his reports."

"I've been reading everything."

"Yes. So I gather." Vosk turned. In the white light from the window his face was tired, and calm, and not unkind. "You've seen the reserve. You've seen the auxiliary lamp. You've seen the hatch at frame forty. And you think I'm a monster."

Orrin said nothing.

"I'm not. I'm a man doing sums." Vosk came and sat at his desk, and folded his hands on it. "The Founders saw this day coming, Counter. Three hundred years ago. They knew the Wick's fuel would run out. They knew there would be more people on the rock by then than any reserve could ever light. So they set aside fourteen cassettes and wrote _held_ against them and told every Comptroller-General after them, in a letter that I have read and you have not, that when the day came the reserve was to light the Count, and the Count was to go on keeping the Account. Not for its own sake. For Caul's. So that whatever happened to the rest, there would always be somebody keeping the books." He spread his hands. "That's not cruelty. That's a plan. It's the only plan there's ever been."

"There are thirty thousand people on Lantern."

"There are thirty thousand people on Lantern and fuel for four. You can light the Wick end to end with your fourteen, Counter, and everyone lives for fourteen years, and then everyone dies. Or you can light a small lamp for a small number, and some of them live for a very long time. That's the arithmetic." He tilted his head. "You were the most careful counter in the third hall. Do it yourself. You'll get the same answer."

Orrin had done it himself. On the second night, on his knees by the hatch, he had done it. He had got the same answer.

"And the Account?" he said. "_Holder, none._ Who are you keeping it for, Comptroller-General? Who's Caul?"

Vosk looked at him for a long moment.

"Caul is the reason people work," he said gently. "That's all it has ever been. A debt that can't be paid, owed to someone who can't be found, kept by people who can't be argued with. It's the finest machine the Founders ever built, finer than the Wick. It's kept thirty thousand people breathing in a rock in the dark for three hundred years. I don't need to know who Caul is, Counter. I only need everybody else to believe it's owed."

He stood up, then, and went to a cabinet in the wall behind the desk — a small brass-fronted thing, old, Company-made — and opened it with a key from his waistcoat pocket. Orrin could not see what was inside. A glint. Something round. Vosk did something to it, briefly, with his back turned, and closed the cabinet again, and locked it.

On the desk, the slate in front of Vosk's chair chimed once.

Orrin felt it before he understood it. A small cold weight on his wrist, where his chit hung on its cord. He looked down. The little screen had gone grey.

"Hask, Agathe, goes into the Cold at second shift," said Vosk. "With the third list. So do you. Released from the Tally's charge, and held quit." He sat down again. "I'm sorry, Orrin. I am. I'd have made you senior counter by thirty. But I can't have a man in the Count who has seen the reserve and doesn't understand what it's for. Brother Lusk will see you down."

* * *

They went at second shift.

Agathe Hask walked. Tamsin had found a chair with wheels, from somewhere, from Wenna, and her mother had looked at it and said, "I'm not being pushed into the dark in a chair, Tam," and got up off her bunk, and put on Tamsin's own winter coat because her own had been sold, and taken the biscuit tin with the farmhouse on its lid down from its shelf and put it under her arm, and walked. Down Twenty-Two to the ring road. Along the ring road past the Salt Docks to the Lip. It was a kilometre and a half. She stopped eleven times to cough. She did not sit down once.

Jory walked on her other side. He was fifteen, and a dependant, and the law said a dependant under sixteen went with the Called parent; he had not argued with it. He carried the bedding and a pan and a sack of ration bricks and his face was set like the face of a much older man.

Tamsin carried everything else. She was twenty-four, and not on any list, and the law said nothing at all about her, and she had not argued with that either.

The ring road was lined both sides. Not with bailiffs. With the Owing. All of Twenty-Two had come out, and Twenty-One and Twenty-Three, and the Salt Docks, and people from galleries Tamsin had never been to, standing along the road in silence as the third thousand came past. Nobody rang anything. Nobody sang. Old women touched Agathe's sleeve as she passed. Somebody put a twist of paper into Jory's hand with sugar in it. A man Tamsin did not know fell into step beside her for twenty paces and said, very low, "Wenna says the Salt's not done," and was gone.

At the Lip there was the table with the lamp on it, and Senior Counter Abney behind it with his slate, and his ink-stained cuffs. He touched Agathe's chit to the slate, and it chimed, and went grey, and he looked up at her and for a moment seemed about to say something; and did not; and looked down.

"Next," he said.

And beyond him, at the iron post where the light ended, Brother Lusk stood with his hands folded, nodding to each as they passed, like a priest at a door.

Beside Lusk, in the dusk, there was a man in a grey coat with the Count's silver torn off the collar and a cut over his eye. He was standing very straight, the way he had stood in the Hask room on the first day with his slate held against his chest, as if it might be taken off him; except that now there was nothing in his hands at all.

Tamsin stopped.

"Counter Sallow is released from the Tally's charge," said Lusk pleasantly, to nobody in particular. "He'll be going with you. I'm told you know each other."

Orrin looked at her. He did not say anything. His face was quite calm. Then he looked at Agathe, and something in his face broke, just a little, at one corner.

"Mrs. Hask," he said. "I'm sorry. I tried."

"I know you did, Counter." Agathe coughed, and wiped her mouth, and looked at him with her pale eyes. "Come on, then. You can carry the tin."

She held it out to him. After a moment he took it.

They walked past Lusk into the Cold together, the four of them: Agathe in front, very slow, and Jory with the bedding, and Orrin with the biscuit tin, and Tamsin last. She did not look at Lusk as she passed him. She felt him look at her. She thought he was counting.

* * *

The Count's provision for the Called was a pallet of ration bricks at the Lip, restocked when the bailiffs remembered, and the Cold.

Three thousand people were in it by now. The first thousand had found the old Pump Galleries, where the air was least thin, and the second thousand had found them, and the third came after, and there was nowhere else to go. They made fires of what would burn: old insulation, the dead wood of the strip-farm frames, furniture from the empty houses, doors. The smoke hung under the low roofs and stung the eyes. People slept in heaps, families together, under every blanket they had brought. Nobody had enough. In the first night forty people died, the old and the sick mostly, and in the second night more, and by the third morning the Called had stopped counting and had started carrying them, quietly, to a dead gallery further aft, and laying them in rows, under the frost.

Agathe Hask died on the second night.

She died in the dark, in a corner of Pump Gallery Twelve, with her back against the cold rock and the biscuit tin in her lap. She had not let go of it since Orrin gave it back to her at the Lip. Tamsin was with her. Jory was asleep, at last, with his head on Tamsin's knee. Orrin was sitting a little apart, against the opposite wall, keeping the fire.

It was not dramatic. Tamsin had thought it would be. She had dreaded it for two years, the way the lung-rot took people at the end, the drowning. It was not like that. Agathe coughed for a while, in the early part of the night, and then she stopped coughing, and lay quiet with her eyes open looking at the fire, and her breath went slower and slower and quieter and quieter, whistling a little, in and out, like a tap that someone has nearly turned off. Once she said something. Tamsin bent close.

"Their names," Agathe said.

"What, Mam?"

"Say their names. When I'm in the tin. Somebody's got to." Her hand moved on the lid of the tin, very slightly. "Nobody's going to cut them on anything."

"I'll say them," said Tamsin.

Her mother's breath went in and out a few more times after that. Then it went in and did not come out. Tamsin sat with her for a long time, with her hand on her mother's hand on the lid of the tin, and the fire crackling, and the frost falling sideways through the firelight from the roof of the gallery, very slowly, the way things fell on Lantern.

Then she got up, carefully, so as not to wake Jory, and went to the gallery wall where an old water main ran along it at shoulder height — iron, frost-furred, three hundred years dead — and picked up a length of pipe from the floor, and struck the main.

Once.

The sound went out through the Pump Galleries, flat and hard, the sound of the Hook. And out in the dark, from the other fires, from all through the Cold where the Called were sleeping or not sleeping, other sounds answered it: someone striking a rail, someone a pipe, someone a pan. Not many. A dozen. They rang once each, for Agathe Hask, whom most of them had never met, and then went quiet.

"Owing and owed," said Orrin, very low, from the other side of the fire.

Tamsin turned round and looked at him.

"No," she said. "Not any more. She doesn't owe anything any more. She's been released." She laughed, once, and it hurt. "Held quit. That's what they call it."

She came back and sat down by the fire, beside her sleeping brother and her dead mother, across from the counter.

"Orrin," she said.

"Yes."

"The keel-ways."

He looked at her blankly.

"Cutters' roads. In the rock. Under the Galleries, in the outer skin." She was speaking low and fast. "The Company cut them for the engineers, for the pipes and cables. They run the whole length of the rock, fore to aft. Most of them are blocked, or iced, or fallen in. Cutters use the aft ends to get into the deep Cold. Nobody goes fore in them. There's no reason to. Nothing up there but the Count." She looked at him. "They come out under the dock levels. Under the Counting House. Under the Hulk."

Orrin did not move.

"The hatch at frame forty," she said. "You opened it. It's a reactor hatch. A reactor's not just got a door at the top, has it? Not on a ship. There'll be one underneath. For the engineers. Into the keel."

"Yes," said Orrin slowly. "There'll be one underneath."

"And there's three thousand people in the Cold," said Tamsin, "with nothing left to lose. And seven thousand more coming. And they all know how to walk into the dark."

Across the fire, Orrin Sallow looked at her for a long time. Then he looked down at the biscuit tin in the dead woman's lap, with the farmhouse on its lid, and the chits inside it.

"Owing and owed," he said again, but differently this time, as if it were a question, or the beginning of a sum.

# Chapter 18: Lien

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

"Five hundred," said Haste.

They were in the wardroom, the morning before the _Good Title_ cleared Nhai's limb: Haste at the head of the table with his slate, Kell at his shoulder, Ode at the far end with her arms folded and Tol behind her, and the ship's medic, Petrosyan, standing by the door as though she would rather be anywhere else. Corvin sat halfway down the table. Nobody had asked him to sit there. Nobody had asked him to speak, either, and he had not.

"We clear the limb in eighteen hours," Haste said. "We'll be at the rock in thirty. Whatever we find there — people or no people — we'll need hands. Enumeration takes bodies. Consolidation takes more. Sergeant Kell's people are very good, but there are twelve of them. I want five hundred of the stock awake and fit for work by the time we dock."

"You can't," said Petrosyan.

"I'm sorry?"

"You can't wake five hundred in thirty hours. Not safely." The medic's voice was flat and hoarse, as if she had been arguing in her head for some time. "The revival bay's built for fifty at a time. Six-hour cycles. Five hundred is ten cycles, sixty hours, and that's with nothing going wrong. If you push it — if you thaw them in batches of a hundred on fifty beds — you'll lose some. A lot, maybe. You saw what happened to the woman on Rack Forty-One."

"What sort of number?" said Haste.

Petrosyan stared at him.

"Of losses," said Haste patiently. "If we thaw in batches of a hundred. Roughly."

"I don't— ten in a hundred. Fifteen. I don't know. Nobody does it that way. It's not done."

Haste made a note on his slate. "Fifteen percent of five hundred is seventy-five. Out of three thousand and eleven. That's within tolerance for a recovery of this kind." He looked up. "Begin the first batch at noon, please."

"No," said Mabry Ode.

She had not moved. She stood at the end of the table with her arms folded and her face like a shut hatch, the way she had stood at the ship's lock at Ambry on the first day.

"Captain?"

"I said no. That's my medic, and that's my revival bay, and that's my hold, and those are people in it, not stock. Nobody's thawing anybody in batches of a hundred on my ship to save you a day's work at the other end." She unfolded her arms. "Tol's locked the hold power board. Master's codes only. If you want five hundred awake, Mr. Haste, you can wait till we dock and wake them fifty at a time like a decent man, and you can write in your book that the master wouldn't have it. That's my answer."

There was a short silence. Then Haste set his slate down on the table and smiled, quite kindly, the way he had smiled at Corvin in Hold 2.

"Captain Ode," he said. "I've been very patient."

"So've I."

"Yes. And I respect it. But this ship is mortgaged to Brightwater Recovery under an instrument that provides for possession on the master's default, and the master's refusal of a lawful order from the lienholder's agent in the course of a recovery voyage is a default. You know the law, I think. You've had it explained to you."

"By people like him," said Ode, and looked down the table at Corvin.

Corvin looked at his hands.

"Give Sergeant Kell the master's codes, please," said Haste.

"Go to hell."

Haste sighed. He did not look at Kell. He did not need to.

* * *

It happened on the bridge, an hour later, because Ode went there.

She walked out of the wardroom without another word and up the companionway to the bridge and sat down in the master's chair with her boots on the console, and told Dacey and the navigator to go below, and they would not go; and she was still sitting there, with Tol beside her at the engineer's station with his hand flat on the lock of the hold power board, when Kell came up the companionway with six of his people.

Corvin came up behind them. He did not know why. He told himself afterwards that he had come to stop it, and knew that was not true; he had come because he could not have stayed below and listened.

"Captain," said Kell. He was perfectly civil. "Mr. Haste asks for the master's codes."

"Mr. Haste can ask."

"I'd rather not take them, ma'am."

"Then don't."

Kell nodded slowly, as though she had confirmed something. Then he made a small movement with two fingers, and two of his people went past him to Tol at the engineer's station, and took him by the arms, and lifted him out of his seat — Tol did not fight; he went limp, like a sack, and they had to carry him — and pinned him against the bulkhead. A third went to the console beside him and began, with a slate and a cable, to work at the lock.

Ode stood up.

She did not shout. She simply stood up out of the master's chair and stepped across the bridge toward the woman at the lock, a gaunt grey old woman in a colourless coverall with nothing in her hands — and one of Kell's people, a young man, not much older than Dacey, who had been standing at the companionway with his slug-gun held across his chest the way they had been drilled, saw the master of the ship move fast toward a bailiff at the hold controls, and did what he had been drilled to do.

It was one shot. It was very loud on the small bridge. Mabry Ode stopped, and looked down at herself with an expression of annoyance, as if she had spilled something on her coverall; and then she sat down, quite carefully, on the deck, with her back against the base of the master's chair, and put her hand over the place.

"Oh, you stupid boy," she said, not to anyone in particular.

Then she said, "Tol."

Tol had stopped being limp. He was fighting the two who held him, silently, savagely, with his teeth bared, and getting nowhere.

"Tol," said Ode again. "Watch the Pellan scar. She grumbles on it. Watch it."

"I'll watch it," said Tol.

"Good lad." She leaned her head back against the chair. Her face had gone very grey. She looked up at the main screen, where Nhai's great banded limb filled half the view, ochre and purple and the hard bright line of the ring, and beyond the limb the black. "Thirty-four years," she said. "And I never once saw what was on the other side of a throat before anybody else did. Always somebody first. Always." She closed her eyes. "Damn."

She died there, against her own chair, before Petrosyan could get up the companionway. Nobody on the bridge moved. The young bailiff who had fired stood with his gun still raised, staring at her, and then slowly lowered it. Kell went and took it out of his hands, gently, the way you would take something from a child.

Then he turned to the woman at the lock. "Carry on," he said.

Corvin stood at the top of the companionway. He found he had his hand on the rail and could not take it off. Behind him, on the steps, Quennell Haste had come up without anyone hearing, in his dark coat, and stood looking at the master of the _Good Title_ dead on her own deck with an expression of grave, sincere regret.

"How very unfortunate," he said. "Sergeant, please log the master's death as a consequence of her resistance to lawful possession. Mr. Saal—" he turned— "a word, please. In my cabin."

* * *

There were three documents on Haste's desk. He laid them out side by side, squaring their edges with his fingertips, and turned them round to face Corvin.

"The first," he said, "is a declaration of default. The master of the _Good Title_ refused a lawful order of the lienholder's agent and resisted possession with force. That constitutes default under the ship's mortgage. Brightwater takes possession of the vessel. The second is the consolidation order: it authorises the enumeration of any persons found at the Sabra Concern's habitat and the merging of their accounts into Brightwater's book under the instrument. The third is the bell. The seizure docket, which you were good enough to concur with verbally the other night. I'd like your mark on it now, for the record."

"And if I don't?"

"Then none of them is valid," said Haste. "That's the point of an adjuster, Mr. Saal. A partner can order. Only an adjuster can certify that the order was lawful. Without your mark, the master's death is a killing. With it, it's a regrettable consequence of a default." He folded his hands. "You see why I brought you."

Corvin looked at the three slates.

"She wasn't armed," he said. "She didn't threaten anybody. She stood up."

"She moved toward a bailiff at a control panel during a lawful possession, in a manner the bailiff reasonably perceived as a threat." Haste said it gently, as if helping a slow child with a lesson. "You've certified that sentence before, I think. Or ones very like it. On Malory's Reach, if I remember the file."

Corvin did not answer.

"I'm not threatening you," Haste said. "I want to be very clear. You're not in any danger. Your discharge is in that drawer. Sign these and it's yours the day we're back through the Throat. Thirty-five years, Mr. Saal. Done." He paused. "Miss Adaire, on the other hand."

Corvin looked up.

"Her family's freedom from bond," Haste said, "dates from the payment of a debt by her great-grandmother, with the proceeds of a painting. I've had our people look into it, on the packet out. It's a little irregular. The painting, it seems, may not have been entirely hers to sell. If that were so — and a court might well find that it was so — the discharge would be void, and the original debt revived. With interest. Attaching to the issue." He let that sit. "I'd hate to see that. She's a remarkable young woman. But a consolidation is a consolidation. One reads everything across."

The cabin was very quiet. Through the bulkhead, faint, Corvin could hear the deep steady hum of the ship's great engine, and under it, from somewhere aft, the other note, lower, steady as breath, that he had heard on the first night out of the Throat, and had known since Hold 2 for three thousand people sleeping in the cold.

He picked up the stylus.

He signed the first slate. _Default certified. C. Saal, adjuster._ He signed the second. _Consolidation authorised._ He signed the third, under Haste's _Adjuster concurs (verbal)_, his full name and number. His hand was perfectly steady. He had signed four hundred and twelve settlements' worth of slates with that hand, and it had never once shaken, and it did not shake now.

"Thank you," said Haste, and gathered them up.

When Corvin came out of the cabin, Fen was standing in the corridor.

He did not know how long she had been there. Long enough. She had been confined to her quarters since the seizure, and someone had let her out, or she had let herself out, and she was standing against the opposite wall with her arms wrapped round herself as if she were cold, and she had heard. He could see that she had heard all of it.

"Fen—"

She did not say anything. She looked at him. Then she turned her head, very deliberately, and spat on the deck at his feet, the way Mabry Ode had spat into the gap between the ship and the dock at Ambry on the first day. And then she walked away down the corridor, and did not look back.

* * *

He went to the bell that night because there was nowhere else he could bear to be.

Hold 1 was dark and cold. Kell's crate-corridors were still stacked across it from the drills, so that the bay was a maze of grey walls in the light of his lamp. The bell stood at the end of one of them, on its sled, strapped down under a tarpaulin with a Brightwater seal on it. He had an adjuster's access now. He had signed for it. The seal opened to his chit.

He folded back the tarpaulin and sat down on a crate beside the bell, with the lamp on the floor between his feet throwing its light up onto the bronze.

He had never been alone with it. It had always been Fen's: in its crate at Ambry, in the blister, under her hand, with her loupe and her lamp. He had only ever looked at it over her shoulder. Now he looked at it on his own. The brown furred surface, every inch of it letters. The worn old names round the lip — the crossing, thirty-one of them, in one careful hand. The colony's dead going up the waist, generation after generation, the hands changing. The dense grey band round the founders' belt, cramped and hurried, nine hundred and four of them, Abara to Zeller, that Fen had read aloud to him in the dark. The bright names high on the shoulder, the last ones, the newest. _Lemuel Adeyemi. Bronwen Price. Joss Teague._ He did not know who any of them were. He had read them so often he felt he should.

He lifted the lamp and looked at the crown.

He had never looked at the crown. Nobody did; it was the top of the bell, where the casting's decoration was thickest, a ring of raised bronze leaves round the shank where the hook had gone. Fen had said once that there were names up there too, crowded in among the leaves, and he had taken her word for it. He looked now. There were names, small, worn, squeezed between the leaves. And on one leaf, the largest, low on the crown's slope — cut very small, in a hand that was not like any of the others, a hand that shook, so that the letters wandered off their line and came back to it — there were words.

He had to put his face very close, with the lamp held up beside it, to read them.

THESE ARE OWED. SAY THEIR NAMES.

He sat back on the crate.

For a long time he did not do anything at all. He sat in the cold dark bay among the grey crates with the lamp on the floor and the bell in front of him, and the hum of the engine through the deck, and under it the other hum, the low note of the hold, three thousand and eleven people asleep with their names on cards. He thought about a dead man in a chair at the throat-watch, with his name on a strip. ROACH. He thought about Marisol Tey asking whether it attached. He thought about Mabry Ode against the base of her chair saying _damn_. He thought about a water-office on Kessane and a man whose face he could not remember signing his name in a shaking hand. He thought about a mother on Malory's Reach asking him whether he would like tea.

He thought about four hundred and twelve settlements, and that he could not, if his life depended on it, say the name of a single person on any of them.

_These are owed. Say their names._

He leaned forward and put the lamp close against the lip of the bell, where the oldest names were, the thirty-one of the crossing. They were so worn he could hardly make them out. He made them out anyway, one letter at a time, with his finger on the bronze the way Fen did it.

"Abel Corrigan," he said aloud. His voice sounded strange in the empty hold. "Mary Corrigan. Agnes Weil."

He went on. It was very slow. Some of the names he could not read at all, and he said what he could of them, a letter, a syllable, and went on to the next. The lamp grew hot in his hand. The hold was cold. On the bridge above him, he supposed, someone was flying the ship who was not Mabry Ode, and in the hold behind him the bailiffs were beginning, a hundred at a time on fifty beds, to wake the stock; and in eighteen hours the _Good Title_ would clear the limb of the great banded planet and come round into the light, and see whatever there was to see.

He did not think about any of that. He sat in the dark on a crate in a hold full of a dead company's property, with his hand on the bell, and read the dead aloud, one by one, to nobody, and did not stop.

# Chapter 19: Quit-Claim

*The Closing · 2387 CE*

Teodor Basu was dying in the Company infirmary on the second floor of the Counting House, because there was nowhere else to take him and nobody left who could do anything about it.

The Company's doctors had gone on the _Covenant_. So had its nurses, and its drugs, and its good beds. What was left was a long room with a row of empty cots and a woman from the Gallery Three clinic called Mrs. Ansah, who had come up the spine-road on her own when she heard there was a man shot in the Counting House, and who had done what she could, which was to pack the wound and give him the last of the Company's morphine and sit with him. When Varga brought Dagny in, at four in the morning on the forty-ninth day, Mrs. Ansah looked up at them and shook her head, once, and got up and went to the far end of the room to give them the bed.

He was very small in it. His glasses were on the cabinet beside him, folded, the cracked lens catching the light. His face was the colour of the sheet.

"Ms. Ostrova," he said. His voice was a whisper. "They let you go."

"He let me go." Dagny sat down on the edge of the cot. "He didn't get on the ship."

Teodor turned his head a little on the pillow, and looked at Varga, standing at the foot of the bed with his big hands at his sides. Something passed between the two men that Dagny could not read.

"No," Teodor said. "I didn't think he would." He closed his eyes for a moment. "Pim?"

"Gone. With Kas. On the _Covenant_."

"I'm sorry."

"I sent him."

Teodor opened his eyes. He looked at her for a long time.

"Then I'm sorrier," he said. He moved his hand on the sheet, feebly, toward the cabinet. "My glasses. Would you."

She picked them up. She was going to put them on his face for him, and he shook his head, very slightly, and she stopped.

"The frame," he said. "The left arm. Where it goes over the ear. It unscrews."

She looked at him. Then she looked at the glasses. She turned them in her fingers. The left arm of the frame was thicker than the right, a little, at the end — she had never noticed; nobody would have — and when she twisted the tip it turned, stiffly, and came away in her hand, and inside it, rolled very small, there was a slip of something pale and thin, like a fingernail. Ceramic. Like the key from his shoe. Not like it. Narrower, and darker, and the marks burned into it were different.

"I lied to you," said Teodor Basu. "On the Wick. I said there was no back door. There's one."

Dagny held it on her palm and did not breathe.

"I made it the first year," he said. "When I built her. I was twenty-six and very proud and I'd just been shown what the Company wanted her to do. And I was afraid. Not of the Company. Of her. Of what she'd be able to do to people when I'd finished, and that there'd be nothing anyone could do to stop her. So I made one thing she'd obey that nobody else knew about. Not the Director. Not the board. Not the Tally's own registry. It doesn't open the root the way the paired keys do. It can't change the line, or the rates, or anything else. It does one thing, once, and then it's spent." He swallowed. "It's a quit-claim."

"What does it do?"

"It changes who is owed." He shut his eyes again. "Listen. Please. I haven't— there isn't much time. You can't free them. I told you that and it was true. Every valve asks the Tally whose air it is, and the Tally answers by looking at who owes it and who is owed. Every account has to have a creditor. That's her heart. That's the thing I built her round. An account with no creditor is an account she can't answer for, and if she can't answer for it the valve fails closed. If you took the Company off every account on Lantern and left nothing in its place, every door and every scrubber on the rock would shut in the same hour." He breathed. "So you can't make them owe nobody. But with that—" his eyes moved to her palm— "you can make them owe somebody else. Anybody. Once. And the Company's claim is quit. Gone. The trust's empty. Whatever comes through the Throat in six hundred years with Halden-Ash's books in its hand will find that Halden-Ash isn't owed a single credit on Lantern. Not one."

Dagny closed her hand on it.

"Who?" she said. "Who do I make them owe?"

"I've thought about that every night for twenty-six years." Teodor almost smiled. "I never had an answer. Anyone living could sell it. Anyone living could die and leave it to somebody worse. Any company could fail and be bought. Any person could be bought." His voice was going. "It has to be somebody who can never collect, Ms. Ostrova. Somebody who can never sell. Somebody nobody can ever buy."

She sat very still on the edge of his cot. She thought of a port of armoured glass at the bottom of a shaft on Caul, and a woman behind it with her palm raised. She thought of the founders' band, and a boy on a box with an old woman's hand on his.

"Yes," said Teodor Basu, watching her face. "Yes. I thought you might."

"Will it hold?"

"It will hold as long as she runs." He moved his hand again, and found hers, and held it, very weakly. "It wants a voice. At the root. The grille. Say who, and say what discharges it. She'll want to know what discharges it — every debt has to have a way to end, even if nobody ever takes it. I built her honest, at least. I'll say that for myself." His fingers loosened. "The third door. It opens the third door too. It was made to."

"Teodor."

"Mm."

"The nine hundred and four. The letters." She did not know what she wanted to say. "Odile cut them. All of them. On the bell. Your list."

He did not answer for a long time. She thought he had gone. Then his lips moved.

"Good," he said. "That's good."

He died a little before five, with Mrs. Ansah on one side of him and Dagny on the other and Varga at the foot of the bed. He did not say anything else. At the end his hand tightened once on Dagny's, and then let go.

* * *

Varga took her down.

The first door opened to his Security override. So did the second; he had set the overrides himself, he said, eleven years ago, and nobody had ever thought to take them back. The corridor beyond was the same cold blue it had been the morning before. Somebody had washed the floor. There was still a dark stain on the rock by the second door where Ezra Hesketh had lain, that the washing had not got out.

The third door was shut. It was a plain steel slab with no handle and a slot at chest height. Dagny looked at the slip of ceramic on her palm for a moment, and then put it in the slot.

The door opened.

The root was as she had left it. The low dome. The pedestal. The desk of dark glass and brass with its two empty slots, side by side, and the little grille beside them like the grille of a confessional. Nothing else. She stood in front of it with Teodor's quit-claim in her fingers and could not, for a moment, remember why she was there.

"There," said Varga quietly, behind her, and pointed.

Below the two slots, where she had not noticed it, flush with the brass, there was a third. Very narrow. You would not see it unless you knew.

She put the slip in it.

The console woke. Not as it had for the Director — there was no chime, no soft brightening of the glass. It simply went from dark to lit, and on its face, in plain white letters, the Tally said:

QUIT-CLAIM PRESENTED. ONE USE. STATE THE ASSIGNEE.

Dagny bent to the grille.

She had thought about what she would say all the way down the stair. She had thought about it in the infirmary with Teodor's hand going cold in hers. She had not found any words for it that were good enough, and in the end she said the plainest ones she had.

"All balances on the Tally," she said. "Every account. Every person on Lantern, living and to come. Assigned to the dead of Caul Deep. The nine hundred and four. They're owed it. All of it."

The glass was still for a moment. Then:

ASSIGNEE: THE DEAD OF CAUL DEEP. ACCOUNT HOLDER?

"None," said Dagny. "Nobody holds it. Nobody can draw on it. Nobody can sell it. It's owed to them, and they're dead, and that's all."

ACCOUNT HOLDER: NONE. CREDITOR TO BE ENTERED AS?

She thought for a moment. "Caul," she said.

CREDITOR: CAUL. STATE TERMS OF DISCHARGE.

_She'll want to know what discharges it. Every debt has to have a way to end, even if nobody ever takes it._

Dagny straightened up and stood in front of the console with her hands at her sides.

She thought of the bell. She had seen it last at two in the morning, two days ago, going into the _Magpie_'s tank-frame in its nest of ice-sacking, between the empty tanks — and it was there still, at the Dock Ring berth, because the _Magpie_ was there still, because the man who was to have flown it had sold his seat and gone. All night in the cell she had thought of it there. Nine hundred and four names round its waist that existed nowhere else now; Varga's men had taken Teodor's folder off Odile's bench on the morning of the root and burned it, Varga had told her so on the stair, not looking at her; the Tally itself forgot a closed account in ninety days, which was why every family on Lantern kept its dead's chits in a tin. Nine hundred and four names on a founders' band, and an old woman's shaking words on the crown above them, that Dagny had read by torchlight when she cut the shackle. _These are owed. Say their names._

"When their names are spoken," she said. "Every one of them. All nine hundred and four, to the Tally, here, at the root, in order. When somebody stands here and says every one of their names, the debt is done. Not before. Not for anything else. Not for money, not for work, not for anybody's seal or key. Their names."

There was a long pause. She had time to think that the Tally would refuse — that a machine built to count credits would not know what to do with a debt paid in names.

CREDITOR NAMES NOT ON FILE, said the console.

"They're on the bell," said Dagny.

Another pause. Then:

TERMS ACCEPTED. DISCHARGE: CREDITOR NAMES, SPOKEN, IN FULL, IN ORDER, AT ROOT. PRIOR CREDITOR'S CLAIM QUIT. OUTSIDE READING KEYS RENEWED. COMPANY RECEIVERS VOID.

And then, at the top of the glass, in the plain white letters:

ACCOUNT ESTABLISHED. CREDITOR: CAUL. ALL BALANCES ON THE TALLY ASSIGNED TO CREDITOR IN FULL. ACCOUNT HOLDER: NONE.

The console went dark. The slip of ceramic slid out of the narrow slot by itself, and lay on the brass, and Dagny picked it up and saw that the marks burned into it had gone grey, like a dimmed chit.

She stood there for a long time.

"Is that it?" said Varga at last. "Is it done?"

"The Company's claim is quit," said Dagny. She heard her own voice as if from a long way off. "Nobody on Lantern owes Halden-Ash a credit. Not one. Not ever. Whoever comes through the Throat with their books, the books are empty." She turned the dead slip in her fingers. "We still owe. Every hour of air. The line's still there. The locks still go to recovery. I couldn't touch any of that. He said I couldn't." She closed her hand on it. "But we owe _them_. Not the Company. Them."

Varga looked at the dark console. He did not say anything for a while. Then he said, in a voice she had not heard him use: "I poured the seal on eleven of them."

"I know."

"Then I'm in debt to them too."

"Everybody is," said Dagny. "Now." She put the dead quit-claim in her pocket. "Mr. Varga. Can you fly a tug?"

* * *
