The _Magpie_ went out from the Dock Ring at seven in the morning on the forty-ninth day, with a Company security chief at her controls and a Wick-tender in the second seat and the bell of the _Ardent Mercy_ strapped down in her tank-frame between the empty tanks, because there had not been time to take it out, and because Dagny had looked at it in its nest of ice-sacking when they boarded and found that she could not bear to leave it behind on an empty berth.

Varga flew her hard. He had flown landing craft in the Concord marines and he flew the _Magpie_ the same way, as if she were something he meant to break and did not much care when. Three days at full burn. The _Covenant_ had a day's start and four times their mass and she was not hurrying; she was a carrier, built to move fourteen hundred people and their cargo through a throat without spilling any of them, and she would be crawling the last of the approach, lining up on the mouth. A tug with empty tanks and nothing in her but two people and a bell could catch her. Just.

They did not talk much. There was nothing to say that would make the burn shorter. Varga slept in the pilot's seat in snatches of an hour, with his hands on the controls. Dagny slept in the second seat and did not sleep. On the second night, with Lantern a small grey grain far behind them against Nhai and the Throat not yet in sight, she said into the dark of the little cabin: "Why didn't you get on?"

He was quiet so long she thought he had not heard.

"I stood at the foot of the ramp," he said at last. "With my bag. The Director went up past me and said, _Coming, Hale?_ And I said yes. And I went to put my foot on the ramp, and I couldn't." He shifted in the seat. "I kept thinking about the ladder at Caul. Three hundred metres. You can hear a long way in a shaft. I thought, if I go up this ramp, I'm going up that ladder again. For the rest of my life. And I thought I'd rather stay down here with them."

Dagny did not say anything.

"You'll want to know what I'll do when we catch her," Varga said.

"Yes."

"I don't know. The men on her were my men. Some of them." He was silent again. "I know the codes for her docking collar. I know the way to her bridge. I know which of them will look the other way if I come up the companionway, and which won't. That's what I know." He turned his head in the dark. "What will _you_ do, Ostrova?"

"Get my son."

"And the shear?"

She had thought about that for two days and nights in the second seat with the bell behind her in the dark of the tank-frame. She had thought about inspectors coming through a throat with a commission, and a receiver, and a lien-house on Kessane, and the Tannet Yards ruling, and a different landlord. She had thought about the root, and the white words on the glass. _Prior creditor's claim quit._ Whatever came through the Throat would find the books empty now. Lantern owed the Company nothing. It owed the dead.

She had thought about the ice at Caul, and the faces in it, and that if the Throat closed nobody in the Concord would ever know their names.

"I don't know," she said.

* * *

They came up on the _Covenant_ at the mouth of the Throat on the afternoon of the fifty-second day.

Dagny had seen the Throat in pictures all her life. Every child on Lantern had; it was in the schoolbooks, a smear of wrongness in the sky with a pink heart. It was not a picture. It filled the whole of the _Magpie_'s cracked forward window — a vast, slow, swimming lens of broken starlight, thousands of kilometres across, its edges rippling, opening and closing, and at its centre a deep hot bruise of red that pulsed like a heart. It was breathing. Teodor's numbers had said so. She could see it breathe.

And against it, small, black, a long blunt shape with a blue flank and a great dark drive-bell at her stern, slowing, turning, lining up on the heart: the _Covenant_.

"She's two hours from transit," said Varga. "Maybe less." He was looking at the scope. "And she's armed it."

"How do you know?"

"Bay four's open. Aft of the drive." He put his finger on the screen. "That's the launch rail. She'll put it out ahead of her into the mouth, on a slow track, and go in after it. It goes off when she's through." His face was grey in the screen-light. "Then the lips fold."

He brought the _Magpie_ in under the carrier's flank, close, so close that Dagny could see the rivets in the blue plating going past above the cracked window, and the Company's leaning letters painted ten metres high on the hull, and then the docking collar, a ring of steel on the _Covenant_'s belly with a lamp beside it. Varga touched the comm and said a string of numbers and letters into it, very calmly, and the lamp beside the collar went from red to green.

"They'll know," Dagny said.

"They'll know someone's docked. They won't know who till they look." He unstrapped. "Stay close behind me. If anyone points anything at you, get down. If I tell you to run, run."

They went up through the collar into the _Covenant_.

* * *

Afterwards, Dagny would remember the _Covenant_ as a place of corridors. Long, wide, clean, lit, full of people in Company clothes who stopped and stared as a big scarred man in Security grey went past them at a run with a woman in a Wick-tender's rig behind him. Nobody stopped them. Twice a man in Security armour stepped out of a side passage and saw Varga's face and stepped back in again. Once a young officer with a pistol on his hip said _Chief—_ and Varga said _Not now, Corrie_ without breaking stride, and Dagny turned her head as she passed and saw the young man's face, and knew it — Gallery Eleven, twenty-two years old — and did not stop.

The bridge was at the top of the ship, a long curved room with the Throat filling the whole of its forward wall. Ruth Achterberg was standing in the middle of it, with her officers round her, and the seal at her throat.

She turned when the door opened. She looked at Varga for a long moment. Then she looked at Dagny.

"Ms. Ostrova," she said. "Hale. I did wonder."

"Stand away from the board, Director," said Varga.

"I don't think so."

"Ruth." He said it quite gently. Dagny had never heard him use her first name. "It's over. Turn her round."

"It's not over. It's two hours from over." Achterberg did not move. Her hands were folded in front of her. "The device is on the rail. It goes into the mouth in ninety minutes, on a slow track, and we follow it through. That's the plan. That's been the plan since spring. I'm sorry you've come all this way to watch it."

"You don't have to fire it," said Dagny.

"I don't have to do anything. I am going to." The Director's face did not change. "Did you think there was some other end to this? The inspectors come through, and Caul is found, and the Company falls, and every one of these four hundred and six people loses everything. Their posts, their pensions, their names. And you lose nothing at all, because you have nothing. You'll just be sold to someone else." She shook her head. "No. I'll close the door. It's the only thing left that's mine to do."

"I've quit your claim," said Dagny.

That stopped her. For the first time, something moved in Ruth Achterberg's face.

"At the root," said Dagny. "Three mornings ago. With a key you didn't know existed. Teodor's. He made it the year he built her." She watched the Director's eyes. "Lantern doesn't owe the Company anything. Not a credit. Not one. Go through. Go to the Concord with your books. They're empty."

There was a long silence on the bridge. Somewhere an alarm was chiming softly, over and over, and nobody had turned it off.

"Teodor," said Ruth Achterberg, at last, very quietly.

"He's dead. Your man shot him at the root."

The Director closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them she did not look at Dagny. She looked at the Throat filling the forward wall, its red heart pulsing.

"Then there's nothing to go back for," she said. "And nothing to go through for." Her hand moved, very slightly, at her side. "Mr. Ferris. Launch now, please."

"Director, we're not aligned for transit, we'd be—"

"I know what we'd be. Launch."

Varga moved. He was across the bridge in three strides, and he had the officer at the launch board by the collar and was throwing him bodily away from it, and Dagny was moving too, toward the Director, toward the seal — and it did not matter, because the officer had already pressed it. Dagny felt it through the deck: a long low shudder from somewhere aft, and then a lurch, as something massive left the ship.

On the forward screen, small, dark, a shape slid out ahead of them into the swimming light of the Throat, on a slow straight track, toward its heart.

"How long?" said Varga.

"Ten minutes to the mouth," said the officer from the floor, white. "Then it— then it goes."

"Turn us round," said Varga. "Full burn. Away. Now."

Nobody moved.

"_Now_," Varga roared, and the helm officer turned them.

Dagny did not wait to see. She had the seal. She did not remember taking it. Ruth Achterberg had not fought her; she had stood quite still while Dagny broke the chain from her neck, and then sat down in the nearest chair and folded her hands in her lap and looked at the Throat. Dagny had the brass disk in her fist and she was running, out of the bridge, along the corridor, toward the passenger decks, toward the long cabins aft where the essential staff and their families had been put for the voyage. Somebody had told her, she did not know who. _Aft. Deck four. The hauler and the boy._

She found Pim first. He was in the corridor of deck four, outside a cabin door, alone, standing very straight with his school jacket buttoned and the tin wings on his collar, as if he had known she would come and had been waiting. He saw her and did not run to her. He said, quite calmly:

"He's gone to the _Magpie_."

She stared at him.

"He saw on the screen. When you docked. He saw the _Magpie_ on the collar, and he said, _She's brought the bell_. And then he went." Pim's chin was trembling, very slightly, and he was not letting it. "He said to tell you it's the plan. He said, _Tell her that was always the plan._"

* * *

She reached the docking collar as the _Magpie_ broke from it.

She saw it through the collar's port: the ugly little tug sliding away from the _Covenant_'s blue flank, her crane-arm folded, her cracked forward window catching the red light of the Throat, her drive lighting — a long white stroke of flame, far too much, everything she had — and turning, not away from the Throat as the _Covenant_ was turning, but toward it. Toward the heart. After the small dark shape on its slow straight track.

Dagny hit the comm on the collar wall so hard she split her palm.

"Kas. _Kas._"

The comm hissed. Then her brother's voice, very close, as if he were in the collar beside her, light and easy, the voice from when he was five and had broken something.

"Hey, Dag. You came."

"Kas, turn back. Turn round. Bring it back—"

"Can't, Dag. It's the plan. Your plan. Out in the wake, with the bell, before the door shuts." He laughed, a little breathlessly; she could hear the drive howling under his voice. "No wake, though. Bit of a problem. She's turning. Never mind. The _Magpie_'s done worse. Haven't you, girl?"

"Kas, listen to me, the names have to stay, I've— at the root, I've— Kas, the names are the only—"

"I know, I know. The names get out." The static was rising. "Somebody has to listen. Somebody in the Concord. That's what you said, in the Slake. I heard you." A pause, full of noise. "I'm sorry about Hesketh, Dag. And the girl. And Basu. I'm sorry about all of it. I'm sorry I sold you. I thought I was getting him out. He wouldn't go, you know. Sat in that cabin with his coat buttoned. Said he'd wait." The laugh again, cracking. "Takes after you."

"_Kas_—"

"Tell him to look after you," said her brother. "Tell him he's got my wings."

The small dark shape on its slow straight track reached the heart of the Throat.

She saw it go. A point of white, very small, at the centre of the red. Then the red was not red, and the lens was not a lens; it was folding. The vast slow swimming edges of the Throat came in toward their centre from every side at once, like a curtain drawn, like a hand closing, silent, enormous, unhurried, and the stars on the far side of it smeared and stretched and went out. And small, black, against the closing light, the _Magpie_ went on into it at full burn, a long white stroke of flame driving into the heart of the fold, smaller and smaller, until the fold closed over her and there was nothing there at all. Only the dark, and the ordinary stars, and a thin bright line drawn across the sky where the Throat had been, slowly spreading, slowly dimming.

The comm hissed. It went on hissing.

Then the backwash hit the _Covenant_.

Dagny had been told, afterwards, what it was — Varga told her, in the long weeks after, in the infirmary, when he came to sit with her; that a collapsing throat throws out a shock of hard light and broken space as it folds, and that a ship too near the mouth takes it across her hull like a wave. She did not know it then. She knew only that the deck came up and hit her, and the collar port went white, a white so complete and so hard that she felt it on her face like heat, like the Wick at the axis, and went on feeling it after the light had gone; and that somewhere aft of her the ship screamed, a long tearing shriek of metal, as the cargo hold where the Company's fuel and the Company's harvests and the Company's furniture had been packed for the voyage opened to the sky along forty metres of seam and emptied itself into the dark.

Then it was quiet. The alarms were going everywhere, but it was quiet.

She lay on the deck of the collar with the seal of the Halden-Ash Company in one fist and her split palm bleeding into the other, and her face burning, and looked at the port, where there was only black now, and stars, and a line of light fading across them like a scar.

After a while somebody was holding her. It was Pim. He had followed her down. He was kneeling on the deck beside her with his arms round her shoulders and his face against her hair, and he was saying something over and over, very quietly, and it took her a long time to understand that it was names.

* * *

The _Covenant_ came home on her own reactor, eleven days later, with her hold torn open and her drive half dead and four hundred and six essential staff of the Halden-Ash Company aboard her who had nowhere else on earth or off it to go.

She could not dock at the fore lock, where she had lain for a year before the Closing. Her flank was too badly torn, and her drive too weak to hold her steady. So Varga brought her in at the old cargo dock under the Counting House, the deep one, cut into the rock beneath the vault for the Company's first freighters ninety years before, and nobody had used for thirty; and she went into it nose first and stopped there, and never came out again. There was nowhere for her to go. Within a month the Wick crews had run lines into her for her reactor's heat. Within a year, people had begun to call her simply the ship, and then something else.

Dagny was carried off her on a stretcher. She had known on the fourth day what the white light at the collar port had done. Mrs. Ansah from Gallery Three knew too, though she did not say it; she sat with her in the Company infirmary in the Counting House, in the same bed Teodor had died in, and gave her what there was for the pain, which was not much, and did not lie.

The Director came to see her on the last day.

Ruth Achterberg had come off the _Covenant_ on her own feet, and walked up the cargo dock past the people of Lantern who had come down to see the Company come home, and none of them had touched her. She had gone up to the long window on the top floor of the Counting House, Varga said, and stood at it for a whole day and a night, looking down the length of the world. Then she had come down and begun to work. The Tally needed keeping. The new keys needed learning. Somebody had to sit at the root, and see to the postings, and answer the valves when they asked, and there was nobody left on Lantern who knew how, except the four hundred and six.

She stood at the foot of the bed. She looked older. She did not have the seal.

"I've come for it," she said. "If you'll let me have it."

Dagny looked at her for a long time from the pillow.

"Somebody has to keep the books," said Ruth Achterberg. "Or the air stops. You know that. I can't promise you anything else. I can promise you that."

On the cabinet beside the bed, beside Teodor's glasses with the cracked lens, lay the brass disk of the seal on its broken chain. Dagny moved her hand, slowly — it was hard to move anything now — and pushed it across the cabinet toward the Director.

"Keep them honest," she said. Her voice was a thread. "Every hour of air. Owed to them. Not to you. Not to anybody. To them."

"To them," said Ruth Achterberg. She picked up the seal. She stood holding it, looking down at it in her palm, for a long time. "And the names? The discharge? The Tally will want them."

"The names are on the bell," said Dagny. "The bell's gone through the Throat."

"Then the account can never be closed."

"Not till it comes home," said Dagny. "Six hundred years. Seven. Then somebody will bring it back, maybe. And say their names. And then we'll be done." She closed her eyes. "Until then it's owed. That's all. It's owed."

* * *

She made them take her to the Dock Ring that evening. Varga carried her, because there was no one else she would let, the whole way down the spine-road and along the ring, with Pim walking beside them and Odile Marchbank coming behind on Grace Mbeki's arm. People came out of the stalls and the gantries and the berths to see them pass, and stood still, as they stood for the bell.

The Hook was where it had always been, above the inner lock. Black iron. Empty. The stub of the cut shackle still hanging from it, where she had put the torch to it seventeen days before.

Varga set her down on the bench under it. She could not stand. She sat with Pim's arm round her and looked up at it.

"I've nothing to ring," said Odile, beside her. The old woman's voice was a cracked whisper. "Thirty years I kept her. And I've nothing to ring for you, Dagny. Nothing at all."

"There's the hook," said Dagny.

Odile looked at her.

Pim understood first. He got up from the bench, and went to the berth wall where the haulers kept their tools, and came back with a length of iron pipe as long as his arm, dark and heavy. He held it out to her. She could not lift it. So he held it, and she put her hands over his — her burned hands over his steady ones, the way Odile's had lain over his on the box under the bell — and together they raised it, and struck the empty Hook.

It was not a bell. It was iron on iron, a flat hard clang, poor and ugly, swallowed at once by the rock. It went out across the Dock Ring anyway. All along the ring road, people stopped what they were doing and stood still for it.

"For Kas," said Dagny.

They struck it again.

"For Teodor Basu. For Ezra Hesketh. For Dorothea Clay. For Joss."

Again. Again.

"For Abara, Joseph," said Pim. "Achebe, Ruth. Adair, Michael." He did not stop. He knew them; he had read them aloud on the box for fourteen nights. "Agbaje, Femi. Aguilar, Rosa—"

Dagny's hands slid off his on the pipe. He did not notice at first. He went on striking the Hook, alone, on his own, and saying the names, one for each stroke, Ahmed and Akande and Albrecht, and the people of the Dock Ring stood along the ring road in the evening light under the Wick and listened to him, and some of them began, under their breath, to say the names after him; and Hale Varga stood with his scarred hands at his sides and his face wet, and did not move; and Odile Marchbank, beside the bench, put her shaking hand very gently over Dagny Ostrova's eyes, and closed them, and left her hand there.

"Owing," said Odile, so quietly that only the dead woman could have heard. "Owed."

Pim went on until he could not lift the pipe. Then somebody else took it from him and went on.

# Chapter 20: The Wick

*The Owing · 311 AC*

They went up the keel on the twenty-seventh day, four thousand of them, in the dark.

The keel-ways ran in the outer skin of the rock, a hundred metres under the floor of the Galleries, where the Company's engineers had cut them three and a half centuries ago for the pipes and the power and the long cold cables that tied the fore end of Lantern to the aft. They were tunnels two metres high and two wide, rough-walled, ribbed with old steel, and most of them were dead: iced shut, or fallen in, or blocked with three hundred years of frozen seepage. Cutters used the aft ends. Nobody had walked one end to end in living memory. Tamsin had walked the first three kilometres of Keel Four a dozen times, chasing copper, and knew where it forked and where the ice began. Beyond that she knew nothing at all.

So they found out.

It took all night. Four thousand people in a line two metres wide and eleven kilometres long, with lamps where they had lamps and hands on the shoulders in front where they had not, going fore along the skin of the world. The cutters went first, Tamsin and the dozen others among the Called who had worked the Cold, with lances and picks, cutting the ice where it had grown across the way and clearing the falls where they could and finding the forks round them where they could not. Behind them came the young and the strong, carrying the old and the sick. Behind them, everyone else. Jory ran the line. He went back and forth along it all night, sideways and quick, carrying word: _ice at the next rib, wait — fork, keep left — a fall, there's a way round, keep close._ Orrin walked near the front with a slate full of old Company plans, the keel-way maps he had copied from the archive on the last night before Lusk caught him, reading them by lamplight and calling out the frame numbers as they passed them stencilled on the ribs.

Nobody talked much. In the keel the sound of their feet came back off the walls and went on ahead of them into the dark, and the cold was so deep it seemed to have weight, and every so often, through the rock over their heads, faint, they felt the Wick stammer — a tremor in the stone, a change in the hum of the world — and everybody in the line stopped, and held their breath, and waited, until it steadied.

At four in the morning they came to the end of Keel Four, where it opened into a wide low gallery full of old pipe-runs and machinery, at the fore end of the rock. It was warm. That was the first thing. After eleven kilometres of the keel the air coming out of the gallery mouth was warm on their faces, and smelled of oil, and hot metal, and something else, faint and dry, like a stove.

Wenna Blackwood was waiting in it with two hundred of the Salt.

She was sitting on an old pump housing with a lance across her knees and her one eye on the keel mouth, and when Tamsin came out of it, white with frost, she stood up.

"You're late," she said.

"It's a long way."

"Your brother said four. It's ten past." Wenna looked past her at the line coming out of the keel behind her — the cutters, the young, the old being carried, the families, filing out into the warm gallery one by one, blinking in the Salt's lamps, more and more and more of them, until the gallery was full and they were still coming. Her face did something Tamsin had not seen it do before. "Four thousand," she said.

"Nearer five. The fourth list came into the Cold yesterday. They came too."

Wenna nodded slowly. She did not say anything for a while. Then she turned and pointed, with the lance, at the far wall of the gallery.

It was not a wall. It was a hull.

A great curved sweep of riveted blue-grey plating, going up out of the floor of the gallery into the rock overhead, and away on either side of them out of the reach of the lamps: the belly of a ship, buried nose-first in the fore end of the world, with the gallery cut round it like a socket round a tooth. There were pipes running into it, old ones, crusted with three hundred years of scale. There was a ladder running up it to a hatch. Beside the hatch, in square white stencil, faded nearly to nothing: FRAME 40 — REACTOR — LOWER ACCESS.

"There's your Hulk," said Wenna Blackwood. "Underneath."

* * *

The hatch was not welded. It was locked, with a Company lock, and Orrin opened it in four minutes, because he had opened its twin in the floor of the fifth hall three nights before and had not stopped thinking about it since.

Inside, the Hulk was a ship.

Tamsin did not know why that surprised her. Everybody on Lantern knew the Hulk was a ship; it was the first thing they taught you. But she had always thought of it as the guides on the Account Day tours talked about it, as bones: a dead thing, a relic, a floor with a brass rail round it. It was not a floor. It was corridors and ladders and bulkheads and doors, rooms with bunks in them and lockers with names stencilled on, a galley with its pans still hanging, everything thick with dust and cold and silent and perfectly, absolutely whole, as if the people who had lived in it had stepped out that morning. The Count had used some of it. There were crates in the first corridors, Count grey, stencilled with the Count's mark. Beyond them there was nothing but the ship.

The reactor room was on the lowest deck, behind a pressure door that stood open. It was warm in there. Warmer than anywhere Tamsin had been in her life, warmer than Wenna's room behind the cargo net; the air shimmered with it. The room was round and high and lined with machinery she did not understand, and in its middle, in a ring of steel cradles round a great dark column that went up through the deck overhead, there were fourteen cassettes.

They were the size of coffins, as Ninian had said. They were dull grey, and cabled each to the column, and each had a small light on its end, steady, amber, like an eye half open. They hummed. Very faintly. You felt it in your teeth more than heard it.

Tamsin stood in the doorway and counted them, because she could not help it. Then she counted them again.

"Fourteen," said Orrin, beside her. His voice was very quiet.

"Fourteen."

Behind them, Wenna's people were already coming down the ladder with the sleds.

* * *

The freight lift was on the old plans at the ship's fore end, where her nose went into the rock, and the controls for it — Orrin said — would be on the bridge. So while the Salt began, with winches and chains and a great deal of swearing, to uncable the first of the cassettes from the column, Tamsin and Orrin went up through the ship to find it.

It took a long time. The Hulk was bigger than she had imagined, and its decks were not level — the ship lay nose-down in the rock, tilted, so that every corridor ran a little downhill toward the fore, and every ladder leaned. They went up through deck after deck of cabins and stores, all dust and silence. Once they passed a room full of chairs in rows facing a blank screen, as if for a meeting that had never been held. Once they passed a nursery, with small beds, and Tamsin did not look into it.

The bridge was at the top. A long curved room, at the ship's nose, whose forward wall had been a window once, a great sweep of armoured glass looking out ahead of her; and now the glass looked out on rock. Raw grey nickel-iron, pressed up against it from outside, filling every pane. The ship had gone into the world nose-first and the world had closed round her face. There were chairs at the consoles, and a big chair in the middle, on a step, and everything was white with dust.

Orrin went to the consoles. Tamsin went to the big chair, because it was in the middle, and stood looking at it.

There was a plate on the console in front of it. A brass plate, screwed to the panel, the kind every ship carried, Orrin told her later, to say what she was and who had made her. It was furred with dust. Tamsin wiped it with her sleeve.

She read slowly. She always had. She moved her lips.

HALDEN-ASH CARRIER COVENANT.
HA 7731. TANNET REACH, 2361.

"Orrin," she said.

He came and stood beside her and read it. He did not say anything for a while.

"_Covenant_," he said at last, as if trying the taste of it. "Not the _Ardent Mercy_."

"No."

"Two thousand three hundred and sixty-one. Seventy years after the colony." He put out his hand and touched the plate. "It's not the ship that brought us. It's a Company ship. A carrier. From the end." He turned and looked down the long dusty bridge at the rock pressed up against the glass. "It's not bones. It came _back_."

"Came back from where?"

"From going." He was very pale. "From the Closing. The Company ran, Tamsin. Everybody knows that. In their ships, through the Throat, and the door shut behind them. Except—" he touched the plate again— "they didn't. Not this one. This one came back here. And they put it in the rock, and they built the Count on top of it." He turned round slowly. "And told us it was the _Mercy_."

There was a log on the console beside the master's chair. Not a slate; a book. A thick bound book of old paper, with the H-A letters stamped on its cover, lying open as though someone had put it down in the middle of reading it. Orrin picked it up. The dust came off it in a cloud. He turned to the front and read, and then he turned pages, faster and faster, his lips moving, and then he sat down on the step of the master's chair with the book in his lap, quite suddenly, as if his legs had gone.

"What?" said Tamsin.

He held it out to her, open. She could not read the old hand; it was small and slanted and full of flourishes. He took it back and read it to her. His voice was not steady.

"_Ship's log, carrier Covenant, at Lantern. Kept by R. Achterberg, formerly Director, Sabra Concern. Year three after the Closing._

"_We came home in her because we had nowhere else to go. Four hundred and six of us. The people of Lantern did not kill us, which was more than we deserved. I keep the books now. Somebody has to, or the air stops. I keep them at the root, under her, with the seal they gave me back._

"_I write this so that whoever keeps them after me will know what they are keeping._

"_The Account is owed to the dead of Caul Deep. Nine hundred and four people who died in the ice on Caul in the spring of 2387 because I would not pay to brace the shaft, and whom I sealed in the ice so that the Concord would not know. Every hour of air on Lantern, from the forty-ninth day of the Closing, is owed to them. That was done at the root by Dagny Ostrova, a Wick-tender, with a key that Teodor Basu made, and it cannot be undone. Nobody holds the Account. Nobody can draw on it. Nobody can sell it. It is owed to them, and they are dead._

"_It is discharged when their names are spoken at the root, every one, in order._

"_Their names are on the bell of the Ardent Mercy, which hung at the Dock Ring. Ostrova's brother took it through the Throat as it closed. We do not have them. Nobody does._

"_So it is owed. Keep the books honestly. Every hour, to them. Not to yourselves._

"_R. A._"

Orrin stopped. He turned a page.

"There's more," he said. "In a different hand. Later. Much later." He read it in a flat voice. "_Year 61. The Director's instruction regarding honest keeping has been reviewed by the Count and found impracticable. A debt that is owed to no living person compels no living person. The doctrine of Caul is adopted in its place: the Account is owed to the dark that kept us. The Founders' log is to be held by the Comptroller-General alone. The reserve in the ship's reactor is to be held for the continuance of the Count. Neither is to be spoken of._" He closed the book. "It's signed by the Comptroller-General of the day. I know the name. He's on the plaque in the fifth hall. He's the one who had the brass rail put up."

Tamsin stood on the dusty bridge of the _Covenant_ with her hand on the master's chair and her face pressed, almost, against the rock of the world.

"Caul's people," she said.

"Nine hundred and four of them."

"We've owed them every breath. For three hundred years. Real people. Dead." She found she was shaking. "And the Count knew. And made it into a ghost story. _Caul is the dark that kept us._ So nobody would ask."

"So nobody would ask who," said Orrin. "Yes."

"And the bell." She thought of the Hook, black and empty over the Dock Ring, and her grandmother saying _We ring the Hook so we remember there was a bell, and there were names, and somebody took them. That's not sad. That's angry._ "The names were on the bell. That's why we ring the Hook. That's what it's for. We've been ringing for them and we didn't even know."

Orrin opened the log again, and looked at the old slanted hand, and closed it.

"_Their names are on the bell_," he said. "_We do not have them. Nobody does._"

Neither of them said anything for a long time. Under their feet, through the tilted deck of the buried ship, they felt a long shudder, and a groan of steel, as the first of the fourteen came out of its cradle.

* * *

The Count came for them through the Sanctum.

Tamsin heard it before she saw it: shouting, far below, and the flat hard crack of slug-guns, and the roar of Salt voices, all of it coming up through the tilted decks of the ship. She and Orrin went down the ladders at a run, the log under Orrin's arm, and came out on the reactor deck into a fight.

The Sanctum opened into the Hulk. Of course it did. Orrin had said it: _the Tally's root is under the ship._ There was a door on the reactor deck — a heavy steel door, Count-grey, that Tamsin had taken for a store — and it stood open now, and through it the bailiffs of the Count were coming, in grey, with batons and guns, and the Salt were meeting them in the corridor with lances and pry-bars and chains, and the corridor was full of bodies and smoke and the white roar of a lance cutting the air.

And in the middle of the reactor room, in the shimmering heat, with eleven cassettes still in their cradles round the dark column and three on sleds by the hatch, Brother Lusk was walking toward Jory.

Her brother had been at the hatch, at the head of the sled-line, running the chain for the winch. He had not seen Lusk come through the Sanctum door. He saw him now. He stood very still beside the sled with the chain in his hands and his chin up, fifteen and all elbows, and Lusk came toward him across the warm floor without hurrying, with his baton held loosely at his side, the way a man walks toward a door he means to close.

Tamsin did not decide anything. She was across the reactor room before she knew she had moved. She had the lance in her hands — her own lance, the cutter's lance she had brought eleven kilometres up the keel — and she had lit it, and the white flame came out of its throat a metre long and roaring, and Lusk heard it, and turned.

He looked at her. For a moment she saw something in his face that she had seen at the Lip, when the Galleries rang: interest. As if she were a sum that had come out in an unexpected way.

Then she put the lance through him.

It was very quick. She had cut steel with it all her life and it went through a man the way it went through copper, without stopping. Brother Lusk looked down at himself. He opened his mouth. Then he folded up, slowly, on the warm floor of the reactor room, among the cradles, and lay still.

Tamsin turned the lance off. Her hands were perfectly steady. She stood over him and found that she felt nothing at all, and thought that she would, later, and did not care.

"Tam," said Jory, behind her. His voice had gone small.

"Get the sled moving," she said. "Go on. Get it up."

In the corridor the fighting had stopped. Not because anyone had won. Because the bailiffs had seen what was behind the Salt — out through the lower hatch, on the ladder, in the gallery beyond, more and more of them, pressing in to see — five thousand of the Called, who had walked eleven kilometres in the dark and had nothing in the world left to lose. The bailiffs were not fools. They began to put their batons down on the deck. Some of them sat down beside them.

Through the Sanctum door, beyond them, Tamsin saw a dark coat going away fast down a corridor, toward a light.

"Vosk," said Orrin. "The root."

* * *

The root of the Tally was a round room, not large, with a low domed ceiling, and at its centre on a pedestal a desk of dark glass and brass with three slots in its face and a little grille beside them. That was all. Tamsin had expected more. She had expected, she realised, something like the Wick.

Imre Vosk was at the desk. He had something in the left-hand slot, on a chain round his neck — he was bent over the console with the chain pulled taut, his face very close to the grille — and he was speaking into it, quickly and quietly, the soft voice that went everywhere.

"—release from the Tally's charge all accounts at present located aft of frame—"

"Stop," said Orrin.

Vosk did not stop. "—aft of frame nine hundred, the Cold Wards entire, and close the—"

Orrin crossed the room and took him by the collar of his dark coat and pulled him away from the console. He was not strong. He did it anyway. Vosk staggered back and the chain round his neck went tight and snapped, and something brass fell out of the slot and rang on the floor and rolled, in a small wobbling circle, and lay still at Tamsin's feet.

She bent and picked it up.

It was a disk. Brass, the size of her palm, worn smooth with handling, warm from the slot. There were letters on it, nearly gone. She turned it to the light. Two letters, leaning on each other like tired men. H and A.

"The Count Key," said Vosk. He was on the floor against the wall where Orrin had thrown him, with his coat torn at the collar and his hair in his eyes. He laughed, a little breathlessly. "Well. There it is. Three hundred years. You'd think it would look like more."

"You were going to close the Cold," said Orrin. "With five thousand people in it."

"I was going to close the Cold with five thousand people walking up a keel-way toward the Count's reactor." Vosk wiped his mouth. "Yes. I'd have closed the keel too, if I'd had time. The sums haven't changed, Orrin. They never change. You've got fourteen cassettes and thirty thousand people."

"Eleven in the column and three on the sleds," said Tamsin. "Going up the lift now."

Vosk looked at her for the first time. He looked at the brass disk in her hand, and at the cutter's rig, and the frost still white on her shoulders from the keel, and the burn of the lance on her gloves.

"Agathe Hask's girl," he said. "Yes. Wenna said. God help us." He leaned his head back against the wall of the root and closed his eyes. "Light it, then. Light the whole thing. End to end, the Cold and all. You'll have fourteen years. And then it'll go out, and you'll all be in the dark together, and you'll owe Caul every breath you take in it, the same as now. You can't close the Account, girl. Nobody can. You've read the log, I suppose. You know why."

"I know why," said Tamsin.

"Then you know it's owed for ever."

"No," said Tamsin. "It's owed till the bell comes home."

Vosk opened his eyes and looked at her.

"There isn't a bell," he said. "There's never going to be a bell. The Company took it."

"A Wick-tender's brother took it. Through the Throat. To tell the Concord what was done at Caul." She closed her hand on the disk. "Your Founders' log says so. You've read it. You had it locked in a book on a bridge nobody goes to, and put a brass rail round a floor, and told children it was the ship that brought us." She stepped closer to him. "You're right. We can't close it. We haven't got their names. But we know who we owe now. And it's not the dark, and it's not the Count, and it's not you."

* * *

The Founders' lift came up into the light of the spine-road at nine in the morning, through a door that had been welded shut for three hundred years and that the Salt cut open from the inside with three lances in forty minutes; and on its platform, on a sled, humming, with its little amber light like a half-open eye, was the first of the fourteen.

The spine-road was full. Word had gone up out of the Hulk faster than the lift. The Galleries had heard; the Upper Wards had heard; the counting families had come out of their houses on the clean white terraces in their good coats and stood on their steps and watched, and nobody had stopped them, and nobody had stopped the Owing either, coming up the terraces from below in their thousands, from Twenty-Two and Nineteen and the Salt Docks, until the road along the foot of the Wick was a crowd from one end to the other, and the bailiffs who were left had taken their grey coats off and stood among it.

They hauled the sled up the spine by hand.

There was no other way. The cargo winches on the spine had rusted solid in the century since anyone had hauled anything up them. So they did it the way the Wick crews must have done it in the Closing year, with ropes: a chain of people on the spoke-ladders and the crawls, clipped on, hand over hand, two hundred of them, pulling a sled the weight of four men up a column of light into the place where there was no weight at all. Tamsin was on the rope. So was Orrin, with his soft hands raw. So was Wenna Blackwood, roaring at them all like a gravel chute. Jory ran the line.

At the master ring, at the root of the Wick, beside his tin hut, Ninian the Wickwright was waiting at the Founders' port with the hatch open and his blind white eyes turned toward the sound of them coming.

"Bring her here," he said. "Gently. Gently. That's it. Let me feel her."

He put both his old paper hands flat on the cassette. He stood like that for a long time, with the light of the lamp pouring down on him from the column above, white and hard, the light that had taken his eyes; and his face, lit from above, was the face of a man touching the face of someone he had thought was dead.

"Yes," he said. "Oh, yes. That's a good one."

They fed her into the port. It took twenty minutes, with Ninian's hands guiding every centimetre of it, talking to it under his breath the way Tamsin had once heard a cutter talk to a bar of copper. Then the port closed. Then he went to the master collimator, by touch, and put his hands on the trim wheels, and turned his face up the length of the lamp toward the aft end of the world, where it stopped.

"Watch," he said. "I can't. You'll have to watch for me."

He turned the wheels.

Tamsin watched.

She had lived her whole life under a lamp that stopped two-thirds of the way down the sky. Under it the Galleries, and the light; beyond it, the dark rod of the dead axis, and under that the Cold. She had walked into that dark a thousand times with a tether on her hip. She had taken her mother into it. She watched it now, from the root of the Wick, along eleven kilometres of the world.

The light went down the axis like water poured along a pipe.

Not fast. It did not need to be fast. It went down the dark rod a hundred metres, and then another hundred, white fire flowering out of the dead bottle and racing along it, and every hundred metres it went the shadows below it shortened and the terraces under it came out of the dark — dead terraces, frost-furred, with their washing frozen on the lines and their doors standing open — and their frost caught the light and blazed, white and gold, and then began, all along the aft walls of the world, very slowly, to drip. And the light went on. Over the Pump Galleries where the Called had burned their doors to keep warm. Over the gallery where they had laid their dead in rows under the frost. Over the service way under the old pump bank, and a welded door, and a woman sitting with her back to the wall with a red shoe in her hands. It went on to the very end of the axis, to the aft wall of the world, where nobody had ever in Tamsin's life seen anything but black, and there it stopped, because there was nowhere else for it to go.

The whole of Lantern was lit.

It was very quiet on the master ring. On the spine-road below, on every terrace, in every gallery, thirty thousand people were standing and looking up. Nobody spoke. Nobody moved. On Lantern you were taught at five not to look straight at the Wick, because it would burn your eyes; and they looked anyway, all of them, at the whole length of it, end to end, for the first time in their lives.

"Tell me," said Ninian.

"It's all lit," said Tamsin. Her voice did not work properly. "All of it. All the way to the end. The Cold's melting. The frost's turning to rain. It's all lit."

The old man nodded. He took his hands off the trim wheels and put them flat on the rail of the master ring and stood there, with his face turned up into the blaze he could not see.

"Thirteen more," he said. "Fourteen years. Fourteen years, end to end." He was smiling. "I won't see the end of them. Good. Somebody else can shorten her, next time. I've done enough of that."

From somewhere far below, on a terrace near the Dock Ring, someone began to sing. Tamsin knew the tune before she could hear the words. It was the Twilight Song: the slow, low one, from before the Count, about the crews who took the lamp down to make the Company listen and the whole world standing on its terraces and seeing the light, for once, as a thing that someone had made. Another voice took it up. Then a gallery's worth. It went round the curve of the world, terrace to terrace, under the blazing lamp, until it seemed to Tamsin that the whole rock was singing, very quietly, to itself.

Wenna Blackwood, beside her on the ring, was not singing. She stood with her great scarred arms folded on the rail and her one eye on the lit length of the Wick, and after a while she said, without turning her head: "Seven to the lamp and seven to me. That was the deal."

"That was the deal," said Tamsin.

"Light them all." Wenna was still not looking at her. "All fourteen. I've no use for a lamp of my own on the Salt Docks with this up there." She jerked her head at the sky. "I'll keep my end in the reserve. In the Wick store, where it was. Held. For the day she starts to gutter. And nobody charges for it." She spat over the rail, and watched it curve away sideways into the light. "God help me. Agathe Hask's girl."

* * *

Tamsin went to the Hook that evening, under a lamp that did not stop.

The square below the inner lock was full, as it had been on the day of the proclamation. Fuller. The Owing were there, and the Called, back out of the Cold with the rain of its melting frost still in their hair, and the Salt, and Wickwrights, and people from the Upper Wards in their good coats standing a little apart and not sure whether they should be there at all. Orrin was beside her, with the Founders' log under his arm. Jory was on her other side. In her pocket there was a brass disk, warm, with two letters on it nearly worn away.

Somebody handed her the bar. It was the same length of iron pipe as always, worn smooth and dark at the gripping end by three hundred years of hands.

She stood under the empty Hook and looked up at it. Black iron. Nothing on it. There never had been, in her lifetime. Only now, standing under it in the full light of the Wick for the first time, she saw something she had never noticed: on the bracket, where the bell's shackle must once have hung, a short stub of old iron, cut through, its end rough and black, as if someone had put a torch to it a very long time ago, in a hurry, at night.

"For my mother," she said aloud. "Agathe Hask."

She struck the Hook.

"For the woman at Aft Store 3, with the shoe. For whoever she was. And for the one inside who waited."

She struck it again.

"For Dobbs. For the first thousand and the second and the third." Again. Again. "For the nine hundred and four of Caul Deep." She stopped. She found that she was crying, at last, in front of all of them, under the lamp, and that she did not care. "Whose names we haven't got. Whom we owe every breath. Who we didn't even know were people." She looked out at the square. "We'll owe them. Honestly. Every hour, to them. Not to the Count. Not to the dark. To them. Till the bell comes home."

She struck the Hook a last time and gave the bar to Orrin.

He looked at it in his raw hands for a long moment. Then he looked at the square, at thirty thousand faces turned toward him under the light, and said, in the clear precise voice of a counter reading a figure he has checked three times and knows is right:

"Owing."

And the square — slowly, raggedly, the Owing and the Called and the Salt and the counting families, all of them, the way they had said it every day of their lives, and for the first time meaning something by it — answered:

"Owed."

# Chapter 21: The Names

*The Healing · 3089 CE*

Fen found him in Hold 1 at six in the morning, sitting on a crate beside the bell with the lamp burned down to a glow at his feet and his voice gone to a whisper, reading the founders' band.

She did not know he was there. She had come for the bell. Tol had let her out of her cabin an hour before with his mechanical key on its loop of wire — Kell's people had confined him to the engine spaces, not to his cabin, and the engine spaces had more doors than Kell knew about — and she had come down through the crate-maze of the dark hold with no plan at all except to be beside it when they reached the rock, because it was hers, because it was going home, because she could not bear that it should arrive alone under a Brightwater tarpaulin. And she came round the last stack of crates and there was the adjuster, on a crate in the cold, with his hand on the bronze and his face very close to it, saying in a cracked whisper: "Okafor, Grace. Okeke, Samuel. Oladipo, Bisi."

She stood still.

He had not heard her. He went on. "Olsen, Erik. Olsen, Marta. Ortiz, Benedicto." He was reading by the lamp's last glow with his finger on each name, slowly, the way she did it. His voice kept failing and he kept pushing through it. "Osei, Kwame. Ostrowski—"

"Anna," said Fen.

He stopped. He turned his head and looked at her, and his face in the dying light was grey and hollow and wet, and he did not seem surprised to see her at all.

"Ostrowski, Anna," he said. "Yes."

"How long have you been doing that?"

"Since about midnight." He looked at the bell. "I did the crossing first. The lip. Then the colony, up the waist, as far as I could read. Then I started on the band." He swallowed. "I'm on the O's."

She came and sat down on the next crate. She did not say anything. She had come down meaning to say a great many things to him and found she could not remember any of them.

"There's something on the crown," said Corvin. "Did you know? On the big leaf. Very small. In a hand that shakes."

"I thought it was a damaged name."

"It isn't." He held up the lamp, and she leaned in, and read it, and sat back.

_THESE ARE OWED. SAY THEIR NAMES._

They sat side by side on the crates in the cold, and neither of them spoke for a long time.

"I signed them," Corvin said at last. "All three. The default. The consolidation. The bell. I signed them because he said he'd revive your family's debt if I didn't, and I believed him, and I'm good at signing things. My hand doesn't shake. It never has." He looked at his hand. "I can't take it back. A mark's a mark. That's the whole of what I am, Fen. You said so. A man who concurs."

"I spat at you."

"You were right to."

"I know I was." She looked at the bell. "What are you going to do?"

"I'm going to take it to the rock," said Corvin, "and do what it says."

Above them, through the deck and the hull and the cold, the deep thrum of the _Good Title_'s engine changed its note. They felt the ship come round. And on the comm in the hold's bulkhead, faint, a young voice — Dacey's, on the bridge, forgetting that Kell's people could hear him — said: "Clearing the limb. Clearing the limb. Oh. Oh, look at that."

* * *

From the bridge it was a rock.

A long grey irregular lump of nickel-iron eleven kilometres from end to end, turning slowly against the banded ochre face of Nhai, pocked and scarred and old. There were structures on it — the remains of gantries, aerials, the ring of a dock at the fore end — and none of them had lights. Corvin had stood in front of four hundred and twelve settlements and he knew what a dead habitat looked like from outside. It looked like this.

Then the rock turned, and showed them its fore end, and the dock ring, and in the middle of the dock ring, the great inner lock.

It was open.

And out of it — out of the rock, out of the inside of the world, through the round mouth of the lock and the wide ring of the dock — light was pouring. White light. Hard and pure and steady, the light of a sun, spilling out of the hollow rock into the dark in a long pale cone that went away from the lock across the black for a thousand kilometres before it faded, like the beam of a lamp left burning in the window of a house at night, for someone who had not come home yet.

Nobody on the bridge said anything.

"Lantern," said Fen at last, very quietly, beside Corvin. "That's why. That's why they called it that."

Haste was at the front of the bridge with Kell. He was looking at the light with his head a little on one side, the way he had looked at the Throat from the queue at Ambry: a performance being put on for him. He turned round.

"A populated habitat, then," he said. "Mr. Saal, you'll be giving the first speech. Sergeant, please have your people and the labour gang at the forward lock in two hours. Comms, raise them, please."

Dacey looked at him. Then he looked at his board, and swallowed, and touched it.

"They're raising us," he said. "Sir. They've been raising us since we cleared the limb. Old Company band. Voice." He put it on the speaker.

It was a woman's voice. Old, steady, unhurried, with an accent Corvin had never heard — the vowels flattened, the consonants soft, as if the language had been kept in a closed room for seven hundred years and had gone its own way in there.

"—ship off our fore quarter, this is Lantern. Ship, this is Lantern. Owing and owed. We see you. We saw you clear the limb. Who are you, and what do you bring? Ship, this is Lantern—"

Haste leaned past Dacey and touched the board himself.

"Lantern, this is the vessel _Good Title_, out of Port Ambry, on behalf of Brightwater Recovery of Kessane, which holds by lawful purchase the instrument of the Halden-Ash Company's Sabra Concern." His voice was warm, pleasant, exact. "We bring you greetings from the Concord, which has not forgotten you. We'll be docking within two hours. Please prepare to receive us."

The speaker hissed. For a long time there was nothing. Then the old woman's voice came back, and it had not changed at all.

"_Good Title_, Lantern. We heard you. Lantern owes the Company nothing. Lantern owes Caul." A pause. "Dock at the inner lock. We'll be waiting."

The channel closed.

Haste straightened up. He was smiling.

"We'll see," he said.

* * *

They had been waiting, it turned out, for seven hundred years, and they had got very good at it.

The _Good Title_ came in through the open inner lock of the Dock Ring at noon by the ship's clock, and Corvin, standing at the forward port with the case in his hand, saw the inside of the world.

He had read the Company's descriptions. They had not prepared him. A hollow world, lit from within, the walls of it climbing away on every side and meeting overhead, every surface terraced and built on and grown over — houses, roofs, tanks, farms, green, so much green, hanging upside down in the sky above him — and down the middle of it all, from end to end of the rock, a bar of white fire so bright he could not look at it, a sword laid along the axis of the world. And under the sword, in the light, on every terrace and road and roof that he could see, people. Thousands of them. Tens of thousands. All of them standing still, all of them looking at the ship.

The dock was full. A square below the inner lock, as much of a square as the curve of the rock allowed, packed from wall to wall, and silent. When the _Good Title_'s ramp went down and Haste walked out onto it in his dark coat with Kell behind him and Corvin behind Kell, the silence did not change at all.

There was an iron bracket above the inner lock. The length of a man's arm, black, very old, drilled into the rock. There was nothing on it. Under it, on a bench, there was an old woman.

She stood up as Haste came down the ramp. She was tiny and very straight, with white hair cut short and pale eyes, almost colourless, in a face like folded paper, and she wore a plain grey coat and round her neck, on a chain, a disk of brass the size of a palm, worn smooth. Beside her stood a dozen others, men and women, young and old, in ordinary clothes; and behind them, the square.

"I'm Senna Hask," she said. "Reader of the Account. You're the Company."

"I'm Quennell Haste, of Brightwater Recovery, which—"

"Holds the instrument. Yes. We heard you." Senna Hask looked at him with her pale eyes, without any particular expression. "We've known you'd come since the Throat began to heal. Our Wickwrights watch it. Three years now. We've had time to think about what to say to you." She folded her hands. "Lantern owes the Company nothing. The Company's claim was quit at the root on the forty-ninth day of the Closing, by a Wick-tender called Dagny Ostrova, with a key the Company's own comptroller made. Every account on Lantern since that hour has been owed to the dead of Caul Deep. Nine hundred and four of them. Nobody holds it. Nobody draws on it. Nobody can sell it. It's owed to them." She paused. "We have kept it honestly for seven hundred years, Mr. Haste. Every hour of air. We'll go on keeping it. But not for you."

Haste smiled. He had a slate in his hand; he held it up.

"Brightwater contends," he said, "that the purported assignment of the Concern's receivables in the year of the Closing was made without the authority of the Company's officers, using an unregistered device, by a person with no standing under the Concern's charter. It was, in short, a fraud. A theft. Under Concord law a fraudulent assignment is void, and the original creditor's claim revives — with interest — attaching to the persons and issue of the original debtors." He lowered the slate. "That's you, Mrs. Hask. All of you. I'm very sorry. I know it's a great deal to take in. My colleague Mr. Saal is here to explain it."

He turned.

They all turned. The square turned. Thirty thousand pairs of eyes — Corvin felt them, the way you feel heat — came to rest on him, standing at the foot of the ramp with the case in his hand.

He had given this speech four hundred and twelve times. He knew it the way he knew his own name. It began _I understand that this is difficult_, and it was calm and sorry and unmovable, and it had a low rate of incident.

He opened his mouth to begin it, and found that it had gone.

"Mr. Saal," said Haste pleasantly.

Corvin looked at the old woman. Then at the empty iron bracket above her head. Then back up the ramp into the ship, to where Fen was standing in the hatchway, with Tol beside her, and between them on its sled, under its Brightwater tarpaulin, something round and heavy and the height of a person's hip.

"Mrs. Hask," he said. "What discharges it?"

Haste's head turned sharply.

Senna Hask looked at Corvin for a long moment.

"Their names," she said. "Spoken at the root. Every one, in order. That's the term Ostrova set. It's in the Tally and it's in the Founders' log. We read it out every year." Her voice did not change. "We haven't got them. Their names were on the bell of the _Ardent Mercy_. It hung there." She lifted one hand, without looking, toward the empty bracket. "Ostrova's brother took it through the Throat as it closed. Seven hundred years ago. To tell the Concord what was done at Caul. Nobody in the Concord ever heard, I suppose. And we've never had them since. We ring the Hook for them. That's all we can do."

"Brightwater holds the bell," said Corvin.

The square was utterly silent.

"It's an asset of the Sabra Concern," he said. "Recovered property. Mr. Haste seized it under the instrument, on the voyage. I concurred. My mark's on the docket. Which makes it — under the instrument Mr. Haste has just read you — property of the Concern, held by Brightwater, in the custody of Brightwater's adjuster." He turned and looked up the ramp. "Fen."

She was already moving. She and Tol came down the ramp together with the sled between them, and the sled's runners scraped on the steel, and at the foot of the ramp Fen took hold of the tarpaulin with the Brightwater seal on it and pulled it off.

The bell of the _Ardent Mercy_ stood in the light of the Wick for the first time in seven hundred and two years.

Nobody in the square made a sound. Then the old woman under the Hook took one step forward, and stopped, and put her hand to her mouth; and somewhere at the back of the square somebody began to cry, a long thin wavering sound that went on and on; and then the crowd moved, all of it, a great slow surge forward, not fast, not angry, the way water moves when a dam goes — toward the bell.

"Sergeant," said Haste. His voice was not pleasant any more. "Secure that asset. And the root. Now."

* * *

Afterwards Corvin could never get it into the right order.

He remembered Kell's people coming down the ramp behind him in their pairs, with their batons low, the way they had drilled in Hold 1, and stopping, at the foot of it, because there was nowhere to go: the square had closed round the bell and the sled and Fen and Tol and the old woman, thousands deep, not touching anyone, only there. He remembered Kell looking at it. Looking at the faces. Looking, Corvin thought later, at the woken sleepers — the labour gang, a hundred and thirty of them still alive from the first three batches, standing in a huddle at the top of the ramp in their thin ship's coveralls, blinking in the light of a sun they had never seen, being stared at by a world. He remembered that one of the sleepers, a thin boy, had begun to walk down the ramp on his own, and that a woman at the edge of the crowd had held out her hand to him without a word, and that he had taken it.

He remembered Haste saying _Sergeant_ again, and Kell not moving.

Then he remembered the Founders' lift: a great iron platform at the side of the square, under the Counting House, and Senna Hask's people running the bell's sled onto it, and the platform going down, into the rock, into the dark, with the bell on it and Fen beside it and Corvin beside Fen, and the old woman holding the rail, and the light of the Wick going away above them in a closing square.

"Where are we going?" he said.

"The root," said Senna Hask. "Under the ship."

"What ship?"

She looked at him with her pale eyes.

"The _Covenant_," she said. "The Company's carrier. She came home, at the Closing. She's under the Count. She's been the floor of the world for seven hundred years."

Fen made a small sound. Corvin turned. She was staring at the old woman.

"The _Covenant_," Fen said. "The Director's statement said the staff would withdraw on the _Covenant_. I've read it a hundred times." She shook her head slowly. "She never arrived anywhere either. None of them did. I thought— everybody thought—"

"She came back," said Senna Hask. "Ostrova brought her back."

* * *

The root was a round room, not large, with a low domed ceiling, at the bottom of a tilted ship buried nose-first in a rock. At its centre on a pedestal there was a desk of dark glass and brass with three slots in its face and a little grille beside them like the grille of a confessional. That was all.

They brought the bell in on its sled and set it beside the desk. Senna Hask took the brass disk from round her neck and put it into the left-hand slot, and the glass woke, and the old woman bent to the grille and said, in her flat soft accent:

"Discharge of the Account. Terms as set. The creditors' names, spoken, in full, in order."

The glass was still for a moment. Then, in plain white letters:

DISCHARGE TERMS PRESENTED. BEGIN.

Senna Hask straightened up and stepped back. She looked at Corvin.

"You've got the bell," she said. "You say them."

He looked at Fen. She was already kneeling beside the bell with her loupe at her eye and her lamp in her hand, and she held up the lamp, and its light fell on the founders' band, on the long cramped grey line of names going round the waist of the bronze in hurried hands. She did not look at him. She put her finger on the first name.

"Abara, Joseph," said Corvin Saal.

Fen struck the bell.

It was not a hammer. There was no hammer. It was her open hand, flat against the bronze, the way Corvin had seen her lay it there a hundred times — but harder, so that the bell spoke. A low note, a little sour, with a waver in it. The note from the recording. It went up into the dome of the root and hung there.

"Achebe, Ruth," said Corvin.

She struck it again.

They went on. Name, and the bell. Name, and the bell. It was slow. It had to be. Some of the names were cut so cramped and so hurried that Fen had to bend close with the loupe and read them to him in a whisper first, letter by letter, and then he said them aloud; and the glass on the console beside them took each one and, after it, in small plain letters, wrote it down. ABARA, JOSEPH. ACHEBE, RUTH. ADAIR, MICHAEL. A line at a time, going up the glass.

Above them, through the decks of the buried ship, through the rock, he could hear the square. It had been silent. It was not silent now. It was ringing. Iron on iron, flat and hard, from the Hook, and from the rails and pipes and posts of the Dock Ring, and from further away, from the terraces and galleries up the curving walls of the world, more and more of them — thirty thousand people with bars of iron, striking whatever would ring, not in time, not together, a great ragged din coming down through seven hundred years of rock.

"Agbaje, Femi," said Corvin.

He was on the H's when Haste came into the root.

He came alone. Corvin heard him on the ladder before he saw him — quick, light steps — and then he was in the doorway, in his dark coat, with his hair disarranged for the first time Corvin had ever seen it, and in his hand a short ugly slug-gun he must have taken off one of Kell's people, held as if he had never held one before and found it a little distasteful.

"Stop," said Haste.

"Hussain, Amira," said Corvin.

Fen struck the bell.

"Mr. Saal, I'm not going to tell you again. That device is the property of the Concern and so is every account on that machine, and I will not have them—"

"Ibe, Chidi," said Corvin.

Haste shot him.

He felt it as a blow to the left side, low, under the ribs, as if someone had punched him there with a fist the size of a melon. He sat down. He did not decide to; his legs went and he was sitting on the floor of the root with his back against the pedestal, and the white letters on the glass above him, and the bell in front of him, and Fen's face, white, turned toward him, with the lamp in her hand.

"Read," he said to her. His voice came out very strangely. "Read me the next one."

"Corvin—"

"Read me the next one, Fen."

She looked at him for one more moment. Then she turned back to the bell and put the loupe to her eye.

"Idowu, Grace," she said.

"Idowu, Grace," said Corvin.

She struck the bell.

Haste had not moved from the doorway. He was looking at the slug-gun in his hand as if it had done something embarrassing in public. Behind him, on the ladder, there were heavier steps; and then Idris Kell was in the doorway too, and reached past Haste, without any particular hurry, and took the gun out of his hand, gently, the way you would take something from a child. Haste turned and stared at him.

"Sergeant—"

"I've got a daughter," said Kell. He said it in the same bored voice in which he had explained enumeration on the gantry in Hold 1. "Twenty-six years attaching, from her mother's side. And there's a boy up there off our hold holding hands with a woman he's never met in his life, in the light, and I'm not putting either of them down in front of their own bell, Mr. Haste. Not for four years off mine. Not for forty." He put the gun in his belt. "Sit down, sir. It's going to be a while."

Haste sat down.

"Ige, Folake," said Fen.

"Ige, Folake," said Corvin.

The bell.

* * *

It took three hours and nine minutes. Senna Hask timed it, she told him afterwards; the Reader times everything, it is in the office. For the last hour of it Corvin did not know very clearly where he was. He knew Fen's voice, reading him each name before he said it, and he knew the bell, and he knew that someone — Senna Hask's people, or Tol, who had come down the ladder at some point and knelt beside him with his greasy hands pressed hard against Corvin's side — was keeping him from bleeding to death on the floor of the root of a dead company's ledger under a ship that had come home. He knew the ringing, coming down through the rock. It did not stop. Once, he thought, it changed — it went quiet, all at once, everywhere, for the space of a breath, and then came back louder — and he learned later that that had been the moment when the glass on the console wrote OSTROWSKI, ANNA, and somebody up in the square who had been counting had called out the number, and the whole world had heard it and stopped, and gone on.

"Zeller, Hanna," said Fen, at nine minutes past three in the afternoon.

Corvin said it.

She put her hand flat on the bronze and struck the bell for the last time, and the low sour note went up into the dome of the root and hung there, humming, for a long time, and slowly faded.

The glass on the console was quite still.

Then the long column of names on it — nine hundred and four of them, Abara to Zeller, in plain white letters, a line at a time — went out, all at once. And in their place, in the middle of the glass, the Tally of Lantern, which had kept every account on the rock every hour on the hour for eight hundred years, wrote:

CREDITOR NAMES SPOKEN IN FULL. ACCOUNT OF CAUL DISCHARGED.

And then, after a pause, as though it were checking:

NO BALANCE OUTSTANDING ON THE TALLY. NO CREDITOR. NO DEBTOR.

And then, in letters no different from the others, but which Corvin would see, for the rest of his life, whenever he shut his eyes:

THE TALLY IS QUIT. VALVES RELEASED TO OPEN.

Above them, through the rock, he heard the ringing stop.

* * *

They hung the bell back on the Hook nine days later.

The old shackle was gone; there was only the stub of it, cut through, rough and black, where somebody had put a torch to it seven hundred years before. Lantern's smiths made a new one. It took them four days, because they wanted it right, and they cast it from the brass of a Company valve-wheel they took off the Pumphouse door, which nobody on Lantern needed to ask any longer whether it could open. Then the Wick crews climbed the Hook with chains and slings, and the bell went up, slowly, swinging a little in the slings, humming, and came to rest on its iron bracket over the inner lock of the Dock Ring, where it had hung for ninety-one years before anyone now living had been born.

Corvin watched from the bench under it. He could sit up now, for an hour or two at a time. Lantern's doctors had taken the slug out of his side on the second day, in an infirmary on the second floor of the Counting House, in a bed that Senna Hask told him had been in that room since the Closing; and they had told him, in their flat soft accent, that he was a lucky man, and he had not argued. Senna Hask had sat by that bed on the third evening and told him, because he asked, how the lamp had been kept alight for seven hundred years: fourteen cassettes, out of the belly of the buried ship, that had lit the Wick end to end for fourteen years; and in those fourteen years the Wickwrights and the ice-runners of the Salt had learned between them to skim the fuel from Nhai's clouds and make their own, badly at first and then well, because they had known exactly how long they had.

He was a bonded man too. He had not argued with that either. The instrument was void; Haste, who was confined to the _Good Title_ with Kell's people standing at its lock and very little to say to anyone, had conceded that much on the fourth day, after Corvin had sent him, from his bed, a single slate with the adjuster's certification on it — _Discharge of the Sabra Concern's receivables under the instrument's own terms. Debt extinguished. Instrument void. C. Saal, adjuster_ — and the slate had come back with a partner's mark under his, in a hand that was, for the first time, not quite steady. Three thousand and eleven contracts assigned to a void instrument had nothing to attach to. Lantern was waking the sleepers fifty at a time, as Mabry Ode had said a decent man would, and finding them berths. But there was no discharge in Haste's drawer for Corvin Saal. There never would be now. His father's water debt on Kessane was owed to Brightwater still, the way it had always been, and it would be owed when the _Good Title_ went back through the Throat, and he supposed he would pay it. He found he did not mind as much as he had expected. It was his. He knew whose it was. That was something.

When the bell was hung, the square went quiet, the way it had gone quiet for the Hook every day for seven hundred years.

Senna Hask came and stood under it. She had the bell-rope in her hands — a new one, braided from hauler cable — and she looked up at the bronze for a long time, at the furred brown surface of it, every inch of it letters.

Then she turned and held the rope out to Fen.

Fen did not take it at once. She stood in front of the old woman with her hands at her sides.

"There's something I never told anyone," she said. "My family never told anyone either. It's inside. In the mouth. Scratched, not cut — with a knife, I think, or a buckle. We always thought it was the salvager's mark. Two words and a name." She looked up into the bell's mouth, where the light of the Wick did not reach. "It says RING IT. And then a name. Most of it's gone. K, and an S at the end."

Senna Hask did not say anything for a moment.

"Kasimir Ostrova," she said. "Dagny Ostrova's brother. He sold her, at the Closing, for two seats on the _Covenant_. And then he took the bell through the Throat as it shut, on his own, in a tug with no business going through a throat at all. The Founders' log says so." She looked up into the dark of the bell's mouth. "He was trying to get the names out. So somebody would listen."

"Somebody did," said Fen. Her voice cracked. "It took a while."

She took the rope.

"For Kasimir Ostrova," she said, and rang the bell.

The note went out across the Dock Ring, low and a little sour, with a waver in it, and round the curve of the world under the blazing Wick, and on every terrace and road and roof that Corvin could see, people stopped what they were doing, and stood still for it, the breath of a bell, and went on.

"For Mabry Ode," said Fen, and rang it again.

"For Marisol Tey."

"For a man called Roach, at the throat-watch, who waited."

She went on. Senna Hask stood beside her and said, after each name, very quietly, "Owing and owed"; and after a while the square began to say it with her, the way it always had — and Corvin, on the bench, understood that it did not mean what it had meant. It had meant a ledger. It meant, now, only that somebody had been here, and was remembered, and that the people who remembered them had not finished being grateful.

When she was done, Fen came and sat down beside him on the bench, and leaned against his good side, very carefully, and put her head on his shoulder. Above them the bell hummed on its hook, slowly, slowly fading. Down the middle of the sky the Wick burned from end to end of the world.

"They'll need somebody to cut the new names," she said after a while. "Senna says. There's no room left on her anywhere. They're going to have to cast another bell."

"Can you cut bronze?"

"No." She did not lift her head. "I can learn. I read old hands for a living. It can't be that different from writing one."

He sat with her under the bell in the light. Somewhere above them, in the Counting House, in a round room at the bottom of a buried ship, a desk of dark glass and brass sat silent at the root of the world, with nothing left to count; and all through Lantern, in the Pumphouse and the Galleries and the farms, every valve on the rock opened when it was asked, without asking whose air it was.

Nobody owed anybody for it. For the first time in eight hundred years, nobody owed anything at all.
